# Where Oryol Ampilogovs Came From

## The First Candidate with an Address on the Father Pakhom Line: Nason Traced in Borshchovka from 1584/85

### A New Lower Limit: «Насонка Пафонов» and His Neighbour Levka Pafomov

A search for early Aleksin extracts (vypisi) produced a stronger result than the
seventeenth-century inspection books (dozory). **«Акты служилых землевладельцев» (Acts of
Service Landholders), vol. II, No. 142** prints in full an extract from the cadastral survey
books (pistsovye knigi) of Elizary Rzhevsky of 1584/85. In Borshchovka, under Fendrik and
Fyodor, the sons of Fyodor Dyakov, six peasant households are listed, among them, one after
the other:

> «(в) Левка Пафомов, (в) Насонка Пафонов».
>
> *Translation:* "(in [the household]) Levka Pafomov, (in [the household]) Nasonka Pafonov."

This is the same Borshchovka and the same rare name Nason as in the petition of 1593, where
he is called **«Насон Пахомав» (Nason Pakhomav)**. Now the man can be traced in one village
across nine years, and the pre-Oryol address moves down from 1593 to 1584/85 — roughly ten
years before the entry for **Ermak Pakhomov syn Anfilogov** (Ermak, son of Pakhom, Anfilogov)
in Oryol.

The letters must not be smoothed over after the fact. The page of the scholarly edition
literally gives three forms of one closely related stem: **Пафомов** (Pafomov) for Levka,
**Пафонов** (Pafonov) for Nason and later **Пахомав** (Pakhomav) for the same probable Nason.
The extract has not survived in the original but in two copies of 1679 and 1682. So the most
likely explanation is a wavering or a copying error in the patronymic Pakhomov, but the Record
keeps the literal reading and the normalisation separately.

Levka Pafomov is a new person in the hypothesis. He may turn out to be Nason's brother, that
is, a second son of Pafom/Pakhom. In that case the comparison with Ermak of Oryol no longer
rests on a single coincidence but on a testable group of three men: **Lev — Nason — Ermak**,
each on the step "son of Pakhom". But the early extract merely places their households side
by side and does not call them kin; brotherhood is not yet asserted.

At the same time the early extract almost resolves the puzzle of the owners. In 1584/85
Borshchovka was demonstrably held by **Fendrik and Fyodor, the sons of Fyodor Dyakov**.
Therefore «Фенорик и Фёдор Денисовы» ("Fenorik and Fyodor Denisov") in the publication of the
1593 petition most likely contains a misreading or a copying error: the rare name, the paired
owner and the village match too precisely. In 1628 Borshchovka is held by **Vasily Fendrikov
syn Dyakov** (Vasily, son of Fendrik, Dyakov), probably a son of the same Fendrik. The 1593
text can be corrected definitively only from a photocopy of archival fol. 46.

Pages 139–140 of the open-access edition are kept in the card as photocopies. Page 140 prints
the exact archival references of both late copies of the extract: **РГАДА, f. 1209, scrolls
(stolbtsy) for Aleksin, No. 493/31109, fols. 11–15** and **No. 882/31459, fols. 146–147**.

### A New Corridor through the Masters of the Household: Aleksin — Tula — Lebedyan

A search by owners proved more productive than yet another sweep through the Pakhomovs.
Fendrik Fyodorov syn Dyakov does not disappear after the Aleksin entry. In 1613 he signed the
Charter of Confirmation (Utverzhdyonnaya gramota) as a **man of Tula** (tulyanin), a
representative of the Tula dvoryane, deti boyarskie, townspeople and district people. The
palace service register (Dvortsovy razryad) of 1618/19 then explicitly names **Vasily
Fendrikov syn Dyakov** as voivode at Lebedyan. In the cadastral survey book of
1627/28–1628/29 this same Vasily holds Borshchovka.

Thus the owning family links three points by documents: **a pomestye in Aleksin district →
the Tula service corporation → service as voivode in the new southern town of Lebedyan**. This
does not prove that Nason, Levka or their children left together with the Dyakovs, but it
opens a new archival corridor: now the Tula and Lebedyan files of Fendrik and Vasily, their
people, their guarantors and their old land grants (dachi) need to be checked, not only the
Oryol lists.

[Palace service register of 1618/19: «На Лебедяни Василей Фендриков сын Дьяков» ("At Lebedyan, Vasiley Fendrikov syn Dyakov")](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVII/1600-1620/Dvorc_razryady_1/9.htm),
[Fendrik Dyakov's signature as a man of Tula in 1613](https://ru.wikisource.org/wiki/Утвержденная_грамота_%28Белокуров_1906%29/Подписи).

### Why the General Search for Pafomovs Is Now Used as a Control, Not as Proof

Six Pafomovs have been checked on the original frames of the Oryol review list (razborny
spisok) of 1620/21: Ivan Dmitriev (fol. 17), Mikhail Mikhaylov (fol. 35), Fedka Mikhaylov
(fol. 113), a man whose name is lost, Ivanov syn (son of Ivan) (fol. 117), Danila Stepanov
(fol. 120) and Gavrila Filippov (fol. 138). The index renders five more men as Pokhomovs — on
fols. 99, 102, 103, 105 and 133.

This is an important negative result. By 1620/21 Pafomov/Pokhomov in Oryol is already a
widespread family designation with different patronymics, not one obvious household of Nason
Pakhomov. So a match of the stem **Pakhom** must not be turned into kinship with Ermak
Anfilogov. The individual frames are kept in the research section of the card; they serve as
a filter against false certainty.

[Name index to РГАДА, f. 210, op. 14, d. 502](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/5703/135781/p5737379.htm).

### The Exact Address of the Pakhom Line and a Check of the False "Second Signal"

The complete **cadastral survey book of Aleksin district for 1627/28–1628/29** made it possible
to correct and localise the address in the petition. The «Обошинский стан» (Oboshinsky stan)
printed in the 1593 act is almost certainly **Boboshinsky stan**: the book explicitly names in
it **«село Покровское, а Борщовка тож»** ("the village of Pokrovskoye, also called Borshchovka")
(РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, bk. 3, fols. 842v–846v; 2024 edition, pp. 312–314). This is the
present-day village of Borshchevka west of Tula, in the former Alyoshinskaya volost.

Thirty-five years later Nason, the Mesishchevs, Polunya and Kirilla are no longer in the full
text of the book. The village is held by **Vasily Fendrikov syn Dyakov**, and his title goes
back to a charter of Tsar Vasily Shuisky of the year 7116, that is, 1607/08. Together with the
extract of 1584/85, this makes the reading **«Фенорик и Фёдор Денисовы»** ("Fenorik and Fyodor
Denisov") in the 1593 publication almost certainly a corrupted rendering of the Dyakovs. A
literal correction still requires the original of fol. 46, but the two pairs of owners can no
longer be treated as equally weighted candidates.

A check of the surrounding lands gave an important negative correction. On the following folios
of Boboshinsky stan the **waste (pustosh) of Krivolapovskaya on the little river Vosma** is
indeed described, but it is shared land: its shares (zherebya) belong to the pomestya of Ivan
Semyonovich Arsenyev, of Anna and Avdotya, the daughters of Alimpy Arsenyev, and of Vasily
Konstantinovich Nesterov (fols. 848v–863; 2024 edition, pp. 314–319). The book does not call it
part of Borshchovka or Nason's holding. Moreover, a separate Krivolapovskaya existed in the
Zasupsky stan of Tula district.

So the earlier conclusion about "two signals in one micro-area" is withdrawn. The Krivolapovo
place names support only the broad Aleksin–Tula direction of the search and cannot link Nason
with the Oryol Krivolapovs. The only strong lead remaining is the personal step **Pakhom →
Nason**; proof would be a document that explicitly names a second son of Pakhom, kinship with
Ermak, or the move of a man from this household to Oryol.

On the other hand, a continuous name search through all 608 pages of the book produced a new,
unexpected pair for checking. In another stan, **Koninsky stan**, in the small village
(seltso) of Matyukovskoye, also called Tovzakovo, on the little river Zhegalovka, a peasant
**Pakhomko Nasonov** is recorded, explicitly called the nephew of Mishka Larionov (fol. 260;
2024 edition, p. 116). The formula means Pakhom, son of Nason. If this turned out to be one
family with the man of 1593, it would give a natural naming chain **Pakhom → Nason → Pakhom**,
that is, a grandson named after his grandfather.

But for now this is only a candidate with an address: a different stan, another household, no
age given, no move from Borshchovka recorded. In the same Matyukovskoye a share was held by
**Artemy Tovzakov syn Nechaev** (Artemy, son of Tovzak, Nechaev). The combination in one late
small village of the two stems Pakhom/Nason and Nechay recalls the two independent paternal
steps of Oryol Anfilogovs — Ermak Pakhomovich and Ivan Nechaevich. It changes the next
search but not the strength of the evidence: what is needed now are earlier reference books
(pripravochnye knigi) for Matyukovskoye/Tovzakovo, where Pakhomko's father, age or old land
grant and the origin of his uncle Mishka Larionov may be named.

A separate card with the address has been created:
`PUB-RGADA-141-1593-ALEKSIN-BORSHCHOVKA-NASON-PAKHOMOV-CANDIDATE`. No profile for Nason and no
route on the map have been added: the source names neither the surname Anfilogov nor kinship
with Ermak Pakhomovich.

Reloading the missed pages of the Yelets file produced a new lead, this time not from general
surname statistics but for one of the seven fathers of the Oryol core. In a collective petition
of 1593 five streltsy (musketeers) of Yelets literally explained where they had come from and
who had stayed behind on the old ploughland.

The brothers **Bogdashka and Vaskya, sons of Semyon Mesishchev**, had previously lived in
**Aleksin district, Oboshinsky stan, the village of Borshchevka**. When they enrolled at Yelets,
there remained behind **«вотчим наш Насон Пахомав, да братья наши Полуня да Кирила»** ("our
stepfather Nason Pakhomav, and our brothers Polunya and Kirila"). The form *Пахомав* (Pakhomav)
in this entry means a dependent naming of Nason after Pakhom, most likely "Pakhomov [son]", and
not necessarily a hereditary surname of the modern type.

This is the first specific pre-Oryol man of the right generation found through the step
**Pakhom → son**. The Oryol book of 1594/95 records **Ermak Pakhomov syn Anfilogov**. In theory
Nason and Ermak could have been two sons of one Pakhom, one of whom stayed in Aleksin district
while the other ended up in Oryol. Such a scheme corresponds exactly to the already proven rule
of family recruitment.

But kinship is not accepted for now. Pakhom is not a unique name; the document does not call
Nason an Anfilogov and does not call him Ermak's brother. Moreover, Nason is the stepfather of
the Mesishchevs, so the surname of his stepsons says nothing about his own origin.
**Aleksin/Borshchevka becomes a personal address for checking, but not a route on the map.**

What is needed now is not a general search for Pakhomovs but the Aleksin cadastral, tax and
transfer (otkaznye) materials for the village of Borshchevka and Oboshinsky stan: Nason's own
children, his father Pakhom, a possible brother Ermak, and the fate of the household after the
stepsons left. Nason has been added inside the Record without a separate profile: he is not a
bearer of the surname under study but a control candidate.

[Petition of the Yelets streltsy, РГАДА, f. 141, op. 1, 1592, d. 1, fol. 46](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/1-20/19.htm).

## Not One Route but Family Selection: Three Direct Moves from Solova to the New Town of Yelets

A full-text check of the published Yelets file of 1592–1593 gave a stronger control than yet
another surname match. At least three different men are documented as having left Solova
district for the new town of Yelets:

- **Pervushka Dementyev syn Penkov** enrolled as a Cossack; three brothers stayed behind in
  Solova, while his wife and his brother Isay were to join him;
- **Afonka Stepanov syn Shorstov** lived with his father-in-law in Solova district under a
  three-year agreement, then enrolled as a strelets «на новой город на Елец» ("for the new town,
  for Yelets");
- **Naydenko Basov** received the Yelets pay but fled back and again lived in Solova district
  under Fyodor Kostomarov.

This turns Solova from a statistical candidate into a proven **manpower reservoir for the new
southern garrisons**. So far the direction Solova → Yelets is proven, not Solova → Oryol, but the
result is independent of the 1596 surname comparison and therefore strengthens it considerably.

The most important find is the literal rule of recruitment. On 6 November 1592 the government
ordered that not ploughing peasants but zakhrebetniki (dependent lodgers living in another man's
household) be accepted as Cossacks and streltsy: **«от отцов — детей, и дядь — племянников»**
("from fathers — children, and from uncles — nephews"), so that people would remain on the old
households and ploughland. Other files show the same procedure in real families. Yemelyan
Yakovlevich Mishin left a Tula household for Yelets together with his father, while his brother
Agap stayed on the former quitrent (obrok). Consequently, the new town did not necessarily
receive a whole family, but a single younger or collateral member of an old household.

It is exactly this mechanism that can explain our "jump". A man could have received an
independent oklad and a stable designation for the first time only in Oryol, while his father,
uncle or brother remained in Solova, Krapivna, Tula, Novosil or
another town and was enrolled there only by his first name and patronymic. So the absence of a
ready-made surname Anfilogov in the early desyatnya (service register) of the town of origin does
not yet rule that town out.

The documents also reject the expectation of a single common point of departure. The biography of
**Yushka Danilov syn Malyavin** has three stages: his father served from Mtsensk and was killed;
the mother and son left for Novosil district; Yushko himself then enrolled as a syn boyarsky of the
new town of Yelets. The last town assignment could already have been the third geographical point
of one family.

In December 1592 the pay at Yelets was intended for 1,017 men, including 150 deti boyarskie, 200
streltsy and 600 Cossacks; 270 Cossacks then fled from the new town. So the core of the new garrison
was formed in waves, broke apart and was replenished again. The model of a single organised
resettlement for Oryol becomes even less likely.

A Record has been created:
`PUB-ANPILOGOV-1967-SOLOVA-YELETS-RECRUITMENT-NETWORK`. It keeps the full names, archival folios,
the different social trajectories and a negative control: there is no bearer of the series under
study in the Yelets roster of deti boyarskie of 1593, and no mass transfer of this roster to Oryol
is visible. The new narrow search looks not only for surnames but for the formulas **«сын при
отце»** ("a son with his father"), **«брат при брате»** ("a brother with his brother"),
**«племянник при дяде»** ("a nephew with his uncle"), and the handing over of a wife and property —
together with the names Mikula, the two Vasilys, Persha, Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty.

The map does not change for now: what is documented is the route of control persons of another
surname, not of an Anfilogov.

[Introduction to the publication of the Yelets complex](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/1-20/pred3.htm),
[list of runaway Yelets Cossacks in Tula and Solova](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/1-20/20.htm),
[petition of Afanasy Shorstov](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/21-40/31.htm),
[family biography of Yushka Malyavin](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/41-60/42.htm),
[rule for recruiting kinsmen who were zakhrebetniki](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/41-60/49.htm).

## A Personal Breakthrough: Two Solova Noviki Found in Krapivna 21 Years Later

After the statistical comparison the tactic was changed again: what was checked was not new surname
matches but the fates of the Solova men already found. In the tax book (platezhnaya kniga) of
Krapivna for 1616/17 (РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 488) two complete formulas from the Solova section of
the 1596 desyatnya were found:

- **Smirnoy Prokofyev syn Trunov** — Solova, 1596; Krapivna, fol. 100,
  1616/17;
- **Kostyantin Ermolaev syn Bologov/Bolotov** — Solova, 1596; Krapivna,
  fol. 99v, 1616/17.

The first name, patronymic, family designation and district all match. Two independent cases make
chance unlikely: the Solova heading of 1596 really does describe a stable Krapivna–Solova service
corporation that survived the Time of Troubles.

The Trunovs give a probable pedigree two generations deep. In 1596 Grigory and Smirnoy Prokofyevich
are recorded. In 1616/17 Smirnoy is still in the book, while another entry names the widow Matryona,
left after Grigory Trunov, and the sons Filipp, Ivan and Trofim; Ivan Grigoryevich Trunov is recorded
separately. The most natural scheme: **Prokofy → Grigory and Smirnoy; Grigory → Filipp, Ivan and
Trofim**. But the patronymic of the deceased Grigory is not repeated in the later entry, so this is a
strong reconstruction, not a finished pedigree.

The Penkovs are even more important. To the known pair **Afonasey Vasilyev syn Penkov** (Solova, 1596)
and **Prokuda Vasilyev syn Penkov** (Oryol, 1596) has been added **Sergey Vasilyev syn Penkov** on
Krapivna fol. 63. This is the first candidate for one specific family divided between the old district
and the new town of Oryol. Sergey is recorded twenty-one years later and may be a younger brother or a
son of another Vasily, so the decisive document will be an old land grant in which the father and sons
are named together.

This result does not yet carry over to Pavel Terentyevich Anfilogov. But Pavel and Prokuda Penkov stand
in the same Oryol section of 1596: confirming the Penkov family will show the real recruitment mechanism
of precisely his closest service group. A card has been created,
`PUB-RGADA-1596-1617-SOLOVA-KRAPIVNA-PERSON-CONTINUITY`, with three open images of the folios. No profiles
for other surnames and no route on the family map are created.

After that the next address was also found, which makes it unnecessary to return to a general web
search. **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 10, d. 86, fol. 63** has published a Solova novik of 1630/31, **Kostka Akishin
syn Penkov**, with an oklad of 200 chetverti. His line Akinfey → Kostka does not match Vasily and the
three possible Vasilyeviches, so kinship is not assumed. The value of the file lies elsewhere: one
"original" (podlinny) list covers Solova and Oryol at the same time. It must be checked in full for old
marginal notes, relatives and changes of town affiliation. Neighbouring references in the same series
are f. 210, op. 14, dd. 97 and 504. This is a new second path in case the Penkovs' land grants are not
found.

## A New First Candidate: Solova/Krapivna by the "Surname Fingerprint" of 1596

The tactic was changed: instead of yet another isolated search for an Anfilogov, the entire surname
composition of the Oryol noviki of 1596 was compared with all the towns of the same nationwide
desyatnya. Witnesses were excluded; the exact surname forms after the words «сын» ("son") and «дети»
("children") were taken from the lines of the noviki. For each town the number of matches with Oryol,
the share of matches, the Jaccard coefficient and the weight of rare surnames were calculated. The
calculation is reproduced by the script
`scripts/research/analyze-1596-city-surname-fingerprint.mjs`.

First place unexpectedly went to **Solova**. Of its 46 surname forms, eight recur in Oryol: **Rtishchev,
Bushmin, Yesipov, Bukalov, Bulgakov, Penkov, Isakov and Pakhomov**. That is 17.39% of the Solova list;
Jaccard — 0.0541, IDF weight — 30.65. Five of the matching forms — Rtishchev, Bushmin, Bukalov, Penkov
and Pakhomov — occur in no more than three town headings of the whole desyatnya. The next candidates
are weaker: Novosil gives seven matches, Tula six, Odoyev four, and Mtsensk, which arose from the
career route of Mikhail Kulnev, only three.

An independent **test of the fathers' generation** has now been added to the surname test. A
ready-made surname might not yet have become fixed, so the seven names of the fathers of the early
Oryol branches were checked across all town headings of the same desyatnya: Mikula, Vasily, Persha,
Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty. In this cross-section first place went to **Tula**: the forms
Vasilyev, Timofeyev, Terentyev and Pershin occur there together. Especially important is **Bogdan
Pershin syn Solomykov** — outside Oryol he is the only Persha in the whole desyatnya reconstructed
through a son's patronymic. Solova gave three forms — Mikulin, Vasilyev and Nechaev — and thereby
independently supported the surname signal already found; Mtsensk and Ryazan also gave three each.

This result does not turn Bogdan Solomykov into a relative of Ivan Pershin Anfilogov: the third
designations differ, and a father's name does not by itself prove kinship. Its significance is
practical. Instead of one blurred Upper Oka direction there are now two different priorities:
**Solova/Krapivna — by family surroundings, Tula — by the names of the fathers' generation of the
1550s–1570s**. Such a divergence fits well with the mixed recruitment of Oryol and with the fact that
the early entries show at least seven paternal lines. In the Tula materials one should look for Persha
and Terenty even without a ready-made surname Anfilogov. No profiles of other people and no route on the
map are created.

But an address search for Bogdan himself gave a stronger result and changed the interpretation. In the
Tula heading Bogdan Pershin syn Solomykov received 100 chetverti, and in the next, Solova heading, with
the same oklad, stood **Druzhina Osipov syn Solomykov**. This does not yet prove their kinship, but
Druzhina can be traced further without a break by his full name. On **19 March 1613** the Pechatny Prikaz
(Seal Chancellery), on his petition, sealed a charter for 10 chetverti in Krapivna; on **10 July 1613** —
a votchina charter of Druzhina Osipov syn Solomykov for a votchina (hereditary estate) in Solova. Both
pages of the full photocopy of S. B. Veselovsky's edition are kept in the project archive: pp. 44 and
232.

The tax book **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, bk. 488** continues this line: a copy of the Krapivna inspection
books (dozornye knigi) of 1616/17 again names Druzhina Osipov Salamykov as a landholder of Zasolovsky stan
on fol. 89v. The compilation by Yu. V. Ankhimyuk and A. P. Pavlov reports, citing **РНБ, ОР, F. IV, No.
465, fol. 76v**, that his votchina then passed to his sons, the Solova men Sevastyan and Nikita Druzhinin
Salamykov.

So the earlier fork "Tula or Solova" turned out to be too crude. The rare Tula patronymic Pershin was met
next to a family designation that was present in both Tula and Solova at once, and a specific Druzhina
Osipovich documentably linked Solova with Krapivna. The working object of the search is now **a single
Tula–Solova–Krapivna basin of service and land**, where even close relatives could be listed under
different town headings.

But the control by S. B. Veselovsky's «Ономастикон» (Onomasticon) does not allow the Solomykovs to be
declared one proven family. Page 277 names Salamykovs in Novgorod
as early as 1550, in Kazan in 1565 and again in Novgorod in 1603. The surname was wider than a single
Upper Oka cluster. So the strong part of the find is the exact biography of **Druzhina Osipovich** in
1596, 1613 and 1616/17; the weak part is the supposed kinship with Bogdan Pershinich. It is exactly this
kind of check that must be repeated for Anfilogovs through shared land grants, divisions of land and
direct kinship formulas, rather than demanding one "town of origin" for all seven paternal lines. The
Solomykovs remain a control group: no profiles and no map route are created for them.

The nominal probability of getting at least eight matches in a simple hypergeometric model is 0.01293,
against an expected 3.25. This is not a "mathematical proof": many towns were tested, the surnames are not
independent, the manuscript is a late copy, and spelling variants were not merged. Here statistics is only a
filter that brought the town forward for manual checking.

The manual check gave two more substantive levels.

1. In Solova **Afonasey Vasilyev syn Penkov** is recorded, and in Oryol **Prokuda
   Vasilyev syn Penkov**. The father Vasily and the rare family designation both
   match. These are possible brothers, distributed between the old service district
   and the new town; there is no direct word "brothers".
2. The Solova heading contains at the same time **Grigorey and Smirnoy Prokofyev
   Trunov** and the difficult reading **Kirilo Okulov syn Bazdurov (or perhaps
   Baydurov?)**. The Trunovs and the Bazdyrevs were the nearest neighbours of the five
   Anfilogovs in the village of Bazdyrevo in 1594/95. Bazdurov is not yet corrected to
   Bazdyrev: it is a rare beacon to be checked, not established kinship.

The combination of three independent signals — the general surname structure, a possible pair of Penkov
brothers and the surnames of the Bazdyrevo micro-circle — makes Solova/Krapivna the first candidate
specifically for a **manpower reservoir**, and not necessarily for the sole homeland of all the Oryol
Anfilogovs.

A geographical check removed the risk of a false point. The passport of the historic settlement of
Krapivna (2008, pp. 39–46) states that until the second half of the seventeenth century the name Solova
was given to the territory whose military and administrative centre was Krapivna, and that the local
pomeshchiki (landholders) and deti boyarskie were called solovlyane ("Solova men"). The authors locate Old
Krapivna 15–18 km south-east of the present town and regard as best founded the version that the town was
moved after the Tatar devastation of 30 June 1587, although there is no direct chancellery record of the
move. The desyatnya of 1596 was compiled nine years later and may record the young generation of an
already reorganised corporation.

The new first address is the Solova/Krapivna pomestye and review extracts of the 1570s–1590s, documents on
the recovery after 1587, and later petitions of the Penkovs, Trunovs and Bazdyrevs containing extracts from
lost books. A detailed card has been created,
`PUB-LIKHACHEV-1909-1596-OREL-SOLOVA-FINGERPRINT`; the official РГАДА spreads have been saved, and a full copy
of the Krapivna passport has been placed in the closed research storage. The route Solova/Krapivna — Oryol is
not yet drawn on the map: neither Pavel Terentyevich nor any other bearer of the series under study is named
as having come from Solova.

## The Kulnev Corridor Has Narrowed to the Town Rosters

A check of the primary text forced us to abandon an overly attractive version. Mikhail Grigoryevich Kulnev
really did serve in Karachev two years running (the entries for 7092 and 7093), was appointed golova
(commander) at Mtsensk on 25 March 1587, and in the entry for 7096 is named golova at Livny. This gives a
real arc **Karachev — Mtsensk — Livny**, but it is the route of an appointed commander, not a proven path of
his Meshchovsk men.

The decisive phrase of the expanded Razryad Book (Razryadnaya kniga, the register of service appointments)
says that «дворяня, и дети боярские, и всякие люди» ("the dvoryane, and the deti boyarskie, and all manner
of people") marched with the voivodes **«по их списком»** ("according to their lists"). The roster itself
groups the commanders by towns: at Mtsensk these are Prince Ivan Yuryevich Tokmakov and Mikhail Kulnev; at
Livny, Ivan Mikhaylovich Buturlin, Ivan Grigoryevich Milyukov and Mikhail Kulnev. Consequently, the rank and
file should be sought not in Kulnev's biography and not in a supposed personal retinue, but in the separate
Mtsensk town roster of March 1587, its field counterpart copy (protiven) and the Livny roster of 1587/88.

One more false lead has been closed. The preface of the scholarly edition states explicitly that the
originals of the official Razryad Books have not survived: they perished in fires or when the documents on
precedence (mestnichestvo) were destroyed. What has come down to us are private copies and extracts of the
seventeenth century. So there is no longer any need to look for the "original" of the published roster. The
new address is the separate executive papers of **РГАДА, f. 210**: town rosters, field counterpart copies,
voivodes' reports (otpiski) and later petitions into which lines from the lost books might have been copied.
Queries must be built not only around Kulnev but around all four commanders and around the towns with dates.

We will check not one surname but the neighbourhood cluster of the Oryol book: **Anfilogovs/variants +
Bazdyrevs + Krivolapovs + Trunovs + Nemtsovs + Tokhtamyshevs**. A single line with the same surname is weak;
several of these families in one early list would already give a testable route. A check of the open
database has not yet revealed such a pre-Oryol cluster: the Tokhtamyshevs and Nemtsovs found belong to the
seventeenth century. This is not a new dead end but a filter that frees the search from late chance matches.

A separate card keeps the exact entries, images of the printed pages and the limit of the conclusion:
**PUB-RK-1584-1588-KULNEV-CITY-ROSTERS-BOUNDARY**. No route has been drawn on the map, because the source
shows Kulnev's movement but not that of a bearer of the surname series under study.

## Before the Fortress: the Cossack Golova Dmitry Turgenev «на Орле» ("on the Orel") in 1564/65

The search has been pushed one more step back — not from a ready-made Oryol to donor towns, but from a
**pre-frontier Cossack detachment** to the future town. In a published seventeenth-century reference book on
precedence, compiled from the files of the Razryad, the Posolsky (Ambassadorial) and other chancelleries, a
line was found: **«73 году на Орле был у казаков голова Дмитрей Тургенев»** ("in the year 73, on the Orel, the
golova over the Cossacks was Dmitrey Turgenev").

The year 7073 ran from 1 September 1564 to 31 August 1565. This is earlier than the chronicle entry for 7075 on
the founding of a town on the river Orel. The discrepancy does not necessarily shift the date of the fortress's
construction: the formula «на Орле» ("on the Orel") could have meant a field Cossack post or a place of service
by the river before permanent walls appeared. But it shows that a service detachment could have preceded the
town.

This substantially changes the question about Anfilogovs. Their eldest generation — Mikula, the two Vasilys,
Persha, Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty — need not have been a ready-made group of deti boyarskie transferred
at one time from one town. One or several of the fathers could first have joined an early Cossack contingent
(pribor) near Oryol, and their sons could later have received pomestya and become established as deti boyarskie.
For now this is a search model, not an established pedigree.

The source calls for a strict caveat. The manuscript БАН 32.15.16 was written in the second half of the
seventeenth century; its editor, Yu. V. Tatishchev, explicitly noted the errors and legends it contains. At the
same time he stressed that the reference book preserved extracts from lost or unlocated chancellery documents.
So the line for 7073 is accepted as a strong archival lead, but not as a definitive revision of the founding of
Oryol without an independent original from the Razryad.

### Who Exactly This Dmitry Was: the Strongest Candidate Found

Three original surety records (poruchnye zapisi) have closed the former gap in the biography. On 20 April 1563 and
12 April 1566 they literally name **«Дмитрей Федоров сын Тургенев»** (Dmitrey Fedorov syn Turgenev); around 28
March 1565 the same layer of time gives the short form **«Дмитрей Тургенев»** (Dmitrey Turgenev). The genealogical
list published by V. V. Rummel and V. V. Golubtsov independently describes Dmitry Fyodorovich as a guarantor of
1563–1566. It is he who is now the strongest candidate for the Cossack golova «на Орле» ("on the Orel") of
1564/65. But the late Oryol line contains no patronymic, so the identification remains probable rather than
final.

At the same time a former trap has been corrected. The title **«Род Тургеневых»** ("The Turgenev Family") brings
together cases of different members of the family, not one continuous biography. The genealogy explicitly knows
another man — Dmitry Mikhaylovich Turgenev, who served with ambassadors and as voivode of Kargopol in 1589–1590.
So the later ambassadorial and Pereyaslavl service can no longer be automatically ascribed to the Oryol Dmitry
and used as proof of a Ryazan origin of his Cossacks.

### 1563: the Name Onfal within the Same Body of Servicemen

The most important link turned out to lie not in Turgenev's later career but in his surroundings. In the surety
record of 20 April 1563, among 102 servicemen, there appear at the same time:

- **Dmitry Fyodorov syn Turgenev** — the probable early Cossack golova on the
  Orel;
- **Ivan Andreyev syn Fefilatyev** — the documented golova of Oryol in 1585;
- **Rychko Onfalov/Anfalov syn Volotskogo** — a bearer of a direct designation after
  his father Onfal/Anfal.

Dmitry and Rychko were each liable for a hundred roubles, but they stood in different parts of the big list.
This does not prove friendship, a common detachment or Rychko's move to Oryol. But for the first time the rare
name-patronymic has turned up in one original document together with the probable early and the undoubted later
Oryol commanders. Now Rychko's card is not just a general onomastic parallel but an addressed node of the early
Oryol personnel network.

The surety record of about 28 March 1565 shows the next layer. Together with Dmitry Turgenev it names Posol
Fyodorovich Ivashkin of Tula, Davyd Ivanovich and Ivan Timofeyevich Fustov of Kashira, and servicemen of Ryazan,
Kaluga, Toropets, Vyazma, Meshchovsk and the Moscow list. Ivan Timofeyevich Fustov later reliably held command in
Oryol. So the new working model is not a single donor town but **a mixed central network of suretyship and
appointments** from which the southern frontier was staffed.

New search addresses: the other eight lists of the precedence reference book, the original Razryad entry for 7073,
Cossack estimates (smety), provisioning and cash books, the papers of the contingents of Dmitry Fyodorovich
Turgenev, and later extracts on their enrolment (verstanie). The search must be made within the circles of
Vorotynsky, Fefilatyev, Fustov and Ivashkin by the fathers Mikula, Vasily, Persha, Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and
Terenty, not only by the already established surname.
A card has been created,
`PUB-MESTNICHESKY-7073-DMITRY-TURGENEV-OREL-COSSACK-HEAD`; no profile of
Turgenev and no point on the family map were created.

[Publication of the manuscript, fol. 11v](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVII/Mestn_sprav_17v/text1.htm).
[Surety records of 1563–1566](https://vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1520-1540/Porucn_zapisi_1527_1571/text.phtml).
[Genealogical list of the Turgenevs](https://turgenev.oreluniver.ru/file/BiografiyaTurgeneva/PaintingTurgenev.pdf).

## Meshchovsk Turned Out to Be Not a "Homeland" but the Base of a Command Corridor

The surety list of 1563 has now been checked as a snapshot of a whole body of servicemen, not only as the meeting
place of Turgenev, Fefilatyev and Rychko Onfalov/Anfalov Volotskogo. **Mikhail Grigoryevich Kulnev** was found in it.
This is not a simple surname match with the Oryol book: around 28 March 1565 the same man is explicitly named a
pomeshchik **in Meshchovsk**, and in the boyar lists of 1588–1591 he was still an elected man (vyborny) for
Meshchovsk with an oklad of 600 chetverti.

At the same time Mikhail's actual service forms an almost ready-made map of the southern frontier. According to
A. Yu. Savosichev's compilation of primary references, he was:

- in 1584/85, namestnik (governor) at Karachev;
- on 25 March 1587, siege golova at Mtsensk;
- in 1587/88, golova at Livny;
- in 1589/90–1590/91, siege golova at Chernigov;
- in 1591/92–1593/94, namestnik at Bolkhov;
- in 1594/95–1595/96, golova at Rylsk.

This is the first biography checked in detail that shows not a hypothetical resettlement but the very **mechanism
of the circulation of personnel**: a man remained a Meshchovsk service landholder and at the same time for decades
was appointed to new and frontier towns. Consequently, an ordinary serviceman could have come to Oryol through the
detachment or contingent of such a golova without receiving the note "transferred from Meshchovsk" in the general
Razryad book.

### Nikita Kulnev in Oryol: a Strong Match with a Scholarly Caveat

On printed p. 933 of the Oryol book a corresponding land trace has been found. By 1594/95 **Nikita Grigoryevich
Kulnev** no longer held the hamlet of Klokovo in Korchakov stan, but the scribes three times preserved him as the
former holder. One share passed to Danila Andreyevich Bolotov; another passed through Besson Bulgakov; yet another
ended up with Varnava Fedosovich Lobynsky. So Kulnev belongs to an earlier layer of the Oryol pomestye system that
preceded the census itself.

The rare full formula **«Григорьевич Кулнев»** ("Grigoryevich Kulnev") for Mikhail and Nikita looks fraternal, and
the old genealogy joined them in exactly that way. But Savosichev specifically questioned this: there is no direct
act of common paternity, and the service weight of the Meshchovsk elected man Mikhail fits poorly with the single
report of Nikita's small former pomestye. So the database keeps three separate levels:

1. fact — Mikhail Grigoryevich is documented as a Meshchovsk pomeshchik and a
   commander in a chain of Upper Oka towns;
2. fact — Nikita Grigoryevich is documented as a former Oryol landholder;
3. hypothesis — they may have been brothers, but this is not yet proven kinship.

Klokovo lies in the same Korchakov stan where the bulk of the early Anfilogovs is concentrated. However, one large
stan is not proof of neighbourhood, a common distribution of land or a joint move. Meshchovsk is now important as
the address of a **personnel channel**, not as a declared homeland of the surname.

The tactic on the next pass changes once more: what must be sought are the compact executive papers of Mikhail's
appointments — Karachev 1584/85, Mtsensk 1587 and Livny 1587/88 — and their entire nominal composition compared
with Korchakov stan in 1594/95. Siege rosters, lists of men attached to a golova, surety annexes and the original
pomestye distributions are especially valuable.

A card has been created:
PUB-SAVOSICHEV-2015-KULNEV-MESHCHOVSK-UPPER-OKA-CIRCUIT; control images of pp. 421–423 of Savosichev's
dissertation and p. 933 of the Oryol book have been saved. The modern dissertation is kept only in the internal
research archive and is opened publicly via its official link; the page of the 1877 edition is shown in the card
itself. No profiles of the Kulnevs and no route on the family map were created.

[A. Yu. Savosichev's dissertation, 2015](https://www.science.vsu.ru/dissertations/2129/%D0%94%D0%B8%D1%81%D1%81%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0%D1%86%D0%B8%D1%8F_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%B8%D1%87%D0%B5%D0%B2_%D0%90.%D0%AE..pdf).
[Surety records of 1527–1571](https://vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1520-1540/Porucn_zapisi_1527_1571/text.phtml).
[Scan of the Oryol cadastral survey book](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia).

## 1579: a Mechanism of Inter-Town Recruitment Found, but Not a Ready Route

The full photostat of the act has now been saved and checked against the official РГАДА images. Frame 0329 opens the
act on fol. 269; on frame 0340 it ends on fol. 280, after which the right-hand page is blank; frame 0341, fol. 281,
already begins another document. So the promised «роспись» ("roster") of the rank and file did not continue this act
and was not sewn to it as an annex. The new object of the search is a separate executive paper of the Razryad
Prikaz: a list by entries, a dispatch copy (otpusk) or a town's reply, not the neighbouring folio of the Storozhevaya
kniga (Book of the Watch Service).

In parallel, the internal search of the database «Служилые люди XVI–XVIII века» (Servicemen of the 16th–18th
Centuries) has been worked out: instead of manual entry it is now possible to query its public output of 421,095 rows
in batches. A check of broad historical and OCR variants of the name in the sixteenth century produced only Anfinogen
Zolotukhin in the Obonezhskaya pyatina (1583). The main result here is negative but methodologically important: the
database does not know even the Oryol lines of 1594/95 that we have already checked, and the filter for 1594 is
entirely empty; 1595 is filled almost exclusively by a single Novgorod book. So it remains an excellent index of
seventeenth-century shelfmarks, but is no longer used as a test of whether people existed in early Oryol.

The next change of tactic: to look not for a surname and not for a neighbouring folio, but for two kinds of
chancellery duplicates. During mobilisation the voivode was to assign the deti boyarskie to golovy, send one list to
the chief voivode, and **«у себя противень оставити»** ("keep a counterpart copy with himself"). In 1577 the Razryad
already required separate nominal lists of three shifts to be kept by entries. So the name we need could have
survived not in the Storozhevaya kniga but (1) in the central list of the Razryad or (2) in the town counterpart
copy/reply of Oryol, Tula, Ryazan, Putivl or a Seversk town. Priority shifts to the inventories of such dispatches,
replies and later extracts from lost desyatni.

A search through the papers of the Oryol commanders gave a more important result than yet another isolated surname
match. In April 1579 the boyar Nikita Romanovich Yuryev and the dyak (secretary) Vasily Shchelkalov questioned the
stanitsa golovy about the defence of the new Kalmius road. Among two Kashira specialists **Ivan Timofeyevich Fustov**
is named — the same man who held command in Oryol in the 1570s.

The verdict literally shows how recruitment was organised. To the Tula stanitsa golova and nine comrades on the Oskol
were added **40 deti boyarskie and Cossacks from the towns**, to the Ryazan group on the Don another 40, and 20–30 men
from the Seversk towns were ordered to be sent to the Seversky Donets. The report on its execution says that the men
were «из Украиных городов по росписи» ("from the Ukraine [frontier] towns according to the roster") and were relieved
every six weeks. The Oryol watch posts (storozhi) are included in this system separately: they covered the roads on
the Seym, and in case of the threat of a major campaign 10–15 men from Oryol were to be posted there.

This changes the model of the origin of the early garrison. A man could first have entered the Oryol milieu as a
participant in a temporary mixed shift while remaining a serviceman of another town; only later could he have
received an Oryol pomestye. So campaign lists and the cadastral survey book must not be read mechanically as one list
of settlers.

A career check strengthened the conclusion. Fustov can be traced in the Oryol records of 1573/74 and 1575/76, in 1579
he takes part in setting up the watch posts, and in
1581/82 he is again in the watch regiment. At the same time, the popular secondary report of his service at Dedilov
in 1582 was **not confirmed** by the primary Razryad record: the Dedilov complement there is different, and Fustov
serves in the north-western army. The false Dedilov thread has been ruled out.

A card has been created, `PUB-AMG-1579-FUSTOV-OREL-WATCH-RECRUITMENT`. It does not claim that Anfilogovs came from
Kashira, Tula, Ryazan or the Seversk towns: the published verdict contains no roster of the rank and file. The next
precise target is the annexes and separate six-week lists of 1579 with the names of men «из украинных городов» ("from
the Ukraine [frontier] towns"). No profiles of other surnames and no line on the family map were created.

## The Pre-Oryol Personnel Network: the Yakhontovs and Three Future Commanders of Oryol

The search for a rare surname was continued not by geography but by **formal surety links**. This turn produced the
first recurring pre-Oryol cluster. In three surety records of 1562–1571 representatives of two Torzhok lines of the
Yakhontovs appear in the same company as men who later held command in Oryol:

- on 20 March 1562, Ivan Ignatyevich Yakhontov and Ivan Timofeyevich Fustov;
- in 1564/65, Nikita Grigoryevich Yakhontov and Ivan Andreyevich Fefilatyev;
- in 1571, the brothers Nikita and Mikhail Grigoryevich Yakhontov, Foma
  Afanasyevich Buturlin and again Ivan Timofeyevich Fustov.

Fustov is later named voivode of Oryol in 1575/76, and Buturlin and Fefilatyev voivode and golova of Oryol in 1585. So
the coincidence can no longer be reduced to one similar surname: two Torzhok lines three times enter the documented
milieu of the future Oryol command.

The family layer could also be reconstructed. The Dvorovaya tetrad (Court Register) names the brothers Ivan and Andrey
Ignatyevich and the brothers Nikita, Mikhail and Vasily Grigoryevich Yakhontov. A donation charter (vkladnaya) of
1570/71 shows Ivan Ignatyevich as a votchina holder in Klin: he gave the Chudov Monastery the ancestral village of
Selyutino with its hamlets and the mortgaged half of Butovo. The act names his father Ignaty, his mother Mariya, his
brothers Andrey and Pyotr, his wife Yevdokiya and his nephew Mikhail Andreyevich. The witness is Mikhail Grigoryevich
Yakhontov, so the document links the two Torzhok lines at least at the level of a common land transaction.

In the surety record of 1571 two more rare beacons turned up nearby — Timofey Grigoryevich Mitusov and Sharap Fomich
Lyubuchaninov. Similar surname forms occur later in Oryol, but the patronymics do not match, and the reading of Vaska
Mitusov/Titulov remains disputed. These are auxiliary threads, not finished pedigrees.

A card has been created,
`PUB-YAKHONTOV-1562-1571-PRE-OREL-SURETY-NETWORK`. The limit of the conclusion is strict: not a single Oryol Yakhontov of
1594/95 has yet matched the people found by patronymic, Anfilogovs are not named in the surety lists, and a list of
hundreds of guarantors does not mean a joint move. But a new addressed tactic has appeared: to look for the small
executive papers of Fustov, Buturlin and Fefilatyev — reception lists, reports (otpiski), enrolment desyatni and land
grants — and to check them for the target surname together with the whole recurring cluster. No profiles of other
surnames and no route on the map were created.

## A New Group Search: Two Surname Beacons Instead of One Donor Town

The tactic was changed: the Oryol cadastral survey book of 1594/95 was compared with the Dvorovaya tetrad of the 1550s
not only for Anfilogovs but for the whole layer of rare surname forms. Frequent forms such as Ivanov, Vasilyev and
the like were excluded from the conclusion; strong candidates were checked by hand in their town headings.

The first beacon is the **Yakhontovs**. In Oryol, Roman Sofonovich, Timofey and Lukyan Ozarovich, the families of Larion
and Yermola, and also Stepan Okulovich Yakhontov are named. In the Dvorovaya tetrad the Yakhontovs form a dense series for
**Torzhok** — four entries of several paternal lines after fol. 148v — and a separate branch for **Tver** after fol. 146.
Not a single patronymic has yet matched the Oryol ones: this is an address for searching surety and pomestye acts, not a
ready route.

The second beacon is the **Ofremovs**. In Oryol, the Ignatovichi Konon, Mitka and Filipp, the Grigoryevichi Naum and
Semyon and the family of the late Grigory are visible. In the Dvorovaya tetrad the surname form is concentrated in the
**Kaluga** heading on fol. 127: four Garyainovichi Ofremov and Ondrey Mishukov Ofremov. There is no matching patronymic
either, so Kaluga remains a direction for checking.

The full text of the Dvorovaya tetrad yielded none of the forms Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Onfilogov, Onpilogov, Ampilogov or
Amfilogov. The Zakharya Panfilov who does occur belongs to a different name, Panfil. The main result of this stage is not
one supposed town of departure but several independent channels by which early Oryol was staffed.
A card has been created,
`PUB-DVOROVAYA-1550S-OREL-1594-RARE-SURNAME-FINGERPRINT`; no profiles of other
surnames and no lines on the map were added.

## 1596: Antip Anfilov syn Vakina Found in the Vladimir Heading

A full re-examination of the 1596 desyatnya produced not a second Anfilogov of the family but a subtler and more useful
find. On **fol. 2v**, in the group of Vladimir noviki with an oklad of 100 chetverti, one reads directly:

> «Антип Анфилов сын Вакина».
>
> *Translation:* "Antip, son of Anfil, Vakin."

This is a three-part formula. **Antip** is the novik's personal name; **Анфилов сын** (Anfilov syn) is a direct
patronymic, that is, "son of Anfil"; **Vakin** is a separate family designation. A modern conventional rendering is
**Antip Anfilovich Vakin**. Consequently, the line reliably reconstructs one more person of the previous generation —
**Anfil Vakin**, although the father himself is not named in a separate line.

The find is especially telling next to the Oryol line of the same desyatnya:

- «Антип **Анфилов сын** Вакина» — Anfilov is the patronymic, and Vakin the
  third designation;
- «Павел **Терентьев сын Анфилогов**» — Terentyev syn is the patronymic,
  and Anfilogov already stands in the place of the family designation.

Thus a single chancellery source shows two different stages in the formation of a designation in -ov. This confirms that
forms derived from the personal name Anfil / Anfilog could arise independently in different service milieus. But the line
does not name Antip's former town, does not link the Vakins with Oryol and gives no grounds for drawing a route Vladimir →
Oryol.

A Record `RGADA-181-2-120-L2OB-ANTIP-ANFILOV-VAKIN` and separate profiles of Antip and of the reconstructed father Anfil
have been created. The full spread and a fragment of the line have been saved. Both personal entries have been added to the
summary register of the pre-surname name; the map has deliberately not been changed.

### Two Later Independent Continuations of the Name: 1663 and 1677

The full official РГАДА catalogue added two personal entries not previously included in the project. The file **РГАДА, f.
281, op. 7, d. 2053, fols. 1–2** summarises a decree charter of Patriarch Joachim of 18 January 1677 on the transfer
(otkaz) of a pomestye to the patriarchal deti boyarskie **Ivan Grigoryev syn and Nikita Anpilov syn — Vsevolodtsky**. The
grammar preserves three parts: Nikita is the personal name; Anpilov syn is a direct patronymic; Vsevolodtsky is the family
designation. The father is thereby reconstructed as well — **Anpil Vsevolodtsky**.
[File card](https://online.archives.ru/guide/10000000001014/10000000228014/10000000642014/10000030128014/).

A separate memorandum (pamyat) **РГАДА, f. 1195, op. 9, d. 17** of 1663 names **Kuzma Anfilov**: the document concerned
the taking of a sum of money from him and his beating by the elder (starets) Malofey. Here the word «сын» ("son") is absent
from the heading, so until the original is examined the form Anfilov is kept as a second designation, without a final choice
between a patronymic and a stable surname form.
[File card](https://online.archives.ru/guide/10000000001014/10000001137014/10000001832014/10000366966014/).

For the question of Oryol both finds are a control of the name, not a route. They show an independent continuation of the
forms Anpil and Anfil in the seventeenth century, but contain no kinship, no common pomestye and no transfer into the Oryol
service corporation. Records and three profiles have been created; the map does not change.

## 7 October 1640: a Separate Three-Folio File on Amfilogovs' Pomestye Found

A full-text search through the new official inventory of the N. P. Likhachev collection produced not yet another line of a
general list but an independent primary document:

> «Грамота царя Михаила Федоровича в Орел воеводе И. С. Исленеву об обыске
> в поместьи Амфилоговых в Корчаковском стану».
>
> *Translation:* "Charter of Tsar Mikhail Fyodorovich to Oryol, to the voivode I. S. Islenev, on an inquiry
> into the pomestye of Amfilogovs in Korchakov stan."

The exact shelfmark: **Archive of SPbII РАН, coll. 238, op. 2, d. 281, No. 16**. The charter is dated **7 October 1640**,
occupies **three folios** and retains a black wax seal. The addressee's initials resolve to Ivan Stepanovich Islenyev: soon
afterwards the Oryol estimate book of 7150 was compiled under him, which already names specific Anfilogovs.

The word **«обыск»** (obysk) here means a chancellery investigation with the questioning of people and the verification of
circumstances, not a modern search of premises. That is exactly why the three folios are especially promising: they may
contain the names of the owners, the exact settlement, neighbours and witnesses, the subject of a land dispute, former
holders and references to old cadastral or separate books. For now this is a list of expected fields, not content that has
been read.
The storage context adds another key. The charter lies in the file «Документы о земельных владениях Протасовых,
кабальные записи, грамоты 1593–1642 гг.» ("Documents on the land holdings of the Protasovs, bondage records,
charters, 1593–1642"). The whole block Nos. 1–19 has been checked. Items Nos. 1–15 really are papers of the
Protasovs — a dowry, Mtsensk and Oryol pomestya, divisions and dependent people; No. 16 is a separate tsar's charter
about Amfilogovs; Nos. 17–19 again concern the people and the Mtsensk land of the Protasovs. So the charter's
belonging to the Protasov family archive is established, but they cannot yet be regarded as a party to the dispute:
the inventory does not say so.

Digital availability has also been checked. The exact query «Амфилоговых» ("of Amfilogovs") in the
[full-text search of the SPbII РАН inventories](https://spbiiran.sovadev.pro/?r=%D0%90%D0%BC%D1%84%D0%B8%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%8B%D1%85)
returns only page 1189 of this inventory; there is no separate publication or images of the three folios in the open
index. So the next step is not another general search but an order for a specific item. The archive accepts requests
for digital copies at **archive@spbiiran.ru**; the procedure and forms are published in the
[official rules](https://spbiiran.ru/pravila_raboty/).

The date unexpectedly brings the find close to an already known document: on **5 October 1640** Afonka Anpilogov of
Oryol wrote a transfer book (otkaznaya kniga) about land in the same Korchakov stan; the tsar's charter appeared two
days later. This calls for reading the two together, but for now Afonka cannot be declared a participant in the
inquiry.

The main next order is now targeted and small: all three folios of **coll. 238, op. 2, d. 281, No. 16** with their
versos and the seal. If the charter cites the origin of the family pomestye, it may provide exactly the secondary trace
of a lost sixteenth-century book that is needed for the question of how the Oryol core was formed.

On 29 August 2026 a separate search for the charter itself was carried out outside the catalogue: by the exact title,
the combinations "Amfilogovs — Islenyev", the collection shelfmark, the date and the formula "Amfilogovs'
pomestye". No open photocopy, transcription or old publication was found. The full PDF of the inventory was checked on
the neighbouring pages: Nos. 13–15 concern the Mtsensk holdings and dependent people of Kirill Protasov, Nos. 17–19 again
his family archive, but no neighbouring entry reveals the settlement or the participants of the inquiry in No. 16. So the
link with the Protasovs remains the archival context of storage, not a proven party to the dispute. A further general
internet search will not advance this branch; the image of the three folios is needed.

A card has been created,
`PUB-SPBII-K238-O2-D281-N16-AMFILOGOV-ESTATE-INQUEST-1640`, and a photocopy of the inventory page has been saved. No new
profiles and no route on the map have been added: the title does not reveal personal names or the exact settlement.

## 1613–1614: the Middle Generation of the Volotskys Confirmed, but Oryol Has Dropped Out of the Hypothesis

In the Vologda service list of June 1613 published by D. E. Gnevashev, not a single man but six Volotskys from four
paternal lines were found at once:

- **Mikhaylo Mikitin syn Volottsky v otsovo mesto** ("in his father's place") — 500 chetverti;
- **Ivan Mikitin syn Volotskoy** — 300 chetverti;
- **Andrey and Aleksandr Gnevashevy deti Volotskiye** (sons of Gnevash Volotsky) — 350 chetverti each;
- **Rychko Andreyev syn Volotskoy** — 250 chetverti;
- **Ivan Bakhteyarov syn Volotskoy** — in the heading for men from their fathers' pomestya and
  minors (nedorosli).

This is the first independent check of the middle part of the genealogical list of 1686. In it Nikita, son of
Tikhan-Rychko, is indeed given the sons Mikhail, Tikhan and Ivan. The coincidence of Mikhail and Ivan Nikitich in one
service town shows that family memory preserved real names. But the same list reveals the lines of Gnevash and Bakhteyar,
omitted by the genealogy, and a separate **Rychko Andreyevich**. He must not be confused with Rychko Onfalov/Anfalov syn
from the surety record of 1563.

In January 1614 the Vologda syn boyarsky **Mikhail Volotsky**, together with a strelets sotnik (company commander),
delivered to Moscow the interrogation records (rasprosnye rechi) on the devastation of Pomorye. Given the rare combination
of name, family, town and the adjacent year, this is most likely Mikhail Nikitich from the 1613 list, but the patronymic is
not repeated in the second entry.

For the Oryol search the conclusion is negative but important. The Volotskys look like a stable Vologda service family even
after the Time of Troubles; neither the list of 1613 nor the report of 1614 names Oryol, Anfilogovs or a transfer to the
south. So the similar-sounding chain Onfal/Anfal — Volotskys should be kept as the history of the name and a control of the
late genealogy, but at present it does not explain the appearance of the Oryol core of the 1590s.

In addition, the published documents 1–38 of the first volume of «Акты Московского государства» (Acts of the Muscovite
State) for 1571–1595 and their name index have been checked in full. The forms Anfal/Onfal/Anfil/Onfil/Anpil/Ampil and the
sons of Rychko claimed by the genealogy are not there. The only Aleksandr with a close family form in the list of 1577 is
printed in the act itself as **«Олександро Янкулов сын Волоской»** (Oleksandro Yankulov syn Voloskoy), whereas the index
normalises him as Aleksandr Yakovlevich Volotsky. No form makes him the Aleksandr Tikhonovich of the genealogy.

Primary reference of the list: **РНБ, Hermitage collection, No. 394, fols. 46–51**. Publication: D. E. Gnevashev,
«“Сыскные” списки вологодских дворян и детей боярских. 1606–1613 гг.» ("Search" Lists of the Vologda Dvoryane and Deti
Boyarskie, 1606–1613), *Исторический архив* (Istorichesky Arkhiv), 2007, No. 5, pp. 184–199.
Card: `PUB-GNEVASHEV-1613-VOLOGDA-VOLOTSKYS-RYCHKO-ANDREEV`.

No profiles of people and no points on the map are created: the Volotskys have not yet been proven to be bearers of the
surname series under study, and no resettlement to Oryol has been found.

## The Belgorod Trace of 1616 Is Now Localised to an Archival File

The line **«Никита Ефимов сын Анпилогов»** (Nikita Efimov syn Anpilogov) published by E. V. Krivtsova belongs to the
pomestye holders of the village of Sazhnoye in 1616. The primary complex has now been established precisely: **РГАДА, f.
1209, op. 1, pt. 2, d. 15828, «Приправочная книга 1616 г. города Белгорода и Саженского стана Белгородского уезда»**
("Reference book of 1616 of the town of Belgorod and Sazhensky stan of Belgorod district"). An independent scholarly
publication by I. V. Maslenkov quotes entries from the same section on fols. 134v and 154, which confirms the shelfmark and
the composition of the file.

However, the folio with Nikita's own line has not yet been found. So the name and the surname form remain a reading after
Krivtsova's publication, not our own transcription of a scan. It is particularly important not to merge him automatically
with Mikifor Efimov syn Onpilogov, known later in the Belgorod circle. The next task is no longer an abstract search for the
book but an examination of the Sazhnoye section of the specific file and the establishment of the folio between the
neighbouring holders Yakov Petrov syn Ozerov and Mitya Semyonov syn Chaplygin.

Source for the archival attribution: I. V. Maslenkov, «О происхождении некоторых служилых родов Масловых» (On the Origin of
Some Service Families of the Maslovs), *Генеалогический вестник* (Genealogichesky Vestnik), issue 71, 2024, pp. 154–155,
notes 76 and 78.

## The First Exact Breakdown of the Oryol Core: Deti Boyarskie and Cossacks in 1571

Changing the search unit from a single surname to executive field rosters produced a document between the already known
records of 1570 and 1572. On **21 February 1571** a boyar verdict assigned to the golova on the Oskol at the mouth of the Ublya
**ten deti boyarskie and twenty Cossacks from Oryol**. To the golovy on the Seym at the mouth of the Khon another **nine Oryol
deti boyarskie and twenty Cossacks** were assigned.

This is the first record found in which the early Oryol complement is not hidden behind the general word «люди» ("people") but
divided into two service categories. The document does not allow ten and nine to be added up into nineteen unique men: these may
have been different groups, shifts or two details of part of one corps. But already four to five years after the fortress was
founded, Oryol acts as an independent service town and simultaneously sends men in two distant field directions.

The composition of these two details has now been revealed as well. On the Oskol the Oryol men stood together with men of
**Dedilov and Krapivna, Dankov, Novosil and Mtsensk**; on the Seym, again with men of **Dedilov and Krapivna**, as well as
Mtsensk and Novosil. Both mixed groups were to be relieved three times by men **«из тех же городов по той же росписи»** ("from
the same towns according to the same roster"). This is no longer chance proximity: in 1571 Oryol and Dedilov belonged to a stable
mechanism of joint field service.

For the candidacy of the Dedilov desyatsky (squad leader) Ivanko Nechaev this is significant. Joint service of the two corporations
is documented seventeen to eighteen years before his record of 1588/89 and twenty-four years before the appearance in Oryol of Ivan
Nechaev syn Anpilogov. It explains a real channel of acquaintance, suretyship or subsequent transfer. But the verdict gives no names,
so Ivanko's move from Dedilov to Oryol itself still remains a hypothesis, not a ready route.

It is especially important not to confuse two layers of the record. The golovy for the Oskol and the Seym were to be chosen from
Murom, Meshchera, Ryazan, Tula and Kashira. This is the possible origin of the **commanders**, not of the Oryol rank and file; these
five towns must not be turned into a route of Anfilogovs. The names of the Oryol men themselves are omitted in the verdict.

The search tactic is now narrower still: what is needed are the executive annexes to the two assignments — the Oryol roster of men
for the golova on the Oskol and the Oryol roster for the golovy on the Seym, their town counterpart copies and later oklad extracts
referring to the service of 1571. It is precisely there that a name, a father, a former town or a pomestye oklad might have stood.

Source: «Акты Московского государства» (Acts of the Muscovite State), vol. I, pp. 6–7; in the publication, Book of the Moscow Desk
No. 1, fols. 39–46; the modern complex is РГАДА,
f. 210, op. 6, d. 1. Card:
`PUB-AMG-T1-P6-7-OREL-CHILDREN-BOYARS-1571`.
[Open text of the publication](https://forum.vgd.ru/1758/63616/0.htm).

On 26 August 2026 what was checked was no longer a printed summary but the whole specified block of folios in the official
РГАДА digital copy. Spreads **0050–0057** successively cover fol. 38v to fol. 46 and have been saved in the project. The check
removed an important fork: fols. 39–46 are the single boyar verdict itself with the town numbers, not a verdict together with
a lost nominal annex. There is no separate roster of Oryol deti boyarskie, no names of Anfilogovs, their fathers or former towns
within this block. Consequently, what must be sought is an independent executive list, its counterpart copy or a late oklad
extract, not yet another line on a neighbouring folio of the same act.
[Official digital copy of the file](http://rgada.info/kueh/index2.php?str=210_6_1).

The map does not change: these are service deployments of the general Oryol corps, not a proven route of a bearer of the surname
under study.

## The Oryol Hundred of 1580–1581: the List Existed, but Rzhev Was Not the Town of Origin

The new tactic — to start not from the surname but from surviving references to nominal registers — produced another exact
documentary address. The Sovereign's Razryad of 1580 names the Oryol voivode Prince Mikhail Samsonovich Turenin and explicitly
orders him to **gather the Oryol men** (orlyane) for the Rzhev muster. The draft regimental roster originally had **a hundred Oryol
men «по спискам, которые писались во Ржеве»** ("according to the lists that were written at Rzhev"); the lists were to be handed
over by the boyar Prince Semyon Danilovich Pronsky. When the regiments were redistributed, this phrase was struck out and replaced
by a different complement. It cannot be read as a final order, but it proves that a nominal list of the Oryol hundred really
existed.

A separate roster of spring–summer 1581 independently confirms the contingent itself: among the servicemen who remained in Veliky
Novgorod are recorded **«орлян 100 ч.»** ("Oryol men, 100 persons"). The sequence is thus: the gathering of the Oryol men at Oryol —
a general muster and lists at Rzhev — the service of a hundred Oryol men in Novgorod. This is the first chain found in which the Oryol
service core can be traced through several stages of one distant deployment.

The main caveat changes the geographical conclusion. Rzhev was not the former town of the Oryol men but the place of a general army
assembly. The Razryad expressly sends the Oryol voivode for the men and orders him to gather them **from Oryol**. So a resettlement
Rzhev → Oryol must not be drawn on the map. The same applies to Novgorod: what is documented there is the service of a general
hundred, not of a specific Anfilogov.

On the other hand, the best address so far for a personal search for the fathers' generation of 1594/95 has been found. What is
needed now is not abstract "Oryol desyatni" but the counterpart copies of the Rzhev muster of 1580, the pay register, the lists of
absentees (netchiki) and the list of the hundred Oryol men itself. They should be checked at once for all seven fathers — Mikula,
Vasily, Timofey, Persha, Pakhom, Nechay and Terenty — and especially for the rare family formula Mikula → Vasily, Timofey and Karp.

### Two Hundred of the Best from Rzhev: the List of Names Was Ordered to Be Sent to Moscow

A search not for the surname itself but for the documents that arose around the Rzhev muster produced a more specific find. In the
captured campaign archive of Prince Vasily Dmitriyevich Khilkov an original sovereign's order of **5 September 1580** has survived.
Prince Semyon Danilovich Pronsky is ordered to send from Rzhev to Khilkov **«детей боярских двести человек лутчих з головами»**
("two hundred of the best deti boyarskie with their golovy"). After their arrival Khilkov was to report to the sovereign and
**«списки имян их к нам прислали»** ("send us the lists of their names"). The order reached the voivode on 9 September; it was brought
by Ivan Bezobrazov.

Originally a strong comparison arose here with the struck-out line about **100 Karachev men and 100 Oryol men**: Pronsky, Rzhev, the
time and the number 200 all matched. A complete reading of all the corrections in the draft forced this conclusion to be weakened.
Pronsky held lists of men of Ryazan, Tula, Yaroslavl, Oryol, Karachev and other towns; several groups of 200 men occur in different
redactions. Consequently, the order of 5 September cannot confidently be regarded as a continuation of precisely the Oryol–Karachev
line. Its town composition has not yet been established.

The wording of the document specifies exactly what should be sought. **«Лутчих»** ("the best") means picked men, the fittest for
campaign service; **«з головами»** ("with golovy") means organised detachments with their own commanders; **«списки имян»** ("lists of
names") means a personal list, not a numerical summary. The names were to be sent **«к нам»** ("to us"), to the sovereign's Moscow
chancellery.

This still leaves a separate, valuable reply document, but the Oryol tactic now rests on a more direct find. The complex of Khilkov's
campaign chancellery in
Warsaw has been published in full: the order itself is there, but there is no reply list of names. So the priority object is the Moscow
incoming papers after 9 September 1580, Khilkov's report on the arrival of the golovy and deti boyarskie, its annexes and later Razryad
or pomestye extracts about the service. But it must be kept separate from the directly attested Oryol list.

### The Full Oryol List Consisted of Two Hundred Men

The most important correction from the full reading of the draft lies in another redaction of the campaign roster. Among the first
dispatches from Rzhev it literally records: **«орлян 200 человек»** ("Oryol men, 200 persons"). Further on the compiler describes the
structure of this corps even more precisely: **«орляне, которые в Ржеве же писались, половина их 100 человек»** ("the Oryol men who were
likewise written down at Rzhev, half of them, 100 persons").

So a hundred Oryol men is not the whole Oryol list of this muster. In 1580 there existed a nominally recorded Oryol campaign contingent
of **200 men**, which for assignments was divided into two halves of a hundred each.
This is not a general census of all the servicemen of Oryol: in 1572 the town put out 350 men and separately 100 Cossacks, so different
rosters count different service selections. Another struck-out redaction shows one half next to a hundred Karachev men. The hundred
Oryol men left in Veliky Novgorod in 1581 may also be one of these halves, although the identity of individual men has not been proven.

For the search for origins this is significant: what is needed is not only the "list of a hundred Oryol men" but **the full list of two
hundred Oryol men written down at Rzhev**, and the counterpart copies of both its halves. This is the earliest precisely delimited
personal corps of Oryol. It may have included men of the generation of Mikula, Vasily, Timofey, Persha, Pakhom, Nechay and Terenty, but
so far not one of these names has been read.

The relevant fragment of the file **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 9, d. 232** begins on fol. 289v; the publication is marked fols. 290–315v, and the
next described entry begins on fol. 322. At first it seemed that the unpublished **fols. 316–321** might hold a continuation or an annex
with the list of names. A full collation of the 1914 edition weakened this hypothesis: at the end of the transcribed text on fol. 315v the
editors explicitly noted that **«дальнейший текст не сохранился»** ("the further text has not survived"), and they reconstructed it from
the parallel Razryad in the «Древняя Российская Вивлиофика» (Ancient Russian Library). So the six folios still need to be examined to check
their physical condition, but they are no longer regarded as the main address for the name search. More likely candidates are a separate
town list, a register of «приездов и нетов» ("arrivals and absences") or a reply report from Moscow.

**On 26 August 2026 the file was found in the digital reading room of the ГИС УИАД.**
This is no longer a supposed future order for filming: an electronic copy of the whole
**РГАДА, f. 210, op. 9, d. 232** exists. The direct state catalogue card:
[file 210-9-232](https://online.archives.ru/guide/10000000001014/10000000157014/10000000358014/10000474722014/);
[unofficial index of digitised files](https://metrics.tilda.ws/gisuiad?fund=210&inv=9&case=232#rgada_opisi).
The catalogue gives the dating of the whole composite item as 1596–1650 and the title «Столбцы Московского стола Разрядного приказа»
("Scrolls of the Moscow Desk of the Razryad Prikaz"); this does not change the attribution of the enclosed draft of 1580. Viewing of the
copies is provided after ordering the service and signing in through ЕСИА (the state identity system). The state portal does not open
from the current network, so fols. 316–321 themselves have not yet been viewed. When access is available they should be checked as a
control fragment; when issued by carrier, this is microfilm No. 5, fols. 287–375. The main search moves to the separate nominal registers
of the same campaign.

Source: **AGAD, Archiwum Zamoyskich, sygn. 3032, k. 30–31**;
«Документы Ливонской войны (подлинное делопроизводство приказов и воевод)» (Documents of the Livonian War: the Original Records of
Chancelleries and Voivodes), part III, document 27. Card:
`PUB-AGAD-AZ3032-K30-31-RZHEV-200-NAMED-LISTS-1580`.

The map does not change: the document does not yet name a single Anfilogov and does not prove a resettlement route of the family.

Sources: «Разрядная книга 1475–1605 гг.» (Razryad Book of 1475–1605), vol. III, pt. I. Moscow, 1984,
fols. 697–698 of the main manuscript; the draft Razryad of 1580 — historical
shelfmark РГАДА, f. 210, op. 9, d. 232, beginning on fol. 289v, published
foliation fols. 290–315v;
V. I. Buganov, «Документы о Ливонской войне» (Documents on the Livonian War), «Археографический ежегодник
за 1960 год» (Archaeographic Yearbook for 1960). Moscow, 1962, document No. 6, fols. 282–288 of the same scroll.
Card: `PUB-RK-1580-1581-OREL-HUNDRED-RZHEV-NOVGOROD`.

The map does not change: no member of the surname series has yet been named in the hundred.

The modern shelfmark has now been checked against the inventory rather than reconstructed from the old publication. The description of
**f. 210, op. 9, d. 232** explicitly notes on fol. 289v «розряд черной, поход ко Пскову великого князя Семиона Бекбулатовича Тверского»
("a draft razryad, the campaign to Pskov of Grand Prince Simeon Bekbulatovich of Tver"), and the next independent entry begins on fol. 322.
An electronic copy of the file already exists in the ГИС УИАД, but access to viewing is arranged as a service through the state portal.
After collation with the explicit caveat of the 1914 editors, fols. 316–321 remain a control order, not
a priority candidate for the list of two hundred Oryol men.

On 26 August 2026 the whole section of the official inventory relating to file 232 and the descriptions of the neighbouring storage
units were also examined. The inventory singles out on fol. 289v the draft razryad of Simeon Bekbulatovich's campaign, but names no
nominal annex of Oryol men with it. This does not prove that the list was lost: it may have remained an undescribed annex, a counterpart
copy in another scroll or a separate register. However, there are at present no grounds for looking for it among the neighbouring files
by inventory title alone. The main attention moves to the related pay-distribution, arrival and absentee documents; fols. 316–321 are
needed only for a final check.

On the same day the current dynamic database of the project «Дом предков» (House of Ancestors), which has grown to **421,095 name
records**, was checked again. Queries for the forms Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Onpilogov, Onfilogov, Ampilogov, Anfilofov and for the personal
names Anfilog, Anfilof, Anfilofy, Anpilog produced no new sixteenth-century bearer. Exact surname hits begin with the already known Oryol
layer of the seventeenth century. This is a negative result of an open index, not proof that people were absent from the unrevealed Rzhev
list of 1580.

## The Earliest Large Count of the Oryol Core: 1572

A primary regimental roster has been found that markedly strengthens the chronology. At the beginning of 1572 Prince Grigory Dolgorukov
was ordered to be brought out «из украинных мест с Орла» ("from the Ukraine [frontier] places, from Oryol") to the regiment of the right
hand **with 350 men**. Separately, **100 Cossacks with arquebuses (pishchali) from Oryol** are recorded in the watch regiment. This is not a
late retelling but a publication of a draft original of the Razryad Prikaz:
**РГАДА, f. 210, scrolls of the Moscow Desk, d. 463, stolpik 3, fols. 21–85**.

The structure of the roster makes it possible to clarify the meaning of the 350 men. They are included in the regiment's total before the
separate listing of streltsy and Cossacks; the final total distinguishes the deti boyarskie from these service categories. So the 350 men
were most likely the Oryol group of deti boyarskie. This is a strong conclusion from the arithmetic and the structure of the document, but
the line itself does not name their category with a separate word.

The record does not yet answer the question "where did they come to Oryol from". The formula «с Орла» ("from Oryol") denotes **the current
service town and its contribution to the field army**, not the place of birth or of former service. But a firm lower date has now been
established: roughly five and a half years after the fortress was founded, two distinct and fairly large campaign contingents already
existed in Oryol. The document we need should be sought in an even narrower window — between the founding of the town in 1566 and the
compilation of this roster at the beginning of 1572.

Source: V. I. Buganov, «Документы о сражении при Молодях в 1572 г.» (Documents on the Battle of Molodi in 1572),
«Исторический архив» (Istorichesky Arkhiv), 1959, No. 4, pp. 175–177,
[scan of the publication](https://www.reenactor.ru/ARH/PDF/Byganov_00.pdf).
It is this publication that corrects the chain of references: V. M. Nedelin cited the figures
350 and 100 with a reference not to an archival document but to the popular book by
A. R. Andreyev, «Неизвестное Бородино» (The Unknown Borodino) (1997, pp. 214, 216).

A rereading of the instruction (nakaz) itself changed the direction of the search. Behind the total figure stood real nominal lists: «роспись
и списки» ("the roster and lists") had already been sent to the voivodes, the deti boyarskie were ordered to be assigned anew to golovy, the
list sent to Vorotynsky, and **«у себя противень оставити»** ("a counterpart copy kept with oneself"). The men with arquebuses were to be
