corrects the earlier over-confident wording and creates no routes on the
map. As early as 1572, 350 Oryol
servicemen and 100 Cossacks really did take part in a campaign, which proves the rapid gathering of a large core but
does not reveal the towns of its origin.

### 1566/67: the first named commanders, but not yet the first settlers

In the entry for 7075 of the Razryad book, the new town enters the annual
list of the frontier (ukrainnye) fortresses for the first time with the formula: **«На Орле воевода князь Василей
Ростовской да Володимер Безобразов»** ("At Oryol the voivode Prince Vasily Rostovskoy and Volodimer Bezobrazov"). The date covers September 1566 – August
1567. In 1571/72 **Vasily Grigoryevich Kolychev and
Denis Ivashkin** already stand at Oryol, and Kolychev then goes out «с Орла» ("from Oryol") to the regiment of the right
hand. In 1572/73 this pair is replaced by **Prince Pyotr Ivanovich Khvorostinin and Mikhail
Nazaryev syn Glebov**; Khvorostinin also leads the Oryol detachment to the regiment of the right
hand.

This is the first firm sequence of Oryol command and of the incorporation of the
fortress into the general field system. But the razryad entry lists voivodes, not
rank-and-file deti boyarskie and Cossacks. The family name «Ростовской» (Rostovskoy) cannot
be read as proof of a transfer of the garrison from Rostov, nor the formula «с
Орла» as the voivode's place of birth: it is his current town post.
A Record `PUB-RK-1566-1573-OREL-FIRST-COMMAND` with a detailed section
"Names and meaning" has been created. Source: «Разрядная книга 1475–1605 гг.» [Razryad Book of 1475–1605], vol. II, pt. 2,
[the year 7075](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XV/1460-1480/RK_1475_1605/text13.htm),
[the years 7080–7081](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XV/1460-1480/RK_1475_1605/text15.htm).

### 1577: Oryol was still short of men

Even before the March verdict, in February, the Razryad Prikaz rebuilt the very
system of choosing men for field service. Act No. 20 ordered that the deti
boyarskie be chosen not by the town voivodes but by the dyaks in Moscow: fit mounted men were
to be recorded in advance by name, divided into three shifts, or "stati", and
the lists kept ready in the Razryad. Copies of the lists were passed to the field commanders
and the corresponding town voivodes.

In the composition of the service on the Seversky Donets at the mouth of the Udy, two Oryol
groups are named outright: **deti boyarskie from Oryol and Karachev** and **Cossacks from Novosil and Oryol**.
So in February 1577 the Razryad Prikaz had lists by name
of at least three shifts, within which the Oryol deti
boyarskie and Cossacks must have been. Source: «Акты Московского государства» [Acts of the Muscovite State], vol. I, No. 20,
pp. 32–34; **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6, d. 1, fols. 213–222**. The formula «из Орла» ("from Oryol")
indicates town registration in 1577, but not the place of birth or the earlier
town of a person.

The next conditional formula of the act preserves one more geographical detail not brought out before:
«а будет из Северских городов люди будут на государеве
службе на берегу и на Орле» ("and if people from the Seversk towns are on the sovereign's service on the bank and at Oryol"). Immediately before
this, **Bryansk, Pochep, Starodub and Novgorod-Seversky** are listed as the "Seversk towns". If their
men were occupied with the riverbank (bereg) service or in Oryol, the Seversk detachment at the mouth of the Udy
was to be reinforced with men from Novosil, Bolkhov and Odoev.

This is the first primary text that directly allows for the presence in Oryol, before the 1590s,
of deti boyarskie of four specifically named outside towns. But the verb
«будут на государеве службе» ("will be on the sovereign's service") describes an assignment, not a resettlement: there is
no Oryol land grant here, no moving of families, no formula «сведены жить» ("transferred to live").
So the Seversk corridor becomes an address for a comparative search of desyatni and
pomestye transfers, but is not drawn on the map as a route of Anfilogovs.

In March 1577 a boyar verdict ordered resident mounted Cossacks to be recruited in the frontier
towns. Oryol was assigned 500 men, but in fact
only 80 had been «прибрано» ("recruited"); of them, 78 were already standing in the three grades of watch service.
The shortfall was **420 men**. The text is printed in «Акты Московского
государства», vol. I (St Petersburg, 1890), No. 23, pp. 35–37; the source of the publication is
book No. 1 of the Moscow Desk, fols. 231–243. According to the printed archival inventory, this is
the present **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6, d. 1, fols. 231–243**. The inventory of 1894, on
p. 106, describes d. 1 as the Watch Book of 1571–1592 and explicitly notes
that it was printed in the first volume of «Акты Московского государства».

The most important sentence comes below the figures. The verdict ordered the names of the chosen deti
boyarskie and Cossacks to be «переписати на список подлинно порознь» ("written down on a list exactly and separately"), these
lists to be brought to Moscow and handed to the dyaks of the Razryad office (izba). So in 1577
**a list by name of the early Oryol core was drawn up**. It is not known
whether it has survived separately from the published verdict; it is now
the main archival target, more precise than a general search for "the first settlers".
In the working queue it is kept separately as
`LEAD-210-6-1-OREL-NAMED-LISTS-1577`.

The literal text of the verdict also allows the exact procedure to be reconstructed.
On 15 March 1577 the boyars ordered a memorandum (pamyat) to be sent to the Pomestny Prikaz. From there
scribes — deti boyarskie and «подьячих добрых» ("good clerks") — were to be sent to the towns
to review in person the Cossacks and the deti boyarskie with small estates, together with their horses,
dismiss the unfit and choose the fit. Then the names of those chosen were to be
written down separately for each town, and the lists, together with the pomestye and
cash oklads, brought to Moscow and handed to the dyaks in the Razryad office.
So there were several administrative traces: the memorandum to the Pomestny Prikaz,
the appointment of a scribe and a clerk, the materials of the in-person muster, land and oklad
records, and the Razryad copy of the list. A new narrow pointer is to establish the name of
the scribe actually sent to Oryol after 15 March 1577, and to look for extracts
by his name and by the formula on the increase from the old 20 to 50 chetverti.

No less important is the formula about land, because it refines the very mechanism
of manning. Fit watch Cossacks were to be given an additional 30 chetverti
**«к старому их поместью к 20 четвертям»** ("to their old pomestye of 20 chetverti"), and the unfit or horseless were to be replaced with
good mounted men **from the rank-and-file Cossacks**. Consequently, by spring 1577
this was no longer a random campaign detachment: the Oryol Cossacks had been given estates,
selection was going on within the existing corporation, and the best were transferred to the more
highly paid watch service. This supports the model of gradual local
recruitment more strongly than the model of a single ready-made detachment transferred all at once in
1566. However, "the old pomestye" only tells of settledness by 1577,
not of the place of birth or the earlier town of a person.

The digitised Watch Book has now been checked not only for the published
sections but also for the direct continuation after fol. 243. Fols. 244–249
contain a different document — a verdict on the enrolment of Ryazan deti boyarskie; on
fols. 250–252 — a verdict on disbanding the field commanders. What begins next is not
an Oryol Cossack list but a general list of boyars, okolnichie and select dvoryane.
The following leaves continue the chronological compilation up to 1592. So
the prescribed Oryol list by name **was not bound in immediately after the verdict**
and has not been found as a separate appendix in the surviving composition of the book itself.
The first stage of the queue is closed. What must be looked for are the resulting "lists"
delivered separately to the Razryad, or their duplicates, as well as memoranda and lists of pomestye
oklads that may have gone from the Razryad to the Pomestny Prikaz.

The fate of the complex can now be formulated more cautiously. To carry out
the verdict, two documentary traces must have arisen: a service list
for the Razryad and a pomestye memorandum or allotment record on the increase of land for fit
Cossacks from 20 to 50 chetverti. A separate Razryad copy has not been found in
the inventories checked, and the main archive of the Pomestny Prikaz perished in
the fire of 1626. So the dispersal or loss of the resulting papers is
**probable**, but not proven for each copy. A duplicate or an extract
may have survived in a late conveyance book, a land lawsuit or a bundle with
an uninformative heading. The formulas sought are now more precise: «прибавлено к
старому поместью» ("added to the old pomestye"), «с 20 до 50 четвертей» ("from 20 to 50 chetverti"), «по списку из Разряду» ("according to the list from the Razryad") and references to
books of 7085/1577.

The complete printed inventory of the scrolls of the Moscow Desk — «Описание документов и
бумаг МАМЮ» [Description of the Documents and Papers of МАМЮ], bk. 11 (1899) — has already been checked for the forms 7085, 1576–1577 and 1577.
The only separately described scroll (stolpik) of this date is **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 9,
d. 1, stolpik 3**, a boyar list of 36 leaves. It has now been identified:
it is the source of the boyar list АМГ No. 26, which has the former address
«Московского стола столбец № 1, дело № 5, лл. 1—36» ("Moscow Desk scroll No. 1, file No. 5, fols. 1–36"), and the volume matches. It
contains no rank-and-file Oryol men, but it does have two assignments from the Kashira section: Prince
**Yury Mikhaylovich Meshchersky** is noted «на Осколе» ("on the Oskol"), and **Grigory Fyodorov
Andreev-Pavlov** «на Орле» ("at Oryol"). So this is not a hidden personal list,
but it provides two new name keys to the resulting papers.

The archival address of act АМГ No. 27 has been corrected separately. At the end of its publication
stands «Москов. ст., кн. № 1-й, л. л. 253—268» ("Moscow Desk, bk. No. 1, fols. 253–268"), that is, **РГАДА, f. 210,
op. 6, d. 1, fols. 253–268**. It is this act that names the commanders, the town quotas
and the three field shifts. It must no longer be confused with the boyar list of inventory 9.

A full reading of the act has clarified under whose names the appendices sought
should be filed. On the Seversky Donets at the mouth of the Udy, three shifts with fifteen Oryol
Cossacks each were successively commanded by **Ignaty Andreevich Tyutchev,
Ivan Semichev and Afanasy Panyutin**. On the Oskol at the mouth of the Ublya, the Kashira man
**Mikhail Eskov** had a mixed group of 34 deti boyarskie — from Tula,
Plava, Solova and Oryol. The number of Oryol men within these 34 is not given.
It is Eskov, and not Prince Meshchersky who replaced him, who is the main key to
the list by name of the Oryol deti boyarskie on the Oskol: under Meshchersky the act
already lists only Tula and Mtsensk deti boyarskie.

The boundaries of the act have also been checked against the official digital copy of the РГАДА file
itself. Before the start of the text on fol. 253 there are blank leaves; the act ends on
fol. 268v, and on fol. 269 a new document of 7086 already begins. So
the list by name is missing not only from the printed version: it is not attached to the act in
the manuscript bundle either. It must be sought as an independent resulting paper
or as an extract quoted in a later land or service file.

An exact full-text search for the four new keys has so far returned only the primary
publication of act No. 27 and secondary retellings of it. No separate call number for an appendix or
a list by name has turned up. This closes the repetition of the same queries in
open indexed texts, but not the manuscript scrolls, whose heading might have
carried only the commander's name, the date or the first phrase of a report.

For Mikhail Eskov an earlier Kashira trace has been found, but only at the level of
the surname. A reconstruction of the Kashira desyatnya of 1570 from a late leaf-by-leaf
alphabet contains the heading «Есковы» (Eskovs). The official inventory at the same time corrects
the citation: unit 853, mentioned online, is a former number; the modern address
is **РГАДА, f. 388, op. 1, d. 102**, «Книга справок Разрядного архива» [Reference Book of the Razryad Archive] of 1787.
The available reconstruction does not give the name Mikhail. So the line
«1579 Кашира Михаил Есков» ("1579 Kashira Mikhail Eskov") found in a
secondary list cannot be considered a new
find: for now it looks like a conflation of the family heading of 1570 with
the field act of 1577.

Act No. 27, on the other hand, shows the February order being carried out. In spring–autumn
1577 three shifts stood successively at the mouth of the Udy. Each had
**15 Oryol Cossacks** and 15 from Novosil. In total it documents 45
shift places, although without names one cannot claim that these were exactly 45 different
people: the composition may have partly repeated. Publication: [АМГ, vol. I, No. 27,
pp. 43–53](https://vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/21-40/27.htm).
A separate Oryol Cossack list still does not appear in the inventory. A check of
the digital copy has ruled out the simple version of an appendix directly inside
the Watch Book. The list may have ended up in a miscellaneous bundle without the word
«Орёл», passed together with the pomestye oklads to the Pomestny Prikaz, or not
survived.

The exact call number of the Watch Book has also been checked in the official РГАДА catalogue.
The archive has now opened a full digital copy of **f. 210, op. 6, d. 1**: this
made it possible to check the neighbouring leaves directly, not from a single printed
heading. The old remark about having to order images first no longer
applies.

This is not a list of places from which the Oryol deti boyarskie came. The act lists
Shatsk, Ryazhsk, Dankov, Epifan, Dedilov, Krapivna, Novosil and Oryol as separate
town levies. It proves something else: eleven years after
the founding of the fortress the state was still urgently manning its population,
and the local pool of men was not enough for the prescribed norm.

The neighbouring act No. 18 of the same Watch Book, dated by the editors to 1577,
explains who exactly made up the figure of 78. The Oryol system had **nine
watch posts (storozhi)**: three unmixed and six mixed with men from Mtsensk, Karachev and
Rylsk. One shift, or "statya", required 26 Cossacks, three stati —
exactly 78. They served «с земель» ("from their lands"). The act says separately that the Oryol
voivode was sent a «роспись Орельским сторожам» ("roster for the Oryol watchmen").

The published text lists the posts and the establishment, but not people's names; a search for
Анпилог/Анфилог/Онфилог/Ампилог is empty. Modern call number: **РГАДА, f. 210,
op. 6, d. 1, fols. 121–202**; publication: [АМГ, vol. I, No. 18,
pp. 19–31](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/1-20/18.php).
The digital copy of the corresponding and neighbouring sections has been checked: there is no personal
copy of the Oryol roster next to fols. 121–202, no lists of the three field shifts
next to fols. 213–222 and no name appendix after fol. 243. So they
must now be sought as independently preserved resulting lists or
duplicates. The mere fact of being neighbours at a shared watch post does not prove
resettlement from Mtsensk, Karachev or Rylsk, but it shows a permanent
service network through which people could have been transferred.

Later, a full check of V. P. Zagorovsky's monograph corrected this date.
Act No. 18 itself has no year: only **15 March** is written. N. A. Popov
and the compilers of the first volume of АМГ put the year 1577 in the margin, guided by
the neighbouring documents. Zagorovsky compared the text with the clearly dated
appointment of Prince **Ivan Okhlyabinin** to the watch post «на Сосне усть Ливен» ("on the Sosna at the mouth of the Livenka") and
with decisions on watch service: the act belongs to **15 March 1576**.

This does not move the whole complex to 1576. The February lists of field service
and the verdict on the shortfall of 420 Cossacks belong to 1577. The correction gives
a valuable year-on-year cross-section: in 1576 the establishment of the nine watch posts was 78 Cossacks, and
a year later Oryol had only 80 Cossacks, including the same 78 watchmen. That is,
there was no mass influx of people between the two decisions. The early core
was stable but small; attempts were then made to expand it by internal selection
of rank-and-file Cossacks and by bringing in deti boyarskie with small estates. The page with
the dating is preserved in the card
`PUB-ZAGOROVSKY-1991-OREL-WATCH-1576-CORRECTION`; the map is unchanged.

### A new archival corridor: fond 141

After the negative check of the Watch Book (Storozhevaya kniga), priority has shifted to
**РГАДА, f. 141, op. 1, "Prikaz Cases of Old Years"** (Prikaznye dela starykh let). This is not a thematic
Oryol fond but a collection of surviving files from various Moscow prikazy (chancelleries), including
the Razryad, Pomestny and Gorodovoy prikazy. The official guide expressly notes in it
files on the building and settlement of Voronezh and Yelets in 1585–1593,
the 1593 roster of the Yelets deti boyarskie, and documents on the enrolment of various
service groups into land and cash oklady (entitlements). So this is exactly where a
duplicate (protiven) of an Oryol list, a Razryad memorandum or a
land extract could have survived by chance without receiving a separate "Oryol" heading in the late inventory.

The detailed reference work needed has been identified precisely: N. P. Voskoboinikova,
«Описание древнейших документов архивов московских приказов XVI — начала
XVII веков» [Description of the Oldest Documents of the Archives of the Moscow Prikazy, 16th to Early 17th Centuries], issue 1: **1505–1613** (Moscow, 1994). The available official
indexes did not show a separate early Oryol file. Now a
digitised copy of the first issue from the University of Michigan has also been found:
**HathiTrust mdp.39015054316867, 440 scans**. The book is closed to page-by-page
reading, but the HathiTrust Research Center lawfully publishes a per-page OCR
word list — a set of words and their frequencies without the order of the text. That is enough to
check years, geography and search forms.

The check gave a strict result. The exact number **1577** appears on digital
pages 111, 114, 233 and 241 (printed pp. 89, 92, 211 and 219), but there are no forms of Oryol or
of Oryol service on those pages: they deal with the Livonian towns, the Ustyug
chetvert and northern land documents. The substantive forms «Орла», «Орле»,
«орловского», «орловскому» in the main part are concentrated on digital
pages 165, 167, 172, 179, 181 and 182, that is, on printed **pp. 143, 145,
150, 157, 159–160**. The neighbouring vocabulary unambiguously ties them to Yelets,
streltsy and Cossacks, the Oryol Cossack head, Osip and Golovachev in
1592–1593. This is the already known **Oryol → Yelets** flow, not the original
recruitment (pribor) of Oryol.

This localisation has now been taken down to an exact archival address. All six
pages describe not different unknown files but a single unglued scroll (stolbets):
**РГАДА, f. 141, op. 1, 1593, d. 1**. Printed p. 143 covers fols. 7–21,
including the charter and petition of Trenka Zakharov syn Logachev on fols. 13–14;
p. 145 covers fols. 33–44 and the charter to Grigory Ivanov syn Andreev on fol. 36;
p. 150 covers fols. 89–96, where there are a copy of the charter on fol. 90, Grigory's repeated
petition on fol. 92 and the petition of Gerasim Yakovlev syn Bykov on
fol. 95; p. 157 covers fols. 170–188 and the Oryol report (otpiska) about Trenka on fol. 179;
pp. 159–160 cover fols. 212–232, including Gerasim Bykov's second petition on
fol. 229. The full 2001 edition reproduces the whole 248-folio scroll. A repeated
search across the identified folios and the whole file found no Anfilogovs or variants
of the surname series. So these pages no longer remain an unchecked
candidate for the source of the Oryol core.

A separate check of the surname also matters: the forms
«Анпилогов» (Anpilogov) found in the reference work stand next to the initials **G. N.** and the publication title «Новые
документы...» [New Documents...]. This is the historian Grigory Nikolaevich Anpilogov, not a man of the 16th
century. On printed pp. 124 and 126 the rare forms «Анфилоще» and
«Анфилошевых» occur in northern land acts of 1625; without the word order one cannot
establish the full name and role, so this is a separate lead, but not yet a profile.

Conclusion: the detailed inventory does not yield a simple file headed "Oryol list
of 1577". The next step is to order visual pages of the entries already localised
and to check archival bundles of 1576–1578 whose headings do not contain
the word "Oryol" at all. Audit card:
`PUB-VOSKOBOINIKOVA-F141-1505-1613-OREL-AUDIT`.
[Official annotation of the fond](https://guides.rusarchives.ru/terms/9/2550/prikaznye-dela-staryh-let),
[fond card in the reading room](https://online.archives.ru/search/full/10000000001014/10000000098014/),
[HathiTrust digital copy](https://babel.hathitrust.org/cgi/pt?id=mdp.39015054316867),
[description of the HTRC search layer](https://htrc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/COM/pages/43295914).

## A full search of the whole volume — done on 21 August 2026

The volume «Писцовые книги Московского государства» [Cadastral Books of the Muscovite State], pt. I, sect. 2, has been examined
in full: about 1,600 printed pages, sixteen districts, 83,807 lines of OCR
(`public/archive/evidence/moscha-regia/moscha-regia_djvu.txt`). The search is fuzzy: all
45,250 unique tokens were reduced to a normalised form and matched against
the target spellings with a Levenshtein distance ≤ 2, so that OCR would not swallow
hits. Full log: `data/genealogy/indexes/moscha-regia-sweep.json`.

### The result is negative, and that is the answer

| district | pages | hits |
|---|---|---|
| Yaroslavl, Rostov, Uglich, Poshekhonye | 1–39 | 0 |
| **Tver** | 40–403 | **1 — a place name** (the hamlet of Onfilogovo, Khorvach volost, p. 111) |
| Staritsa, Beloozero | 404–420 | 0 |
| Polotsk and Polotsk povet | 421–566 | 0 |
| Vyazma | 567–831 | 0 |
| Medyn | 832–850 | 0 |
| **Oryol** | 853–1073 | **8** |
| Tula and Tula district | 1073–1260 | 0 |
| Dedilov | 1261–1299 | 0 |
| Kashira | 1299–1537 | 0 |
| Venev | 1537–1588 | 0 |
| Epifan | 1589–1595 | 0 |

**Not a single Anfilogov in Tula, Dedilov, Kashira, Venev or Epifan**. This is
an important negative result, but it does not refute the northern
route that has been found: Belyov, Novosil, Krapivna, Serpukhov, Kaluga, Mtsensk, Bolkhov and
Karachev are either not represented in this volume by comparable lists or
require other sets of documents. Besides, the father of the family may have been registered
under a different surname, and Anfilogovs may have taken their byname from his given name.

### What instead

Perhaps the surname **took shape locally**, but for now this is a hypothesis. The eight households of 1594/95 are
one generation that took its byname from a father or grandfather named
**Anpilog** (Anfilog). He himself still bore a different surname, and that is why
he cannot be found in the documents as "Anfilogov".

That this is not a guess is shown by the entry on **p. 948 of the same volume**, Korchakov
stan, the same neighbourhood: «что было за дѣдомъ его за **Анпилогомъ за Ивановымъ
сыномъ Онаньина**» ("which had been held by his grandfather, Anpilog Ivanov syn Onanyin"). The given name Anpilog with the surname Onanyin, held by a previous
holder of a share (zherebye) in the very stan where Anfilogovs sit in 1594/95.
The formula calls him the grandfather of an adult landholder, but it gives neither the time
of his tenure nor his years of life, and it does not allow him to be confidently assigned to the first half
of the 16th century. Record created: `PUB-PKOU-1594-P948-ANPILOG-ONANIN`.

This adds a second framing of the task. Alongside the search for a possible
transfer of servicemen, we need to find **who Anfilog was** — under what surname a man of that name
was registered in the 1550s–1570s, whose sons Vasily, Mikula, Persha,
Nechay, Pakhom and Timofey sit in Bazdyrevo, Belaya, Sofonova and
on the Radomlya in 1594.

### Register of the name before 1600: no single centre is visible

So as not to substitute a search for a ready-made surname for the search for origins, a
separate prosopographical register has been compiled of all bearers, confirmed by the project, of the
given name **Anfilof / Anfilog** and of direct patronymic forms before 1600.
It includes twelve personal entries from seven regional settings.

- In the **Ryazan land**, between 1483 and 1503, a prince's man
  Antipa Anfilogov syn is named; his patronymic attests a father Anfilog of the preceding
  generation. In the 1530s the grand prince's peasant
  Anfilog Ivanov syn Konstyantinova appears independently.
- In **Yaroslavl district**, around 1495–1497, Anfilof Selivanov is known.
- In **Kashin district**, Peshok Anfilofov syn Lodygin takes part three times in
  land acts before 1516; his father Anfilof belongs to the preceding
  generation.
- A northern Russian deed of purchase of 1516 names two different
  Anfilofs at once: the former owner Anfilofy Ivanov syn and the witness (posluh) Anfilof Moiseev
  syn.
- In **Uglich district** in 1536/37 lives Tonkoy Onfilogov; the dependent
  form preserves another, older Onfilog, most probably the father.
- The Tver **Onfilogovo** requires an eponym Onfilog of its own, but does not
  reveal his identity.
- In Oryol district before the book of 1594/95 there was Anpilog Ivanovich
  Onanyin, a man of a different family.

The distribution is too wide to turn these people automatically into
one lineage. It shows something else: the form in **-ov** served as a direct patronymic
long before there was a stable surname. So the Oryol core may have consisted of
several families in which the same rare name passed independently from
father to sons and then became fixed as a family name.

Ryazan remains a strong direction thanks to the recurrence of the name and the known
chancellery formulas for transfer to new towns. The Kashin–Uglich circle matters
for the Tver eponym, but so far shows no service in Oryol. Within Oryol
itself the name was already present among the Onanyins. Consequently, the next search
must distinguish at least three models: a Ryazan or upper-Oka transfer,
an upper-Volga origin, and local formation in Oryol.

Card: `PUB-ANFILOG-PERSONAL-NAME-PRE1600-REGISTER`. Structured
register: `data/genealogy/indexes/anfilog-personal-name-carriers-pre1600.json`.
During the audit the missing profile of **Peshok Anfilofov syn
Lodygin** (`P9682`) was created, along with a cautious profile of the Uglich Onfilog of the preceding
generation (`P9684`).

### Onanyin turned out not to be a loner

A full check of the Oryol section showed that Anpilog Ivanovich belonged
to a sizeable nest of **Onanyins**. Besides his grandson Kondraty Vavilovich,
the book names Samoyla Yuryevich; Yakov and his young brothers Uvar and
Nikita, children of Trofim Onanyin; and Timofey Lamanovich and Daniil Olimpievich
Onanyin. The last five holding entries are concentrated in a single village,
Medna Vtoraya, near the Korchakov forest. So the name Anpilog was current not with a
random solitary holder but within another Oryol service family.
This strengthens the hypothesis of a local formation of the byname, but it still does not prove kinship between the Onanyins
and Anfilogovs.

The nest has external parallels.

- A series of private acts of the **Yuryev Ananyins** of 1504/05–1588/89 provided
  a rare control model of how an ancestor's name became a surname. In
  the second half of the 15th century lived **Ananya Petrov**. In the act of 1504/05 his
  sons are still written as «Григорей Ананьин сын Петрова» ("Grigorey, son of Ananya, of the Petrovs") and Boris, and the name
  index of the scholarly edition renders the form as **Petrov Grigory Ananyin
  syn**. In the next generation we already find, consistently, «Григорей Володимеров сын
  Ананьина» and «Богдан Григорьев сын Ананьина» ("Grigorey Volodimerov syn Ananyina", "Bogdan Grigoryev syn Ananyina"): Ananyin has taken the surname
  position. These are not ancestors of the Oryol family — their votchiny (hereditary estates) lay in Yuryev
  district and there is no document of resettlement. But the mechanism is confirmed by primary
  acts: **ancestor's name → children's patronymic → grandchildren's surname**. For Anfilogovs
  this means that the Anfilog we are looking for really could have borne a completely different
  surname. Detailed card: `PUB-ASZ-1504-1589-YURYEV-ANANYINY`.
- In 1558/59 **Levonty Onanyin** is recorded among the Moscow dyaki (clerks). In
  1562–1566 he is already a sovereign's dyak in Veliky Novgorod: he carries out
  orders, affixes the seal to charters and handles land and trade
  matters. This is an early serviceman Onanyin from before the founding of Oryol, but so far without a named
  family. The sources are collected in `PUB-RK-1558-1566-LEVONTY-ONANIN`.
- In the Epifan desyatnya (service register) of 30 October 1591, under No. 287, stands **Onton Evseev
  syn Onanyin**, an Epifan serviceman with an oklad of 30 chetverti. He is recorded
  as a netchik — one who failed to appear at the muster. Epifan lies on the supposed northern
  corridor to Oryol, and the date is only three years earlier than the Oryol book. But
  absence from a muster does not automatically mean flight or resettlement;
  Anton himself has not been found in Oryol, and the characteristic neighbours of Bazdyrevo are absent from the Epifan
  desyatnya. Card: `PUB-DESYATNI-1591-EPIFAN-ANTON-ONANIN`.
- In Pereslavl-Zalessky in 1590 **Elizarey Osipov syn Onanyin**
  came to the sovereign's service for the first time and was enrolled as a non-serving novik of
  the first category with a land oklad of 250 chetverti. In Murom in 1597
  **Ileyka Matveev syn Onanyin** is recorded as a non-serving novik with an oklad of 100
  chetverti and a first payment of 4 roubles; three Murom
  comrades stood surety for him. The editor of the publication separately names, among the new men
  of the Murom desyatnya of 1605, **Pyotr Ilyin Onanyin**.
- **Fedka Onanyin syn Novokreshchenov** of Kolomna, 1577, cannot be
  counted as a fourth bearer of the surname. By the grammar of the entry, Onanyin here
  means "son of Onanya", and Novokreshchenov is the family name.

Another outwardly convincing Moscow find turned out, after checking, to be
the same kind of patronymic case. ААЭ (Acts of the Archaeographic Expedition), vol. I, No. 237, p. 241 prints:
«Ямской Дмитрей **Онаньин сын Шугина** руку приложил» ("The yamskoy [post-station] Dmitrey Onanyin syn Shugina set his hand"); the internal date of the act is
8 December 1553. In АСЗ, vol. I, No. 147, p. 122 the same scribe on 17 June 1569
is called «ямской Дмитрий **Ананьин сын Шуйгина**». The name index of АСЗ
removes the ambiguity: **«Шуйгин Дмитрий Ананьин сын, ямской»** ("Shuygin, Dmitry Ananyin syn, yamskoy"). So
this is Dmitry, son of Ananya, with the family name Shuygin, not Dmitry
Onanyin and not the bearer of a double surname "Shuygin-Ananyin". The reference work
of S. B. Veselovsky placed him under the Onanyins and in addition dated the first
mention to 1558; the 2015 reference book on the prikazy rearranged the components
as "Dmitry Shuygin Ananyin". Both secondary renderings have now been corrected from
two publications of the acts. Card:
`PUB-AAE-ASZ-1553-1569-DMITRY-SHUYGIN`.

A full check of the published desyatni shows that Onanyin was not
the unique surname of a single upper-Oka family. Anton of Epifan remains
the closest external parallel to the Oryol nest in time and geography, but
for now this is **a trace of the surname, not a proven Epifan–Oryol route, still less
a route of Anfilogovs**. What has to be checked now is not a single surname coincidence but
family names, sureties, former estates and direct formulas such as "transferred from
town to town". Comparative card:
`PUB-DESYATNI-1577-1605-ONANIN-PARALLELS`.

### What was clarified along the way

- **The shelfmark of the Oryol book has been established.** The heading of the publication (p. 853):
  «Списокъ съ писцовыхъ книгъ 7103 (1594—1595) г. помѣстныхъ земель въ
  станахъ Неполоцкомъ, Каменскомъ, Корчаковскомъ, Ноугорскомъ и Тайчуковѣ,
  письма и мѣры **Дементья Яковлева** да подьячаго **Леонтья Софонова**. —
  Хранится въ Московскомъ Архивѣ Министерства Юстиціи подъ **№ 399** писцов.
  кн.» ("Copy from the cadastral books of 7103 (1594–1595) of pomestye lands in the Nepolotsk, Kamensk, Korchakov, Nougorsk and Taichukov stans, written and measured by Dementy Yakovlev and the clerk Leonty Sofonov. Kept in the Moscow Archive of the Ministry of Justice under No. 399 of the cadastral books.") The modern official catalogue corrects the archival reference:
  **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 329**. Number 399 is the former МАМЮ numbering,
  and the surviving manuscript is a 17th-century copy, not the scribes' original by
  Dementy Yakovlev and Leonty Sofonov.
- **All of ours but one are in Korchakov stan.** Belyay, Osip, the three
  sons of Mikula, Ivan Pershin, Sofon, Ivan Nechaev — Korchakov; Ermak Pakhomov —
  Nugorsk. No one in Nepolotsk, Kamensk or Taichukov.
- **What they were in service.** The stan heading: lands «за дѣтми боярскими за
  полковыми и за станичными и за самопалными и за осадными и за вдовами и за
  недорослями въ помѣстьяхъ» ("held in pomestye by deti boyarskie — regimental, stanitsa, samopal and siege men — and by widows and minors"). Stanitsa service means patrols into the steppe.
- **The neighbours in Bazdyrevo are named one by one**: the Trunovs, Nemtsovs, Krivolapovs,
  Bazdyrevs. The village bears the Bazdyrevs' name; at the pogost there is a church of St Nicholas
  the Wonderworker on the sovereign's land. This is a ready-made list for comparison with the
  Belgorod and Khotmyzhsk neighbours of the 1640s.

## What is already visible in the book of 1594/95

### By 1594 this is already the second generation — but the book does not name its homeland

#### Eight surname entries turned out to be three different layers

The position of the lines within the book makes it possible to refine the earlier formula "eight
Oryol landholders of 1594". **Ivan Nechaev syn Anpilogova** is recorded
after the overall total of the ordinary holdings of Korchakov stan, under a separate
heading about deti boyarskie to whom wild field was «давано в службу и на
льготу» ("given for service and on exemption"). The exemption is dated quite precisely: seven years **from 20 January 1595
to 20 January 1602**. His entry contains only wild field, hay meadow and
oak wood; there is as yet no landholder's homestead and no ploughed land. This is not simply a survey of
an existing holding but a dated act of a new service grant.

**Osip Vasilyevich Anfilogov**, by contrast, had a homestead and ploughed land, but
his share is expressly called the former pomestye of Onokha Deev syn Krivolapov.
Lev Deev syn Krivolapov continued to hold land next to him: Osip received one part
of an already existing local nest, not an empty plot. The remaining six
surname entries show landholders' homesteads and ploughed land without the formula of a
new exemption grant or a named outside predecessor.

Thus the surname circle in the book consists of at least three types:

- the already settled shares of Belyay Vasilyevich, the three Mikulin brothers, Ivan
  Pershin, Sofon Timofeevich and Ermak Pakhomovich;
- the transferred pomestye of Osip Vasilyevich;
- the new exemption grant of Ivan Nechaevich of 20 January 1595.

This rules out the simple model in which all Anfilogovs arrived as one ready-made
family in a single intake of 1594/95. But Ivan's new grant still does not reveal
his former town: the recipient could have been a newly arrived serviceman, a local
novik or a junior member of a surname circle already living in Oryol.
So the date is added to the profile and the Record, but no migration route is drawn on the
map.

For Ermak Pakhomovich, who sat apart in Nugorsk stan, an important
onomastic control has been found. In the first book of the *Novgorod Cadastral Books*
(Novgorodskie pistsovye knigi), in the census of the Derevskaya pyatina of about 1495, col. 594 quotes the line:
**«Д. Папышево: дв. Матрейко Кузмин, дв. Ермачко Пахомов»** ("Hamlet of Papyshevo: household of Matreyko Kuzmin, household of Ermachko Pakhomov"). The exact reading
is given by E. Kuznetsov in a study of the name Ermak; the title of the volume, its dating and
the page are confirmed by the bibliography and the search layer of the digital copy, but
an image of the column itself has yet to be saved. This is certainly not the Oryol Ermak:
about a hundred years separate the entries, and there is no surname Anpilogov in the Novgorod line.
But the example shows that the seemingly rare pair **Pakhom → Ermak** could
arise independently as an ordinary son's name and father's name. So a single pair
of names is not enough for a route; matching neighbours, service,
land or a direct record of a move will be needed. No profile or map point is created
for the Novgorod Ermachko.

#### The exemption holders of 1595 and the noviki of 1596 are two different cohorts

All eighteen named recipients of the Korchakov exemption grants have been
compared with the full Oryol section of the desyatnya of noviki of April 1596:
147 mentions, 146 different people. **There is not a single match of one and the same
person by given name, patronymic and family name.** This is to be expected:
someone who had received land in 1595 was no longer landless, and the desyatnya of the following
year selected precisely non-serving landless sons, brothers and nephews.

Yet five surname circles continue across the lists:

- Ivan Nechaevich Anpilogov — Pavel Terentyevich Anfilogov;
- Petrushka Fyodorovich Semenikhin — Saveley and Vasily Trofimovich and Grigory
  Ignatyevich Semenikhin;
- Mikula Pavlovich and Okinsha Sozonovich Sotnikov — Ostafey Yuryevich and Vasily
  Kurbatovich Sotnikov;
- Ivan Vasilyevich Shchelkunov — Timofey Antonovich Shchelkunov;
- Uchuk Zuevich Yarygin — Ivan Naumovich and Lomak Klementyevich Yarygin.

A shared surname without a shared patronymic does not yet establish the kinship of each
pair. But at the level of mechanism the conclusion is solid: in 1595 and 1596 the state
consistently granted land to and enrolled **different generations and branches of the very same
Oryol service families**. For Anfilogovs, Ivan Nechaevich and Pavel
Terentyevich are not two traces of one settler but two parallel people.
So the desyatnya of 1596 is also not a list of a new party of settlers;
it formalises the young intake of an already established corporation.

A full check of the whole Oryol section has given quantitative support to
what had previously been visible only in individual families. The text contains **no
fewer than 193** direct formulas «отца его/их/её поместье» ("his/their/her father's pomestye"). In roughly a hundred more
entries a plot is described as having formerly been «в поместье за» ("in pomestye held by") another holder.
This is a lower bound: the old OCR garbles letters and breaks words at hyphenations;
what is counted is formulas, not unique people.

Behind the dry number stand living families. Thirteen-year-old Vasily Ivanovich Yurlov
held his father's pomestye, which had earlier been held by Ignaty Levshin. The widow
Akulina Dolgova has children of eight, six and five years recorded, and one-year-old Ustin —
all on the paternal pomestye of Lukyan Dolgov, which before him belonged to Olesha
Aksentyev. Grigory Filippovich Dolzhonkov combined «старое его поместье» ("his old pomestye") with
what had been held by his father. Finally, Kondraty Vavilovich Onanyin held land
that had been held by his grandfather Anpilog Ivanovich Onanyin.

The point of these names is not that the Yurlovs, Dolgovs or Onanyins already lived here before
1566. Such a conclusion would be too strong: the fathers could have received grants after
the founding of the fortress. The document proves a narrower but important thing: by
1594/95 the Oryol service milieu had already gone through a wholesale change of
generation and repeated transfers of plots. This is not "an intake of the 1590s" but a long-
functioning landholding organism.

At the same time the full search did not yield a single direct formula «сведён из такого-то
города» ("brought from such-and-such a town"), «пришёл из» ("came from"), «служил прежде из» ("formerly served from") or «переведён из» ("transferred from"). The scribes described
land and succession, not the original intake. So the inheritance
statistics confirm early rooting, but do not choose between Belyov,
Novosil, Tula, Mtsensk or a local origin. What is needed is the same type of pair
already found for Aleksey Khotyaintsov of Kashira: an old pomestye in one
district and a new grant in another.

Separate audit card:
`PUB-PKOU-1594-OREL-INHERITANCE-AUDIT`. The map is unchanged: inheritance within the town
is not a proven family resettlement.
**Eight households and at least seven different fathers**: Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom,
Vasily, Mikula, Persha and — from the desyatnya of noviki of 1596 — Terenty. This is not
one small family of parents and children. But these may be several branches
of one wider lineage named after a common ancestor, Anpilog. What we see is
an adult generation whose fathers were servicemen
in the 1550s–70s, that is, **before the founding of Oryol or at the very
moment of it**.

This general model now has a concrete local candidate, not just
an abstract name. The same book documents the chain **Anpilog Ivanovich
Onanyin → his son Vavil → grandson Kondraty Vavilovich Onanyin**. Generation by generation
it runs exactly parallel to the one being sought: Mikula, Vasily, Timofey, Persha, Pakhom,
Nechay and Terenty should have lived roughly in Vavil's generation, and their children,
Anfilogovs of 1594–1596, in Kondraty's. So the working hypothesis
is now formulated strictly: could these seven fathers have been sons of one
elder Anpilog and have formed branches of Anpilogovs already in Oryol?

This is not yet a pedigree. Kondraty is still called Onanyin, the entry
does not call the seven fathers sons of Anpilog, and Ermak Pakhomovich sits apart
in Nugorsk stan. But the hypothesis has become falsifiable. The decisive search is the middle
generation: a formula **«Вавил Анпилогов сын Онаньин»** ("Vavil Anpilogov syn Onanyin") next to Mikula,
Vasily, Timofey, Persha, Pakhom, Nechay or Terenty; a word about brotherhood,
a common father, a common earlier grant or joint surety. Until such a record exists,
the profiles are not linked by kinship and no route is drawn on the map.

**They sit in nests, not scattered.** In the village of Bazdyreva on the Sychov kolodez (spring)
under the Minov forest there are five at once: the brothers Belyay and Osip Vasilyev and the three
Mikulin brothers, Vasily, Timofey and Karp, all «на их жеребью» ("on their share"). In the village of Belaya on the
Otsna, under the same Minov forest, is Ivan Pershin. This is a picture not of solitary
enrolment but of **settlement by kin, in a cluster**. That is how both the transfer of a group of
servicemen to a new town and the growth of an already local family nest could look;
the cadastral book does not allow a choice between these two explanations.

**The land is barely touched.** Ploughed land against wild field: 4 to 36, 9 to 51,
6 to 51, 5 to 19, 7 to 19, 4 to 29. The first or second decade of cultivation.

**The hamlet of Sofonova shares its name with its holder** — Sofon Timofeev
syn Anfilogov. The name is probably connected with its holder or
founder, but the book does not say outright that it was he who founded it.

### A new find: the same nest in the 1710 census of Kromy district

A public surname-by-surname rendering of the original census book **РГАДА, f. 1209,
op. 1, pt. 1, d. 1040** has preserved no fewer than ten entries of the surname series:
the reitar (cavalryman) «Антилогов» (Antilogov) in the small village of Sloboda (fol. 106); three Anpilogovs in Cheremoshnaya —
a reitar on fol. 119v, a reitar and a minor (nedorosl) on fol. 122; a minor in Teterevinka
on fol. 128; four entries in the village of Lyutskoye on fols. 149–150; the widow Anpilogova in
the hamlet of Dolgaya Lopteva on fol. 150. The form «Антилогов» is left
literal for now: without a scan one cannot decide whether this is a new variant or a misreading.

The publication here is an abridged surname digest: the given names,
patronymics and families of most households are omitted. So no profiles are created for the unknown
reitars, the soldier, the minors and the widow. The original book itself
promises a household-by-household record with names and ages; the names should come to light on
target folios **106, 119v, 122, 128, 149–150**.

The main find is not the number of households but the repetition of the microgeography. In 1594/95
Sofon Timofeevich Anfilogov held the hamlet of Sofonova **under the
Cheremoshensky grove**. The very next entries describe Cheremoshna and
lands **on the Lyudtskaya plot** under the same Cheremoshensky forest. In 1710
Anpilogovs again live at the same time in **Cheremoshnaya** and **Lyutskoye**, and then
a widow is recorded in **Dolgaya Lopteva**.

There is a second repetition as well. Immediately before Lyutskoye the census describes
Kurasovka: on fols. 148–148v the Bazdyrevs and Trunovs appear there. In Bazdyrevo
in 1594/95 these same two rare surnames were neighbours of Anfilogovs. Thus,
after 115 years, the cluster **Anpilogovs—Bazdyrevs—Trunovs** recurs, and
at the same time the cluster **Cheremoshnaya—Lyutskoye**. This is the strongest
evidence found so far of a long-lived Oryol–Kromy service-and-land nest.

The limits of the conclusion matter: it has not yet been proven that the administrative move from
Oryol district to Kromy district was a resettlement rather than a change of boundary; no
generational pedigree has been built; the exact modern location of
Lyutskoye has not been established. The intermediate stage requires a look at the survey (mezhevaya) book of 1678–1681:
**РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, pt. 1, d. 1038**. So no connecting route
has been put on the map and no approximate coordinates have been set.

Structured card:
`PUB-RGADA-1209-1-1040-KROMY-ANPILOGOVY-1710`.

**The circulation of pomestye land is already under way**: Osip Vasilyev received what «что было в
поместье за Онохою за Дѣевымъ сыномъ Криволапова» ("had been in pomestye held by Onokha Deev syn Krivolapova").

**Secular, non-calendar names**: Nechay, Ermak, Belyay, Persha, Mikula, Sofon.
The same register as in the earliest record of the family — «Тонкой Онфилогов» (Tonkoy Onfilogov), Uglich,
1536.

**One book, three spellings of the surname**: Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Onfilogov.
One scribe, one district, one year. This is direct proof that in the 16th century
"-f-/-p-" and "A-/O-" still alternated freely, and that arguing about the "correct"
form is pointless.

## Where to look for the origin — in descending order of likelihood

### A new narrow corridor: Tula — Solova — Odoev — Dedilov

After moving from a single surname to a whole circle of neighbours, the first
recurrence of all four distinctive Bazdyrevo surnames at once has been found in an external
archival complex. A public name-by-name rendering of the payment books
**РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 488** covers 2,862 entries for nine towns
in 1614–1620. Then an open set of all 744 digital frames was found, together with a
name index giving patronymics and folios. Checking the index and the folios themselves
corrected the heavily abbreviated web names:

- **Tula**: Ivan Grigoryev syn Krivolapov, Zaupsky stan, fol. 4v; Ivan
  Fyodorov syn Trunov, the same stan, fol. 12; Chyuzhin Andreev syn Nemtsov,
  Zaostrozhsky stan, fol. 19v;
- **Dedilov**: Pyotr Osipov syn Trunov, fol. 59;
- **Krapivna**: Grigory Trunov, his widow Matryona and sons Filipp, Ivan and
  Trofim, fol. 93v; Fedka Ivanov syn Trunov, fol. 99v; Ivan Grigoryev and
  Smirnoy Prokofyev, the Trunov children, fol. 100;
- **Solova**: Semyon Mikulin syn Trunov, Podgorodny stan, fol. 108v; Agey
  Matveev syn Bazdyrev, Zasolovsky stan, fol. 110v;
- **Odoev**: Fyodor Petrov syn Bazdyrev and Ustin Stepanov syn Nemtsov on the same
  fol. 467 of Uderevsky stan; Ustin recurs on fols. 470v, 513v, 557 and
  567v; Ivan Grigoryev syn Bazdyrev, fol. 576.

A significant error in the search database also came to light: it assigned people from
the Krapivna fols. 61–103 to the next heading, **Solova**. The structure of the file itself
sets Krapivna apart as an independent section and begins Solova at fol. 104.
So the former "Solova" Matryona, Filipp, Ivan and Trofim Trunov are in fact
in the Krapivna section. A label from an external index must from now on
always be checked against the folio.

However, a section of the book cannot automatically be turned into a separate town.
The scholarly passport of the historic settlement of Krapivna, prepared by
the historian and archivist D. M. Yatskin, specifically analyses the formula «на Плове,
Солове и на Кропивне» ("on the Plova, the Solova and the Kropivna"). The authors consider mistaken the reading of three parallel
towns: it referred to a single locality with Krapivna as its fortified centre.
The landholders of its neighbourhood were called **solovlyane**, and Solova district later became
Krapivna district. So the file does indeed have two book headings, but in
the historical and geographical sense the Trunovs and Bazdyrev belong to a single
Krapivna–Solova service-and-land milieu.

This is the very same rare set of surnames that surrounds Anfilogovs in
Bazdyrevo in 1594/95: the Trunovs, Nemtsov, the Krivolapovs and Bazdyrev. Now
we can see not only the distribution across the books but also three main local nodes:
Krivolapov and Trunov in one and the same Zaupsky stan of Tula; the Trunov family group
and the Trunov–Bazdyrev pair in the single Krapivna–Solova district; Bazdyrev and
Nemtsov on one Odoev folio. So the general upper-Oka search has been narrowed to
the line **Tula—Krapivna/Solova district—Odoev—Dedilov**. But the direction of movement has not yet
been established: the payment books were compiled 19–26 years after the Oryol book
and after the Time of Troubles. The families could have come from there to Oryol, dispersed from Oryol back again,
or had branches in several towns at once.

The names do not allow free identifications. Ivan Fyodorovich Trunov, as
a later record of 1622 showed, cannot be the husband of the Oryol widow
Marya: he is a different Tula holder of the same lands who was still alive.
Ivan Grigoryevich Krivolapov is not connected with Onokha and Lev Deevich or with
the sons of Ivan Krivolapov; Agey Matveevich, Fyodor Petrovich and Ivan
Grigoryevich Bazdyrev do not match the Oryol Mikifor Oksenovich;
Ustin Stepanovich and Chyuzhin Andreevich Nemtsov do not match Gavrila Fyodorovich.
Ustin's fivefold entry means repeated Odoev land records of
one man, not five Nemtsovs. Anfilogovs themselves are not in the index.

#### The neighbourhood trace could be moved back to 1587–1589

An open scan of the printed edition of an earlier book changes the chronology of this
corridor. The complete 17th-century archival copy is kept in **РГАДА, f. 1209, op.
1, d. 124, fols. 1–174**; a second, incomplete copy is in d. 487, fols. 861–1024.
Both reproduce the cadastral book of Ivan Zherebtsov and his colleagues of
1587/88–1588/89. So it is important not to confuse the date when the
surviving copy was made with the date of the land description it reproduces.

On p. 1128 **Ivan Fyodorov syn Trunov** holds the hamlet of Ulybasheva in Zaupsky
stan together with three daughters of Grigory Trunov — Darya, Ustinya and Olga.
On p. 1223 the same full name appears in Glutensky stan: the Lepekhovsky
pochinok (new clearing) is expressly called his **old pomestye**, and another 36 chetverti are registered to
him in Zaupsky stan. On the following p. 1224 **Mikula Sidorov syn Trunov** also
holds an old pomestye in the hamlet of Efimovskaya. This is no longer a single surname but
several generational lines of the Tula Trunovs before the Oryol book.

The full page 974 of the Oryol edition gave an important correction. The entry reads:
**«За вдовою за Марьею за Ивановою женою Трунова да за ея детьми за Власком,
а он служит, да за Илейкою, а он 6 лет, да за Демкою, а он 4 лет, да за
Дениском, а он 8 лет, отца их поместья в дер. в Баздыреве…»**.

> *Translation:* "Held by the widow Marya, wife of Ivan Trunov, and by her children Vlasko, who serves, Ileyka, who is 6 years old, Demka, who is 4 years old, and Denisko, who is 8 years old: their father's pomestye in the hamlet of Bazdyrevo…"

Consequently,
the dead husband really was called Ivan Trunov, but the children are not nameless:
**Vlas was already serving; Denis was eight, Ilya six, Demyan four**.
The order of the entry is not by age. A photocopy of the page is saved in the card.

For a short time the Tula Ivan Fyodorovich looked like a strong candidate for
the Oryol husband of Marya. A new control record refuted this hypothesis.
In the published **Tula review desyatnya (razbornaya desyatnya) of 1622**, fols. 172–172v,
the same Ivan Fyodorov syn Trunov is named as living, though old and crippled. In his place
his son Vasily was preparing to serve. Ivan was still registered as holding **36
chetverti in the hamlet of Ulyboshevo in Zaupsky stan** and **15 chetverti in
Glutensky stan** — an exact continuation of the two holdings of 1587–1589.
The desyatnya additionally states that he had no pomestya or votchiny in other towns.
The match of the full name, two places and the size of the land reliably links
both Tula records. A man alive in 1622 could not have been the dead father
of the Oryol children in 1594/95: **the Tula and Oryol Ivan Trunovs are different
people**.

The full entry of 1622 has so far been checked against a published quotation from the work of
M. R. Bedelev and I. G. Burtsev, not against the project's own photocopy of the archival
folios. So fols. 172–172v remain an order for palaeographic verification,
but the earlier candidate has been withdrawn. The new search is aimed at the family signature
of the Oryol Ivan himself: **the widow Marya; Vlas Ivanovich already in service; Denis,
Ilya and Demyan Ivanovich should have been approaching enrolment at the beginning of the 17th
century**. Their joint appearance in a desyatnya or review list would be far
stronger than a single surname match.

The new family signature has been checked against the full open table of servicemen
of 421,676 rows. Before 1640 it yielded no exact Vlas, Denis or Demyan Trunov.
The only name hit is **Ilya Trufanov, Ryazhsk, 1619,
РГАДА, f. 210, op. 10, d. 204**; a different surname form, a different town and
the absence of a patronymic do not allow him to be linked with the Oryol Ilya Ivanovich.
The full index to the Oryol review **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 14, d. 502** has now
revealed patronymics and folios. In 1620–1621 there are recorded **Semyon Filatov syn
Trunov** on fol. 132, **Boris Onisimov syn Trunov** on fol. 136 and **Fyodor Semyonov
syn Trunov** twice, on fols. 25 and 54. In the list of Oryol servicemen of 1642
exactly the same three full names recur: Fyodor Semyonovich on fol.
374v, Semyon Filatovich on fol. 380v and Boris Onisimovich on fol. 382v. This is
a strong continuity of one Oryol group over twenty-one years,
although final identification of each person will require comparing the entries
themselves.

For Boris Onisimovich an intermediate link has been found that changes the search
tactics. In the original Oryol list of 1627/28 he is recorded on fol. 139 with
a separate formula: **«Новик 133-го году, поверстан в Розряде за полон 250
чети, денег з городом 8 рублев»** ("Novik of the year 133, enrolled in the Razryad for captivity at 250 cheti, money with the town 8 roubles"). This places the Razryad decision in 1624/25.
At the same time, the original fol. 136 of the book of 1620/21 already contains
an independent service entry for the same Boris Onisimov syn Trunov.
Consequently, "novik of the year 133" cannot be understood as the first appearance
of the man in Oryol: it concerned a new enrolment or a change of the service
oklad.

The main new search address is the Razryad decision of 1624/25 itself, **«за
полон»** ("for captivity"). Such papers could report the circumstances of capture and return,
the former regiment or town of service. This is not yet the origin of Anpilogovs, but it is
a concrete mechanism of post-Troubles replenishment of their closest Oryol milieu.
The scholarly index confirms the three full names and the folios of 1627/28;
the full publication preserves Boris's formula. The 1620/21 original and a separate
fragment of the line are saved in the project. [Publication of the list
of 1627/28](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/5703/135781/p4800932.htm), [scholarly
name index](https://oldbasman.ru/f/almanax-13_boyare_spiski_imena.pdf).

For Marya's family the result is negative, but decisive: none of these people
is Vlas, Denis, Ilya or Demyan **Ivanovich**. File 502
no longer needs to be examined as a possible hidden list of the four brothers.
The search moves to the lists of the Time of Troubles, early enrolments and army-wide
rosters, where the serving Vlas could have been recorded without a surname or with a different spelling.
[Full index to the file of 1620–1621](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/5703/135781/p5730808.htm#pp5730808),
[full index to the Oryol list of 1642](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/5703/135781/p5388544.htm#pp5388544).

There is also a geographical trace of the third surname: immediately after Ivan
Trunov's Zaupsky entry, the **Krivolapov pochinok** and the **Krivolapova clearing (polyana)** are described. No living
Krivolapov is named as a holder here, so the place names cannot be turned into
proven kinship. But the sequence shows that the Trunov and
Krivolapov names were already localised in one part of Zaupsky
stan.

On p. 1271 **Olfim Bazdyrev** is recorded among the Cossacks of the Lutyuva sotnya (company)
of Dedilov district. He and four comrades were given five chetverti of land each along
the upper reaches of the Shivoron under the Cheremkhov moss, explicitly **«за послугу»** ("for service rendered"). So
the Bazdyrevs were present not only in the Ryazan–Ryazhsk and Oryol landholding
milieu but also in the neighbouring Dedilov Cossack stratum.

The full folio-by-folio text gave a narrower candidate for one particular
Oryol branch. **Ivan Nechaev** is recorded in Dedilov twice. On fol. 15v
he had a storehouse (klet) inside the fortress among the storehouses of the Cossacks of the Lugovaya and Dergilevskaya
sotni. On fol. 36v he is called **the desyatsky (squad leader) Ivanko Nechaev** with a separate
homestead in the Dergilevskaya sloboda; on his land lived **a newly recruited Cossack, his
brother Ponkratko**. The double registration shows a settled service householder,
not a chance coincidence of two names. Six or seven years later, in the new Oryol
exemption distribution, Ivan Nechaev syn Anpilogov is recorded first.

A coincidence is still not an identity. The Dedilov scribe gave Ivan no
family name Anpilogov; in another upper-Oka source an Ivan
Nechaev syn Zaytsov has been found. However, the test signature now consists not of the pair
Ivan + Nechay but of five features: generation, Cossack service, the rank of desyatsky,
a storehouse in town and a homestead with his brother Ponkratko. The archival target has also been refined:
what is needed are photocopies of **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 124, fols. 15v and 36v** and
outgoing Dedilov separation or release extracts (otdelnye or otkaznye vypisi) after 1589.

#### An internal check downgraded the Dedilov candidate

All eighteen recipients of the twelve Korchakov zaimishcha with an exemption from
20 January 1595 have been compared with the ordinary entries of the same Oryol
cadastral book. The result changes the meaning of the section: **all seventeen family
names of the group are already represented in Oryol district outside the exemption distribution**.
This is not a list of one arriving party.

At least five exemption holders are independently named as the same people in the local milieu:

- Miney Vasilyevich Dolzhonkov already had a landholder's homestead and land in Lednya
  (printed p. 999);
- Filipp Semyonovich Sizov lived on the land of his brother Sava (p. 943);
- Boris Fyodorovich Sidorov lived on the land of his brother Matvey (p. 989);
- Ivan Martynovich Kovyrshin lived on the land of his uncle Ivan Chelishchevich;
- Ivan Vasilyevich Shchelkunov is expressly called the uncle of the holders of a paternal
  pomestye in Vyshnie Mokrishchi (p. 961).

Several more formulas give a local family one generation deeper. Pyotr
Fyodorovich Semenikhin has probable brothers Semyon and Ostanya Fyodorovich;
Kirey, the father of Dokuchay Kireevich Shchenikhin, already lives on the land of his brother Overky;
Lomaka Ivanovich Rezantsov has a probable brother Maksim Ivanovich; Pavel, the father of
Mikula Pavlovich Sotnikov, is an independent Oryol landholder, and Sozon, the father of
Okinsha Sozonovich, lives with his nephew Khariton Sotnikov. Afanasy
Leontyevich Tenetilov was earlier named as a bordering holder, and next to him
Aleksey Leontyevich Tenetilov is known. Where the source does not say the word "brother",
kinship remains probable but is not declared proven.

Consequently, the exemption section records the expansion of an already established
Oryol corporation: independent holders, residents on relatives' land and
junior members of local families received new plots. Ivan Nechaevich may have been
a newcomer, but his new grant itself no longer supports that reading. It is no less
plausible that he was a junior member of an already existing
Oryol cluster of Anfilogovs.

The Dedilov Ivan Nechaev remains a namesake worth targeting, but the hypothesis of a move
**Dedilov → Oryol has been downgraded**. Only an act of 1589–1595
that names the surname Anpilogov, the brother Pankrat, the former
Dedilov homestead or a direct transfer of service can raise it again. For the list of 1577 priority
returns to the elder generation: Mikula, the two Vasilys, Persha, Timofey,
Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty. Profiles and the route do not change as a result of this reassessment.

#### A new turn: a possible father of two Oryol Rezantsovs found in Dedilov

Single coincidences turned out to be too noisy, so the full Oryol
section of 1594/95 was compared not only with Ivan Nechaev but with the whole
neighbourhood membership of the Dergilevskaya sotnya. This technique gave the first two-family
signature.

On Dedilov fol. 33v, among the Cossack households of the Dergilevskaya sotnya, is recorded
**Ivanko Rezantsev**. Six or seven years later there are two
Rezantsovs with a father named Ivan in Oryol at once:

- **Maksim Ivanov syn Rezantsov** already had a landholder's homestead, one inhabited and
  one empty peasant household in the hamlet of Oblomnoye Boloto on the Orel by
  the Ponikov kolodez (printed p. 942);
- **Lomaka Ivanov syn Rezantsov** is named twice on pp. 1011–1012 in the very
  exemption section where Ivan Nechaev syn Anpilogova stands first.

A direct check of the full scan corrected, along the way, Smirnov's old reference:
Maksim is printed on **p. 942**, not on p. 1055; p. 1055 has a different
person, Melekh Fyodorov syn Rezantsov.

Maksim and Lomaka are probable brothers, since their patronymic and rare
family name coincide. The Dedilov Ivanko fits as their father by name,
generation and surname. Next to him in the same sotnya was the desyatsky Ivanko
Nechaev with his brother Pankratko. This yields two parallel possible chains:

`Иван Резанцев (Дедилов) → Максим и Ломака Ивановичи (Орёл)`

`Иван Нечаев (Дедилов) → Иван Нечаевич Анпилогов (Орёл)`

(Ivan Rezantsev (Dedilov) → Maksim and Lomaka Ivanovich (Oryol); Ivan Nechaev (Dedilov) → Ivan Nechaevich Anpilogov (Oryol).)

This is not a proven transfer: the Oryol book does not call Maksim and Lomaka
brothers, the Dedilov one does not call Ivanko their father, and Ivan Nechaev is not called
Anpilogov. But two consistent family imprints in one service
milieu are considerably stronger than the coincidence of a single name. They suggest
a new search model: not to wait for a list of a one-off party of 1595 but
to look for an earlier **stage-by-stage resettlement of a family**, in which the father stayed in
the old town, one son already had an Oryol homestead and another received new
land. This route has not yet been put on the map.

The book itself helps narrow the date of a possible departure. For other households the scribe
noted outright: **«Гаврила сшол в Епифань»** ("Gavrila has left for Epifan") and **«Митка сшол за Шат»** ("Mitka has left beyond the Shat"). Against
Ivan Nechaev there is no such note, so in 1588/89 he was still considered
a Dedilov serviceman. If this is the future Oryol Ivan, the move
must have taken place between this book and the issue of the Oryol exemption of 20 January
1595. The difference of rank does not prevent a move: in neighbouring Epifan in 1585
300 Cossacks were enrolled as deti boyarskie by a single act. This proves
that the mechanism was possible, but it does not replace a personal act of Ivan's enrolment.

A later check did not refute the candidate. The open name-by-name
**muster roll (smotrenny spisok) of the vanguard regiment at Dedilov of 1629** has been checked in full: РГАДА, f. 210,
op. 9, stolbets 57, fols. 27–31 and the subsequent published parts
([open rendering](https://document.wikireading.ru/hTYw7C4SPa)). Neither Ivan
Nechaev nor Ponkrat/Pankrat is in it. Then a fuller
rendering of the **cadastral book of Dedilov district of 1627–1629** was checked in full: РГАДА,
f. 1209, op. 1, d. 491
([text of the publication](https://www.universalinternetlibrary.ru/book/96847/chitat_knigu.shtml)).
The brothers are not found in it either, but there are two other sons of Nechay with other surnames:
**Zloba Nechaev syn Baranov** on fol. 728v and **Poznyak Nechaev syn Kolpakov**
on fol. 743v. So the patronymic Nechaev is not unique in the local milieu.
The estimates (smety) of
1627, 1663 and 1665 have also been examined, but they are mainly numerical and
do not give a comparable full list of names. The disappearance of precisely these brothers by 1629
is compatible with departure from Dedilov, but does not prove resettlement to Oryol:
a forty-year gap allows for death, a change of service and a gap in the records.
The practical result: what must now be looked for is a transitional separation, release or
enrolment record **between 1589 and 20 January 1595**.

The folio-by-folio rendering also revealed a second namesake worth targeting who was absent from
the abridged printed volume. On **fol. 25**, among the households of the Lugovaya Cossack sotnya,
is written: **«д. сторожевой казак Шерапко Сысоев да на ево земле, д. бобыл
Осипко Васильев»** ("household of the watch Cossack Sherapko Sysoev, and on his land the household of the landless cottager [bobyl] Osipko Vasilyev"). Six or seven years later in Oryol's Bazdyrevo stands
**Osip Vasilyev syn Anfilogov**. Given name, patronymic, generation and
the upper-Oka frontier milieu coincide; Dedilov has for the first time given a pre-Oryol candidate
for the line Vasily → Osip as well.

But this find is weaker than Ivanko Nechaev. Osip and Vasily are common
names; the Dedilov man is not called Anfilogov, he is registered as a bobyl on the land of
a watch Cossack, and there is no note of his departure. The Oryol Osip was already a syn
boyarsky and received the former pomestye of Onokha Krivolapov. An administrative
move between social strata is possible, but no document attests it for Osip specifically.
There is also a direct control of non-uniqueness: on 23 September 1615 another or
unidentifiable **Osipko Vasilyev** is recorded among Cossacks sent from
Moscow prisons to Kasimov (РГАДА, f. 210, Stolbtsy of the Bezglasny Desk,
No. 11, fols. 24–26). His former town and surname are not named, so this line
can be attached neither to Dedilov nor to Oryol; it shows how easy it is
to obtain a false identity from one common pair.

What is needed is a photocopy of **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 124, fol. 25**, then the later
Dedilov books and a separation or release charter for the Oryol pomestye.
For now no profile, identity or route is created.

#### An alternative control: the large Novgorod corpus did not repeat Dedilov

So as not to take the chosen direction for the only possible one, a
geographically distant and much larger corpus of the same time has been checked:
the name-by-name rendering of the inspection (dozornaya) book of empty lands of the Derevskaya pyatina
of 1593–1595, **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 974, fols. 1–158v**. The open
index has about fifteen hundred lines. The academic description dates the book
to 1594–1596, that is, it is almost synchronous with the Oryol distribution.

The whole set of seventeen family names of the Oryol exemption holders was checked
not for a single lucky match but in full. The only exact substantive
matches turned out to be **Shalimov** and **Rezantsov**; there is one ordinary
Isakov and **Yarugin**, close to but not identical with the Oryol Yarygin. Not
found: Anpilogovs/Anfilogovs, Semenikhins, Vanins, Dolzhonkins, Shchenikhins,
Sizovs, Gnezdilovs, Sidorovs, Sotnikovs, Ovdokimovs, Kovyrshins, Shchelkunovs,
Tenetilovs, nor the exact form Yarygin.

It is especially important not to make a false match with the Mikulins. In Novgorod there are
people with the family name Mikulin, but the Oryol Vasily, Timofey and Karp
are called **Mikulin's children Anfilogovs** (Mikuliny deti Anfilogovy): Mikula is their father's name, and
Anfilogov is the surname under study. So the Novgorod Mikulins cannot be
counted towards the family imprint.

The control does not prove Dedilov, but it changes the relative weight of the directions. In
the huge Novgorod list the Oryol bundle broke up into two scattered
surnames; in the small Dergilevskaya sotnya five stems occur together
that are absent even from the full Epifan list: **Nechaev, Rezantsov, Suvorov,
Polyanskoy and Bredikhin**. So the Dedilov concentration looks less like ordinary
surname noise and remains the priority address for an act of 1589–1595.
No Novgorod route or profiles are created.

[Name-by-name rendering of the Derevskaya pyatina book](https://dompredkov.ru/novgorodskie-pomeshhiki-derevskoj-pyatiny-1593-1595-gg/).
[Academic description of the source](https://onomastics.ru/content/2015-%E2%84%961-18-4).

The corridor now consists of two chronological layers: in 1587–1589 the Trunovs,
Bazdyrev and Krivolapov place names are already visible in it; in 1614–1620
all four surnames of the Bazdyrevo neighbourhood cluster recur. The
Anfilogovs themselves, and the personal forms Anfilog/Anfilof, are absent from the whole recognised volume.
So the tactics change from searching for one surname to reconstructing transfers of
old pomestya, Cossack sotni and family formulas. A direct route Tula or
Dedilov — Oryol is still not drawn.

Card: `PUB-PKMG-1587-TULA-DEDILOV-TRUNOV-BAZDYREV`.
[Open scan of the edition](https://disk.yandex.ru/d/d1HmQq6eUb9pL).
[RSL catalogue](https://search.rsl.ru/ru/record/01003509062).

The next step has become even more precise: to read the faded names of the
pomestye settlements with confidence, then to look in pre-Oryol extracts for the fathers already named:
Grigory Krivolapov, Fyodor Trunov, Andrey Nemtsov, Mikula Trunov,
Matvey and Pyotr Bazdyrev. The map does not change until there is a direct formula of former
service or proven kinship. Card:
`PUB-RGADA-1209-1-488-UPPER-OKA-OREL-NEIGHBOURS`.
[Public list for the file](https://dompredkov.ru/sluzhilye-lyudi-iz-platezhnyh-knig-raznyh-gorodov-1614-1620gg/).
[Open set of scans and index](https://disk.yandex.ru/d/beTOHQ7SI7x1RQ).
[Scholarly passport of the historic settlement of Krapivna, 2008](https://www.krapivna.org/files/files/Biblioteka/e9c4c82103a2ec078ce671d945e0678f.pdf).

### 1. The northern and upper-Oka corridor

According to the published reconstruction, the main common flow went through Serpukhov,
Kaluga, Belyov, Novosil and Krapivna; Mtsensk, Bolkhov,
Karachev and the lands of the future Livny district also took part, and a separate branch came from Pronsk.
For Anfilogovs none of these towns of origin has yet been proven. What has to be checked
is not one surname but the rare name Anpilog/Anfilog and the whole circle of neighbours from
Korchakov stan.

- **«Писцовые книги Московского государства» [Cadastral Books of the Muscovite State], vol. I, sect. 2** — **the same volume**
  from which the Oryol book is taken. It also contains localities of the Tula, Kaluga,
  Yaroslavl and Tver provinces. That is, the neighbouring districts should be searched in the same
  edition. **Done on 21 August 2026 — the result is negative**; see the section
  "A full search of the whole volume" at the beginning of the note. The neighbouring southern districts in
  this volume (Tula, Dedilov, Kashira, Venev, Epifan) do not know Anfilogovs.
  The full books of Karachev, Bolkhov, Mtsensk, Novosil, Chernigov and Bryansk are not
  in this volume. However, the early Mtsensk, Bolkhov and Karachev books
  have now been partly checked through surviving later extracts; the result
  is set out below.
- **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 4** is not, as was previously supposed, a ready-made
  collection of Oryol desyatni of the 1570s–1590s. The surviving inventory
  mostly begins with files of 1638/39. It is needed for descendants and for
  reconstructing the 17th-century service stratum, but it does not replace the search for early
  Razryad documents in f. 181 and in published books.
- Priority town complexes: Belyov, Novosil, Krapivna, Serpukhov,
  Kaluga, Pronsk, Mtsensk, Bolkhov and Karachev. Search terms: Anpilog, Anfilog,
  Onfilog, Amfilokhy, Onanin, and also the Trunovs, Nemtsovs, Krivolapovs,
  Bazdyrevs and Grevtsovs as a possibly migrating circle.

#### What survives of the early books of Mtsensk and Bolkhov

The official catalogue of РГАДА cadastral books and four volumes of «Акты служилых
землевладельцев» [Acts of Service Landholders] make it possible to replace the general "Mtsensk needs checking" with a precise
archival programme.

In **1573/74** Mtsensk district was inspected by Ushak Petrov and the clerk Semeyka
Pankratyev. No separate book is named in the catalogue, but its existence
is attested by three later extracts. The most important is a charter of 9 February 1576.
It cites the sovereign's principle: deti boyarskie were ordered
«испомещивать в одних городех, кто откуда служит» ("to be given pomestya in the same towns from which each serves"). Fyodor Nechaev syn Oshikhmin
had land in Mtsensk district, but he was not listed in the original Mtsensk service list:
he was registered under Chern and served from Chern. So his Mtsensk
pomestye was transferred to Stepan Kuzmin syn Merkulov.

This is the first document found that shows the mechanics literally:
**land, place of residence and service town were not one and the same**.
The Razryad Prikaz checked a man against the service list and could redistribute
his former pomestye. Consequently, a surname match in two districts does not yet
mean resettlement; what is needed is a formula «служит с такого-то города» ("serves from such-and-such a town"), a record
of re-registration or a network of sureties.

For **1584/85** two exact extracts survive from the Mtsensk books of Grigory
Koledinsky and Pyatoy Saburov: for Artemy Grigoryev syn Koptev in
the Bolshoy Gorodskoy stan, and for Vasily and Pakhom, sons of Filipp Stepanov
Saklakov, in Chern stan. Later acts show that books of the same
Grigory Koledinsky were also used for Bolkhov. These extracts do not replace complete town lists,
but they give the scribes' names, the year and specific stolbtsy where
other fragments may lie.

A full search of the complete text of volumes II–IV of «Акты служилых
землевладельцев» found no Anfilogovs, Anpilogovs, Onfilogovs,
Ampilogovs or Onanyins. Nor was the distinctive Bazdyrevo circle found —
the Bazdyrevs, Krivolapovs and Grevtsovs; the Trunov in the corpus turned out to be a Moscow
clerk named Pyotr, and a separate Ivan Kirillov syn Nemtsov is not yet connected with
the Oryol Gavrila Fyodorovich Nemtsov.

The frequently cited **list of elected dvoryane of 1577** has also been checked. It is
a Moscow list of the elite and court ranks, not a general register of the deti boyarskie of
Oryol. It has some service notes «в Новосили» ("at Novosil") and «в Карачев» ("to Karachev"), but
the family under study and its neighbourhood cluster are not there. So this list cannot
be turned into a roster of the first settlers of Oryol, nor into proof of a move
Kashira—Mtsensk.

Structured audit card:
`PUB-ASZ-1573-1585-UPPER-OKA-SERVICE-BOOKS`. Sources:
[АСЗ, vol. III](https://djvu.online/file/XShJZdkt8XXun) and
[catalogue of РГАДА cadastral books](http://rgada.info/materiali/baranov_catalog.pdf).

#### The first named bridge: the Koptevs between Tula, Mtsensk and Oryol

Comparing the books gave the first example in which the general upper-Oka flow
is visible not only through the organisation of service but also through a recurring family
formula.

- In the watch-post roster (rospis storozh) of 1577 **Timofey Grigoryev syn Koptev** is named
  head of the Tula watch post. The Tula cadastral book of 1587–1589
  shows the same Timofey as holder of old pomestya in Zaupsky and
  Rastovetsky stans. Half of the village of Pyatnitskoye came to him from his expressly
  named brother **Bulgak Koptev**. So Timofey was not by chance
  at Tula in 1577: his service continued with a stable
  attachment to land.
- In the Mtsensk books of 1584/85 and an act of 1587 is recorded **Artemy
  Grigoryev syn Koptev**, landholder of the hamlet of Prokina on the Sukhaya Mtsena.
- In Oryol district in 1594/95 **Ivan Grigoryev syn Koptev** held
  a share of the hamlet of Golodnye Sychi under the Korchakov forest. He was not alone:
  in the same hamlet lived Epish Fateev syn Koptev, his expressly named brother
  Afonka, and Ivan Bulgakov syn Koptev; a separate cluster of Koptevs was in
  Brusen.
- The desyatnya of 1596 adds young servicemen of the next generation:
  **Vasily Ivanov syn Koptev** for Tula and **Foma Vasilyev syn
  Koptev** for Oryol, both with an oklad of 250 chetverti. Foma with the same full
  name is already recorded as a landholder of Brusen in 1594/95: this is almost certainly
  one person, first visible in the cadastral book and then officially
  enrolled. In the Tula book of 1587–1589, next to Timofey, is named
  **Ivan Stepanov syn Koptev**, co-owner of the village of Pyatnitskoye; he fits as
  father of the Tula Vasily Ivanovich, but the document does not say so directly.

Three men in a row — Timofey, Artemy and Ivan — have the same formula
**«Григорьев сын Коптев»** ("Grigoryev syn Koptev") in Tula, Mtsensk and Oryol. They could have been sons
