of one Grigory Koptev and represent branches of the family dispersed across towns.
This is a strong working hypothesis, but not yet a proven fact: no document found
calls them brothers. Only the brotherhood of Timofey and
Bulgak, and of the Oryol Epish and Afonka, is directly proven.

The find matters as **a model of the origin of the Oryol core**: by the 1590s
a group of one service surname is already visible in Oryol, while the elder bearer
of the same patronymic serves and holds land steadily in Tula, and another
appears in intermediate Mtsensk. This is much more concrete than the general story
of resettlement "from the upper towns" (verkhovskie goroda), but it is not yet a route of Anfilogovs.
The evidential link needed is a record of the children of Grigory Koptev, a surety
record or a transfer of pomestye between the Tula, Mtsensk and Oryol branches.
The pair Foma 1594/95—1596 does not show such a move, but it does prove that
the enrolment of a novik could formalise a man already inside the
Oryol landholding community.

Structured card: `PUB-KOPTEV-TULA-MTSENSK-OREL-1577-1596`.
Sources: [watch-post roster of 15 March 1576, misprinted under 1577](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/1-20/18.php),
[cadastral books](https://www.prlib.ru/item/355286),
[АСЗ, vol. III](https://djvu.online/file/XShJZdkt8XXun) and
[desyatnya of 1596](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1580-1600/Desjatnja_novikov/text.php).

#### The Koptevs are no exception: four family bridges

After the Koptev find, all the holding headings of the Oryol book were
compared with the books of Tula, Dedilov, Kashira, Venev and Epifan. The sample
included 1,089 Oryol, 321 Tula, 71 Dedilov, 386 Kashira, 57
Venev and 10 Epifan formulas of "given name + patronymic + family name".
No exact full name of one person on both sides has been found yet, but
three more strong family parallels emerged.

- **The Elagins.** In Tula district in 1587–1589 Menshoy Grigoryev syn
  Elagin held an old pomestye in Glutensky stan. In Oryol in 1594/95
  Stepan Grigoryev syn Elagin and his expressly named full brothers Nikon and
  Fedka received their father's pomestye in Ostrovnya. The surname and the father
  Grigory coincide; Menshoy may have been another brother, but he is not expressly called a brother.
- **The Glebovs.** Semyon Ivanov syn Glebov held an old Tula pomestye and
  another 28 chetverti in Aleksin. Ivan Ivanov syn Glebov is recorded as an Oryol
  landholder at the Lyutskoye gorodishche (hillfort) on the Otsna. In 1596, among the Tula noviki,
  Fyodor Ivanov syn Glebov appears. This looks like several branches of the children
  of one Ivan Glebov, but there is no direct kinship record yet.
- **The Bolotovs.** Vasily Ivanov syn Bolotov had the Kashira waste (pustosh) of Bolotovo
  in 1578/79, Mitya Ivanov syn Bolotov had land in Tula district in
  1587–1589, and Timofey Ivanov syn Bolotov had an Oryol share in
  the hamlet of Bolotovo in 1594/95. The three-town sequence is strong,
  but special caution is needed here: the byname could have formed independently
  from different settlements called Bolotovo.

Thus the Koptev formula recurred in **four family groups**.
This is not yet a list of settlers, but it is already a systematic feature: Oryol received not
random individuals but, probably, separate branches of families whose other branches
remained in the old upper-Oka towns.

#### What a real intake list looked like: Venev, 1571/72

The same publication preserves an almost perfect specimen of the document that
needs to be found for Oryol. When dividing up Venev district, the scribes wrote outright
that they were allotting pomestya **«новым помещикам Коширянам и Тулянам»** ("to the new landholders, men of Kashira and men of Tula"). Then
the mechanism is repeated in more detail: deti boyarskie from the court towns (dvorovye goroda) were given half
of their oklad in land already ploughed, and the other half from wild field and
oak woods. The first formula stands as early as **fol. 3v** of the manuscript, right after
the heading and the sovereign's instruction, so it states the purpose of the whole campaign, not
a later guess based on individual surnames.

This is not an analogy from a later century but a document of **1571/72**, only
five years after the founding of Oryol. So when the new fortress was set up
the same set of papers could have existed:

1. the sovereign's instruction (nakaz) to the scribes;
2. a list of new landholders indicating their towns of origin;
3. a separation book (otdelnaya kniga) with the size of each oklad and the specific land;
4. later records of missing land, surrender and transfer of pomestya.

It is precisely such an Oryol instruction or separation book, not an ordinary late
cadastral book, that can answer directly where the core came from. The Venev
example proves the administrative form itself, but it does not allow us automatically
to call the Oryol men people of Kashira or Tula.

This specimen now has an established modern address. A complete 16th-century copy
survives in **РГАДА: f. 137, op. 1, Venev, d. 1, pt. 1, fols. 1–200**;
the official inventory notes a microfilm. The Epifan continuation is in
the same file, pt. 2, fols. 201–247. So the next stage can be carried out on
the manuscript: check the margins, the clerks' signatures across the joins (skrepy) and the original headings, which may
not have made it into the 1877 edition, and find out whether the town of origin is written next to
particular holders.

Most importantly, the method has been tested on a specific person. **Aleksey
Fyodorov syn Khotyaintsov** is recorded among the new Venev landholders of 1571/72:
in Yasenetsky stan he received a share of the village of Tetyakovo, ploughland and wild field with
a four-year exemption. The Kashira book of 1578/79 reports that a share of
the waste of Surovshchina had previously been held by the same **Aleksey Fyodorov syn
Khotyaintsov**, then «после Алексея лежал в порозжих землях» ("after Aleksey lay among the vacant lands") and was given to
Vasily Mikulin.

The Kashira text does not utter the ready-made phrase "Aleksey was transferred to Venev",
but the rare full name, the direction named in advance by the scribes, and the
chronology all coincide. Here we have the first reconstructed personal trace:
**old Kashira pomestye → new Venev grant → the former land
lies vacant and is passed to another**. It is exactly this kind of pair of documents that now
has to be sought for every candidate among the first settlers of Oryol.

Structured card:
`PUB-PKMG-UPPER-OKA-OREL-FAMILY-BRIDGES-1571-1596`. Main publication:
[«Писцовые книги Московского государства» (Cadastral Books of the Muscovite State)](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia),
[official inventory of fond 137](http://rgada.info/opisi/137-opis_1m.pdf).

### 2. The desyatnya of noviki of 1596

Already known: f. 181, op. 2, d. 120, fol. 58v, Pavel Terentyev syn
Anfilogov, oklad 250 chetverti. The full publication changes the meaning of the find.
The sovereign ordered the young "non-serving landless" sons,
brothers and nephews of the deti boyarskie of each town to be recorded, with
local servicemen being questioned about them. Pavel stands in the Oryol group and so shows
that by 1596 Terenty's family already belonged to the Oryol service milieu.

However, the surviving manuscript is a late copy of the early 18th century made from a lost
original. N. P. Likhachev noted that it had been checked against the original, which still existed then,
with slips corrected and omitted words inserted. The introductory formula
speaks of questioning the town deti boyarskie, but **does not prove** that separate written
family protocols existed for the desyatnya or that the copyist
omitted them. The neighbouring lines give only other Oryol noviki with the same oklad of
250 chetverti and do not name Pavel's former town. The next target is town
review desyatni and surety and pomestye files, not a supposed omitted
part of this list. Publication: [«Десятня новиков 1596 года» (Desyatnya of Noviki of 1596)](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1580-1600/Desjatnja_novikov/text.php).

The reading has now also been checked against the digital image of the file itself. In
[the official РГАДА viewer](http://rgada.info/kueh/index2.php?str=181_2_120)
the spread `0060.jpg` shows fols. 58v–59; on the left-hand page one reads plainly
**«Павел Терентьев сын Анфилогов»** ("Pavel Terentyev syn Anfilogov"). The archival card clarifies the status of
the manuscript: it is a late copy of the late 17th or early 18th century on 88 folios;
the original is lost. N. P. Likhachev's publication appeared in 1909; the Oryol
section occupies pp. 169–173.

On the last folio of the Oryol heading another, previously
unused name has survived: **«А у верстанья у роспросу был Тарас [Онсин?] сын
Мартемьянов»** ("And present at the enrolment, at the questioning, was Taras [Onsin?] syn Martemyanov"). Likhachev hesitantly printed the patronymic as «Аисин (?)», but
the cadastral book of 1594/95 independently resolves the discrepancy. In Kamensk
stan are recorded **Taras and Larya, Onsin's children Martemyanova**; both had
separate landholders' homesteads in the shared hamlet of Martemyanova under the Dolim forest,
at the head of the Domoy. The rare name Taras, the town, the date and the third name coincide.
So Pavel Terentyevich and the whole Oryol group were vouched for by an adult
local landholder, documented in the district two years earlier.

This does not reveal the homeland of Anfilogovs and does not make the Martemyanovs relatives.
But a specific person has now been identified who was present at the questioning
and could have known whose son, brother or nephew each novik was. The desyatnya does not
say whether his testimony was recorded separately. The targeted search narrows to
town review books and surety and pomestye files in which Taras Onsin syn
Martemyanov appears as a witness or an okladchik (assessor). Scan: РГАДА, f. 181, op. 2,
d. 120, fol. 62, spread `0063.jpg`;
cadastral parallel: «Писцовые книги Московского государства» [Cadastral Books of the Muscovite State], Oryol
district, pp. 995–996, original folio 516.

**Names and meaning.** Pavel is the given name. "Terentyev syn" is a patronymic that
directly names the father, Terenty; Anfilogov is a stable family name.
The modern short form would be "Pavel Terentyevich Anfilogov", but the source
does not use patronymics in *-ich*. The word **novik** here means a young
man officially enrolled with a pomestye for the first time and included in
the sovereign's service. It does not mean "a man who has recently come to Oryol".
The oklad of **250 chetverti** is a reckoned norm of land provision, not the area
that Pavel was already ploughing as a single plot.

The Oryol list has been counted and compared in full. It has 144 name
lines; three lines name two brothers each, so there are 147 mentions.
Mikhaila Ignatyev syn Kaznacheev is mechanically repeated twice, so there are **146** different
noviki. All of them are placed in one oklad group of 250 chetverti.

The main new observation comes from comparison with the cadastral book of 1594/95. Not
only Foma Vasilyev syn Koptev, but **at least 32 noviki** are already named there by
the same combination of given name, patronymic and family name. Among them:
Grigory Tretyakov syn Yakunin, Dmitry Danilov syn Pankov, Lukyan Larin syn
Kamenev, Ivan Grigoryev syn Durnev, Ivan Domashnev syn Durakov, Prokuda
Vasilyev syn Penkov, Evdokim Matveev syn Kamenev, Foma Vasilyev syn Koptev,
Ivan Vasilyev syn Shchetinin, three Kaznacheevs — Mikhaila Ignatyevich, Yakov
Nikitich and Ivan Ivanovich, Ignaty Savin syn Esipov, Osip Ermolov syn Rodtsov,
Ivan Nikonov syn Pakhomov, Ignaty Titov syn Isakov, Klementey Antropov syn
Vorobyev, Volodimer Timofeev syn Savenok, Mina Grigoryev syn Ofremov, Zhdan
Ekimov syn Kornilov, Foma Khudyakov syn Demin, Selivan Vasilyev syn Savin,
Khariton Stepanov syn Savenok, Vasily Fyodorov syn Yudin, Grigory Vasilyev
syn Shevlyakov, Semyon Ivanov syn Rozinkov, Ovdey Aksenov syn Yushkov and Pyotr
Prokofyev syn Olekhin. Both books also name the brothers Bogdan and Grigory,
Istoma's children Zolotorev, and Saveley and Vasily, Trofim's children
Semenikhin.

This changes the working model. Many of these people in 1594/95 are expressly
marked «в службу поспел» ("has come of age for service") or already «служит» ("serves"), and a year later they appeared in the enrolment
desyatnya. Consequently, the Oryol section of 1596 largely
records **the official registration of the young generation of families already settled in
the district**, not a new wave of incomers. For the search for towns of origin, what matters more is
the preceding generation — the fathers, widows and family pomestya in the book of 1594/95 —
and independent review, surety and land documents. The structured card has been supplemented:
`RGADA-181-2-120-L58OB-PAVEL`.

The full list has now been checked across all **72 town and territorial
sections**. The official РГАДА catalogue counts the same material as 67 district
service corporations: these are different ways of grouping, not two lists.
There is only one Anfilogov in it — Pavel, and he stands precisely in Oryol; in
Belyov, Bolkhov, Mtsensk, Novosil, Chern and Karachev the surname is absent. The
Oryol group of noviki itself also contains no Onanyins, Trunovs, Nemtsovs,
Krivolapovs, Bazdyrevs or Grevtsovs. This does not prove that there were no such adult
servicemen in Oryol: the desyatnya lists only noviki. But it
shows that looking for Pavel's origin from the neighbouring lines is pointless.
The key layer must be sought in documents of the elder generation, not in
a hypothetical lost appendix to this desyatnya.

#### The noviki continue in documents of the Time of Troubles, 1605–1618

A new check was made against Aleksandr Shatokhin's open index
«Служилые люди Смуты 1605–1618 гг.» [Servicemen of the Time of Troubles, 1605–1618]. The author reports **2,730 entries from
various files of the Razryad Prikaz in РГАДА**. This is a heterogeneous corpus: most often
it records those taken captive and the circumstances of capture, but it also includes
landholders whose peasants were taken captive, and servicemen who received
additional cash or land grants. The public table retains only
surname, given name and town; the archival shelfmark, full text and image of each
entry are not disclosed.

For the town of Oryol **23 names** were found. Two of them give a strong continuation
of the Oryol desyatnya of noviki of 1596:

- **Dmitry Golyanev(?)** very probably matches **Dmitrey Kuzmin
  syn Golenev** of 1596: the rare surname differs by one letter, and
  the late index itself marks the reading with a question mark;
- **Bogdan Zolotarev** very probably matches **Bogdan Istomin
  syn Zolotorev**, recorded in 1596 next to his brother Grigory.

These are not yet final identifications: the Troubles table has no patronymics.
However, the combinations of given name, surname and town are rare enough for
both entries to be treated as priorities for obtaining full extracts. Three more lines —
Mikita Esipov, Andrey Nevbytov/Nevbutov and Aleksey Toktomyshev — continue
the surname series of the Oryol noviki of 1596, but do not match them by
given name and therefore prove only the stability of the family milieu.

Especially important are two technically paired entries in the database
[sluzhilye.ru](https://sluzhilye.ru/):

- **Semyon Lipovsky** stands under Yelets and Oryol in adjacent rows
  191117–191118;
- **Yakov Tikhonov** stands under Oryol and Dorogobuzh in adjacent rows
  354772–354773.

The identical name and consecutive internal numbers make it probable
that each pair arose from one source document that was given two
town labels. But this is **not yet a resettlement route**: an entry may
describe captivity, service, a petition, a land grant, or merely mention
people of two towns. Semyon and Yakov are not among the exact names of the Oryol
landholders of 1594/95 or the noviki of 1596; if the full texts really
show their move to Oryol, this will become direct evidence of the continued
replenishment of the corporation after the first landholding core.

So the interim conclusion has become more precise: Oryol was not a closed group
recruited once and for all by 1566 or 1594. Young men enrolled in
1596 continued to serve during the Troubles, and alongside them appear candidates for
subsequent inter-town replenishment. To prove the direction, what is now needed
is not a new surname search but four targeted full extracts —
Golenev, Zolotorev, Lipovsky and Tikhonov. Structured card:
`PUB-RGADA-210-SMUTA-1605-1618-OREL-CROSSCHECK`. Open publications:
[the "Dom predkov" index](https://dompredkov.ru/sluzhilye-lyudi-smuty-1605-1618gg/),
[desyatnya of noviki of 1596](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1580-1600/Desjatnja_novikov/text.php).

The popular claim about "48 families resettled from Kashira to Mtsensk",
which turns up in genealogical discussions, likewise cannot yet be
accepted as a ready-made list of settlers. The available Kashira desyatnya
of 1570 survives only as a late folio-by-folio alphabetical index (РГАДА,
f. 388, op. 1, d. 102, fols. 260–264; former number 853), and the figure 48 was obtained by a modern
comparison of the surnames in this index with the Mtsensk landholders of 1596–1627.
A coincidence of surnames creates a hypothesis about direction, but does not replace
the formula of transfer of a specific person.

### 3. The Razryad books, 1475–1605

The accessible parts of the digital publication have been checked for surname variants,
the rare given name, and the formulas «в Орёл», «на Орёл», «из Орла», «орляне» ("to Oryol", "towards Oryol", "from Oryol", "men of Oryol"). No direct
transfer of the first servicemen to the fortress has been found. The formula «на орловские
места» ("towards the Oryol places") refers to the direction of an enemy incursion, and «збирать орлян» ("to gather the men of Oryol")
to mustering people already registered under Oryol. These lines cannot be turned into
evidence of resettlement.

An important name parallel has been found. In the entry for the year 7065, among the Moscow dyaki,
stands **Onfim Seliverstov**; other copies of the same text give **Olfim** and
**Onfil**. This is 1556/57, nine or ten years before the founding of Oryol. The entry
shows a living fluctuation of a closely related name form right in the Moscow
service milieu, but it does not link the dyak with Oryol or Anfilogovs. A
profile `P9659` and a card `PUB-RK-1556-MOSCOW-ONFIL-SELIVERSTOV` have been created.

Consequently, a general reading of the Razryad book does not replace the missing
rank-and-file roster. Special priority remains with papers on the first staffing
of Oryol in 1566–1572 and personal appendices to the acts of 1577.

### 4. The Uglich trace of 1536

«Тонкой Онфилогов» (Tonkoy Onfilogov), the small village of Priluk in Gorodskoy stan of Uglich district — a household
without ploughland, a peasant. If this line is to be checked seriously: the cadastral and
reference (pripravochnye) books of Uglich district of the 16th century and the alphabetical indexes to the books of
the **Moscow (op. 1) and Vladimir (op. 2) desks** of f. 210.

## A separate lead: neighbours move together

In the Oryol book of 1594 the zaimishche of Ivan Nechaev syn Anpilogova lies
«противъ Захара Нечаева сына **Гревцова** съ товарищи» ("opposite Zakhar Nechaev syn Grevtsov and his comrades").

In the Belgorod census of 1646, in the village of Sazhnoye, next to Mikifor
Anpilogov are recorded the **Krivtsovs, Ozerovs, Ofrosimovs, Mezentsovs,
Chursins, Lodygins, Voloboevs** — a set of surnames of the upper-Oka,
Oryol–Kromy circle. Grevtsov is not named in the Sazhnoye line itself:
he cannot be added to the neighbours merely because of the Oryol record of 1594.
The same Ofrosimov whose namesake sat as voivode in Kromy.

It is important not to glue all the Mikifors together. The Belgorod release (otkaznaya) book
of 1637–1642 names **Nikifor Dmitrievich** Anpilogov, and the desyatnya of
20 July 1646 names **Mikifor Efimovich** Onpilogov. These are two people.
In the Sazhnoye census the patronymic is omitted, so the landholder cannot confidently
be assigned either to Dmitrievich or to Efimovich. The neighbourhood cluster confirms
the presence of the surname series in the Belgorod settler milieu, but
not the biography of one particular Nikifor.

Servicemen moved to new towns **in groups, as neighbours**, and
a recurring set of neighbours is an independent argument about the direction of
movement. This is testable: compare the list of neighbours in the village of Sazhnoye in 1646 with
the lists of Oryol and Kromy landholders of 1594/95. Even a large number of
matching surnames will be a strong group indicator but not final
proof of a route: what is needed are matches of given names, patronymics, kinship, or direct
formulas about transfer to a new town.

### Correction: the "Livny guide (vozh) Anpilogov" of 1599 has not been found

A popular article about the first inhabitants of Belgorod includes Anpilogovs in a long series of
surnames of Sazhnoye landholders who had supposedly already appeared among
the **Livny guides (vozhi) of 1599**. This looked like a rare ready-made route and
called for a check against the name-by-name source itself.

All five parts of the open rendering of the separation book of
Belgorod of 1599 — **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, pt. 2, d. 15828** — have been checked in full: 180
numbered land entries and the descriptions of lands that follow them. The forms
Anpilogov, Anfilogov, Onpilogov, Onfilogov, Ampilogov, Amfilogov and
Antilogov do not occur in the text. So the formula "Anpilogov — a Livny guide" cannot
be considered an archival fact. Perhaps the author of the article transferred to the surname a general
conclusion about the neighbourhood circle, or used a different reading; until a
literal line is found, this route is excluded from the evidential scheme and is not put
on the map.

Separately retained is the form **«Никита Ефимов сын Анпилогов»** ("Nikita Efimov syn Anpilogov"), given
by a secondary article for Sazhnoye in 1616. It conflicts with the reliably
published **Mikifor Efimov syn Onpilogov** of the Belgorod desyatnya
of 1646. Nikita cannot automatically be turned into a separate profile or
merged with Mikifor: first the source folio of the inspection/reference
book of 1616 is needed. Audit card:
`PUB-RGADA-1209-1-15828-BELGOROD-1599-ANPILOGOV-AUDIT`.

## A full scan of the 1977 edition of the otkaznye books: Oryol, Kursk and Belgorod

### The razbor of 1620/21: three different stages, but not a single former town

The patronymic on original fol. 67 preserves one more man of the older
generation: **Dmitry, the father of Vasily Anpilogov**. He is not given a separate
service entry, but the formula «Анпилогов Василей Дмитреев» ("Anpilogov, Vasily, son of Dmitry") establishes
paternity just as directly as «Павел Терентьев сын Анфилогов» ("Pavel, son of Terenty, Anfilogov") preserves the name of
Terenty. A research profile `P99345` has been created for Dmitry, without a point of his own
on the map.

The full roll of the surname group in the 1594/95 book gives Mikula,
two different Vasilys, Persha, Nechay, Timofey and Pakhom as fathers or elders,
but **Dmitry is not among the eight landholders**. This is an important limitation: the father
of Vasily Dmitrievich cannot be attached to any early branch on chronology
alone. His absence does not prove that he came from another town: Dmitry may have
died before the survey, may have held no land of his own, or may simply not have been entered in
the land book. The document to look for now is not another surname
match but an entry on Vasily's first enrolment (verstanye), on his inheritance of a share in
Bazdyrevo, or a late genealogical extract naming the father together with
his place of service.

In the open photocopies of **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 14, d. 502** three separate entries of the surname
series have been located and checked. What matters is not only the names
but also each man's place within the service rubrics:

| person | folio | rubric and readable formula | what this proves |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Vasily Dmitrievich Anpilogov | 67 | «в службу написан вновь» ("newly enrolled in service") | in 1620/21 he began independent service at Oryol |
| Oleksey Vasilyevich Anfilokhov | 94 v. | «без окладов» ("without oklads"); «Поместья ему нету» ("he has no pomestye") | was in the Oryol razbor before the subsequent novik enrolment of 1621/22 |
| Ivan Vasilyevich Anfilokhov | 109 | noviki of the years 7128 and 7129 | already belonged to the novik group; the exact year within the pair and the oklad figures are still being read |

The rubrics were not established by a single modern guess. Vasily stands between
Kotov and Selekhov, who are listed with him in the published
section of the newly enrolled; Aleksey stands between Teplov and Onakhin of the
section without oklads; Ivan is followed by Chyupakhin and two Kozakovs of the novik
section. The order of the name index matches the continuous order of the blocks
on the original folios.

For the question of origin the result is both strong and negative.
It shows **the internal replenishment of the Oryol service core**: one
Anpilogov was newly entering service, a second did not yet have a pomestye, a third was
a novik. But none of the three entries has so far yielded "from such-and-such a town",
"transferred" or "formerly served". A novik could be an adult newcomer, but he could
equally be the son of a serviceman already settled at Oryol. So these folios
support the model of several local branches growing outward better than the hypothesis
of a ready-made family arriving all at once; nor are they proof of local birth.

Cards for the originals: `RGADA-210-14-502-L67`, `RGADA-210-14-502-L94`,
`RGADA-210-14-502-L109`. Published structure of the list and name index:
[ВГД forum thread describing the file](https://forum.vgd.ru/5703/135781/).

### The early Kursk branch: Semyon in 1619 and Ivan in 1624

A. I. Razdorsky's name index to the receipt sections of 18 Kursk
customs books led to two early entries of the surname group: «Анпилогов
Семен; *; **; 1619» and «Анпилогов Иван; *; Курск; 1623/24» ("Anpilogov Semyon … 1619"; "Anpilogov Ivan … Kursk … 1623/24"). The full texts
had already been published by S. I. Kotkov and N. S. Kotkova in 1982 and
make far more sense than the index lines.

**On 22 June 1619** Tereshka Ivanov registered in Kursk the purchase of
a dun (savrasaya) mare for five roubles and was called «Семенов крестьянин
Анпилогов» ("Semyon Anpilogov's peasant"). This proves directly that Semyon Anpilogov had a dependent
peasant, but it does not allow us to call Semyon himself a
pomeshchik automatically: the entry tells neither the kind of right to the land nor where the holding lay.
Original: РГАДА, f. 210, op. 8, vyazka 1, pt. 1, no. 10, fols. 62–62 v.;
publication: pp. 93–94.

**On 21 May 1624** «курченин Иван Онпилогов» ("Ivan Onpilogov, a man of Kursk") bought a dark bay gelding
for two roubles. Here the place is stated directly: Ivan is called a resident of Kursk.
The archival reference has been corrected from the closing note on p. 132 of the edition and from
Razdorsky's later work: РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6-zh, kn. 79, fol. 78 v.
The card's earlier reference to kn. 82 and p. 159 was wrong: kn. 82 belongs
to the next Kursk book, of 1626/27.

The Kursk Ivan cannot be merged without a new document with the Oryol Ivan
Vasilyevich Anpilogov. The latter already belonged to the Oryol service core
in 1620/21 and is again called a man of Oryol, a syn boyarsky, in 1625;
the Kursk Ivan's patronymic is unknown. For the question of origin both finds matter as
evidence of an early Kursk–Oryol zone over which the surname spread, but not
as a route Kursk → Oryol: the Oryol group is documented from 1594/95,
that is, well before both Kursk entries.

The academic edition by S. I. Kotkov and N. S. Kotkova,
"Monuments of the South Great Russian Dialect. Otkaznye Books" (Moscow, 1977), has been read in full. Its introductory
part gives the exact correspondence between the published sections and the archival books of
РГАДА, f. 1209: Belgorod — op. 156, kn. 15820; Kursk — op. 188,
kn. 15684; Oryol — op. 199, kn. 10054.

**Oryol, 19 April 1625.** On fols. 39–41 of the Oryol book, at the allotment
of land below the Minov forest, among the «орлян детей боярских» ("Oryol deti boyarskie") Oleksey
Onfilogov and Ivan Onfilogov stand side by side. By their names, the rare surname, the town and the chronology
this is a strong continuation of the profiles of Aleksey and Ivan Vasilyevich, known
from the razbor of 1620/21 and the muster of 1626. The 1625 entry itself does not
repeat the patronymic, so the proof of identity is composite, not literal.

On 30 August 2026 the archival reference was checked in yet another independent
way, against РГАДА's official handwritten act-by-act inventory. The opening table
of the volume assigns book 10054 to register folios 49–145, and on fol. 49 the act of 19 April
7133 stands as **article no. 1**. The short heading names the recipients of the land:
Semyon and Yakov Rodionov and Savely Konstantinov; the article runs to three folios.
The names and date match the publication of fols. 39–41, where Onfilogovs are listed
among the witnesses. This not only confirms the reference: it also became clear why a search
of the register headings does not find Ivan and Aleksey. Witnesses are not carried into the short inventory.
So two parallel reviews are needed next: owners and
transfers of pomestyes across the whole register, and hidden appearances as witnesses in the
full articles of book 10054.

**Belgorod, 1637–1642.** Nikifor Anpilogov is three times called a Belgorod
syn boyarsky. On 7 January 1637 and 23 March 1642 he acted as
otkazchik: he rode out to the land, gathered the local people, described and allotted
pomestyes. On 27 March 1641 he is himself among the Belgorod deti
boyarskie petitioning for land. The name index at first allowed him to be read
as Nikifor Dmitrievich and suggested brotherhood with the Oryol Vasily
Dmitrievich. A full check of pp. 24, 29–31 overturned this: Nikifor has no
patronymic, and the one called Dmitrievich on p. 24 is the voivode Afanasy Turgenev. The derived
index ranks below the full text here; the brotherhood hypothesis is withdrawn.

**Kursk district, 28 January 1642.** On fols. 628–630 of the Kursk book it is said
that Vaska Onpilogov formerly lived in the Podgorodny stan, «в деревне
Анпилоговой дяди своего в Гавриловом поместье Анпилогова» ("in the hamlet of Anpilogova of his uncle, in the pomestye of Gavrila Anpilogov"). This is the first
full formula found that links two relatives, the surname and
a hamlet of the same name. By that time Gavrila's pomestye had already been given out to Ivan
Lukin, Efrem Luzhetsky and Logvin Pospekhov. The reason for the redistribution is not
given. The Kursk Vaska is kept separate from the Oryol Vasily Dmitrievich:
without a patronymic it is too early to merge them.

**Oryol, 5 October 1640.** The role of Afonka Anpilogov has been clarified. He is not
merely a participant in a land entry: the closing formula says outright
«а отказныя книги писал орленин Афонька Анпилогов» ("and the otkaznye books were written by Afonka Anpilogov, a man of Oryol"). The reference has been established:
РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 199, kn. 10054, fols. 851–852 v. The form «Аеонька
Фнеилогов» in Vashchenko's article reflects how his writing was rendered, but without
the original it cannot be called an autograph.

This series changes the direction of the conclusion. So far it shows no migration
**to Oryol** from Kursk or Belgorod. On the contrary, the most cautious model is that
the Oryol service nest already existed by 1596, and by the 1630s–1640s
the circle of kin had spread out to Kursk and Belgorod. A proven route
would need the Kursk Vaska's patronymic and a direct record of kinship between Nikifor and Vasily
Dmitrievich.

## What not to do

Do not look for "where from" by the surname alone. In the 16th century it is spelled three ways,
and half its bearers still go by secular (non-baptismal) names. What must be sought is a **circle** —
a nest of kin plus their constant neighbours — and then a move shows up as the relocation
of a whole cluster, not of one man.

### Central service registers of the 1550s: a negative check

The **Thousand Book of 1550** with its appendices and the
**Court Register (Dvorovaya tetrad) of the 1550s** have been checked in full. They contain no Anfilogovs, Anpilogovs,
Onfilogovs, Ampilogovs or any close spelling of the surname under study. Nor
does the Oryol neighbourhood circle of the Bazdyrevs, Trunovs, Nemtsovs,
Krivolapovs and Grevtsovs come together there.

The rare names of the presumed fathers of the first Oryol
generation were checked separately. Mikulas, Nechays, Pakhoms and Terentys occur in the big lists,
but none of them yields the right family. Especially important is the negative result for
the combination **Mikula — Vasily, Timofey and Karp**: there is no such father with such sons.
The name Persha was not found in a suitable family formula either. For example,
the Zubtsov men Ivan and Mikula, sons of Kitay Lvov, are merely namesakes with no
connection to Oryol.

This does not mean that the ancestors did not serve or belonged to the lowest layer:
both sources reflect the central service elite, not all the provincial deti
boyarskie of the Russian state. But the version that the Oryol nest descended directly
from the Sovereign's Court or the chosen thousand now has less
basis. The search should concentrate on provincial town lists,
survey-book extracts and otkaznye books of Belyov, Novosil, Krapivna, Mtsensk,
Bolkhov, Karachev and Bryansk.

Structured audit card:
`PUB-TKD-1550-DVOROVAYA-TYSYACHNAYA-ANFILOGOV-AUDIT`.
Sources: [the Thousand Book](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1540-1560/Tys_kniga_1550/text.htm) and
[the Court Register](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1540-1560/Dvor_tetrad/text1.htm).

### All ten surviving desyatnyas of the 16th century: the corpus closed negatively

The repeat check has been widened from individual promising towns to **the entire
surviving corpus**. In the preface to his edition V. N. Storozhev states outright
that only ten 16th-century desyatnyas (muster rolls) survived in the Archive of the Ministry of Justice
and that he publishes their full text. They are Kolomna 1577,
Pereyaslavl 1590, Murom 1597, Epifan 1585 and 1591, Kashira
1599, and four Ryazhsk books of 1579, 1591, 1594 and 1597.

All thirteen electronic parts of these ten books were brought into a single
encoding and checked for the forms Анфилог-, Анпилог-, Онфилог-, Ампилог-,
Анфилоф-, for the name Mikula and its derivatives, and for combinations of Vasily,
Timofey and Karp with a patronymic from Mikula. Then the context of every line
with the stem Микул- was read. **Neither the surname under study nor the combination Mikula — Vasily,
Timofey and Karp is in the corpus.** Ivashka Mikulin syn Voltsov, Kirilo Mikulin
syn Khomutskogo, Mikula Vasilyev syn Khomutskogo and the numerous Ryazhsk
Mikulshins have different full formulas and are not our family.

The negative result is stronger than the earlier individual checks, but its limit
is strict. It closes repeat searches of the ten surviving books, not all the
service towns of the 16th century: most desyatnyas were lost, and the father may have been
recorded before he had a surname. Priority now passes definitively to
personal extracts from lost desyatnyas, separate (otdelnye) and otkaznye books,
mestnichestvo cases and late lawsuits that quoted old service records.
No route is drawn on the map. Card:
`PUB-DESYATNI-XVI-SURVIVING-CORPUS-ANFILOGOV-AUDIT`.
[Storozhev's preface](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/pred.htm).

### Mikula's rare family signature: the published corpus is exhausted

After the general surname search, the strongest formula of the
older generation was checked separately: **Mikula — Vasily, Timofey and Karp**. In the Oryol book
it is recorded not as a reconstruction but literally: «За Васильемъ, да за
Тимоффемъ, да за Карпомъ за Микулиными детьми Анфилогова» ("held by Vasily, and Timofey, and Karp, Mikula's sons, Anfilogov"); the brothers held
three pomeshchik homesteads in Bazdyrevo «на их жеребью» ("on their lot").

The full electronic text of "Survey Books of the Muscovite State" was checked
for every form of «Микулины дети» ("Mikula's sons"). The exact trio occurred only in the
Oryol article itself. The other family formulas belong to the Enkovs, Vlasovs
and Grekovs and contain different sets of sons. The Thousand Book, the Court Register, the ten surviving
16th-century desyatnyas and four volumes of "Acts of Service Landholders" have already been checked
with the same result.

This is a strong negative result, but not proof of local
origin. Most town lists were lost, and Mikula himself may have been
recorded without the later surname. Nor does the formula «на их жеребью» say outright
"the father's pomestye". So the next source must be of another genre:
an early separate or otkaznaya entry for Bazdyrevo, an extract about Mikula's old pomestye,
a land lawsuit, or the full Oryol books of 1625/26 quoting the
earlier grant. This audit creates no new profiles and no route on the map.

### The "Dom predkov" search database: the surname was replaced by the fathers' names

An open database of 421,095 index rows was also checked. The search ran
not only on the familiar beginnings **Анпи-, Ампи-, Анфи-, Онпи-, Онфи-**, but also
on the names of the presumed fathers of the Oryol group — **Mikula, Pakhom, Nechay,
Persha** — and on Bazdyrevo's neighbours: **the Trunovs, Bazdyrevs, Krivolapovs and
Nemtsovs**. Year filters 156, 157, 158 and 159 covered the results for 1560–1599.

There is no direct pre-Oryol Anpilogov/Anfilogov in these results. A search for Неча-
gives dozens of Novgorod namesakes, which shows once again that the name is not unique
and does not allow any Ivan Nechaev to be turned into the Oryol landholder. For
Баздыр-, Криволап- and Пахом- no suitable row came up in the decades checked
either.

The only early match on **Анфи-** turned out to be a useful control, not
an ancestor: **Anfinogen Zolotukhin**, Novgorod, Obonezhskaya pyatina, 1583,
file 1209-1-725. Anfinogen is an independent calendar name; it is not a variant
of Anfilog or Anfilof. So no profile or map point is created for him.

The negative result is limited by the scope of the index: it does not cover all
surviving documents, let alone the lost desyatnyas. But it closes
any repetition of the general prefix search in this database. The next move must be
targeted: extracts from lost desyatnyas, otkaznye books and late lawsuits that
might have quoted the former place of service. Audit card:
`PUB-DOMPREDKOV-FASTTABLE-XVI-ANFILOGOV-SEARCH-AUDIT`.
[Database](https://next-fast-table-gold.vercel.app/),
[page of the only control match](https://dompredkov.ru/pomeshhiki-obonezhskoj-pyatiny-novgoroda-1583g-ch-1-nagornaya-polovina/).

## Interim conclusion

### Modern scholarly reconstruction: the source towns are already named

There is also primary-source confirmation that a long-distance northern flow into the neighbouring
Upper Oka garrisons was a reality and not merely an inference from late
surnames. An archival fragment of 1593 names Bolkhov service Cossacks:
Mikhalko Mikulin the Novgorodian, Ondryushka the Muscovite, Vaska the Yaroslavl man,
Vaska the Balakhna man and Ivashko the Meshchera man; among their comrades are Ivashko of Yuryev,
Vaska the Kazan man, Kondrashko of Vologda and Fedka of Yuryev. Publication reference:
РГАДА, f. 141, op. 1, 1593, d. 1, fol. 33 v.; V. I. Koretsky, 1975,
pp. 84–85.

The names work here as geographical labels: Novgorod, Moscow, Yaroslavl,
Balakhna, Meshchera, Yuryev, Kazan and Vologda came together in one small group
of Bolkhov Cossacks. For Oryol this is strong confirmation of the very mechanism of
long-distance recruitment into a neighbouring garrison. But this is still **Bolkhov, not Oryol**:
the nine men cannot be rewritten as first settlers of Oryol. A check of the full
Oryol section of 1594/95 gave not a single full-name match with
these Cossacks; two nameless «москвитина» ("Muscovites") among the Oryol traders are useless for
identification. Card:
`PUB-KORETSKY-1975-BOLKHOV-COSSACK-ORIGINS-1593`.

In 2023 K. B. Kudlanov and R. O. Golubkov published a dedicated
comparison of the settlement trajectories of Putivl and Oryol districts. For
early Oryol they single out not a vague "north" but specific sources
of the flow. After the town's founding in 1566 the authors link the service
settlement above all with **Belyov, Novosil and Krapivna**, and also with
**Pronsk**. A strong
nearby line ran from **Bolkhov, Mtsensk, Karachev and the Livny area**;
a northern flow is additionally traced from **Serpukhov and Kaluga**.

Checking the footnotes calls for a substantial caveat. On p. 95 this list is
attributed to information from V. M. Nedelin, but the reference given, [9], in the bibliography is
a modern "Map of Oryol District" on dor-zhur.livejournal.com, not
a 16th-century archival register. The Serpukhov and Kaluga directions the authors
derive from surnames in D. K. Zelenin. So the exact towns are a well-founded
historiographical search map, but not a found list of svedentsy.

The authors distinguish three groups among the first settlers: **svedentsy** ("transferees"), whom
the state transferred from old service towns; **skhodtsy** ("comers"), who came
of their own accord; and **local people**. They consider the main flow to be the northern
Great Russian one from the Oka river system, especially from the towns of the Tula
defensive line. The local and nearby flow was second in importance;
the western, eastern and southern flows were auxiliary.

This study agrees independently with our check of the primary books.
The surnames Kaluzhin, Serpukhovitinov, Putivltsov and Starodubtsov really
can be read in the Oryol book of 1594/95; the Koptev, Elagin, Glebov and
Bolotov families form cautious bridges to Tula, Mtsensk and Kashira; the Venyov book
of 1571/72 directly shows the state mechanism itself for granting pomestyes to
Tula and Kashira men in a new district.

But the article does not turn Belyov or Novosil into the proven homeland
of Anfilogovs. It establishes **the corridors of the Oryol population as a whole**.
So these towns have been entered in the research card, but no general route
has been drawn on the family map: a line would need an early Anpilog/Anfilog,
a matching family, or a direct formula «сведён на Орёл» ("transferred to Oryol").

Card: `PUB-KUDLANOV-GOLUBKOV-2023-OREL-SETTLEMENT-TRAJECTORIES`.
[Article, pp. 91–101](https://vestnik-brgu.ru/wp-content/numbers/2023-02/v2023-02.pdf),
DOI: 10.22281/2413-9912-2023-07-02-91-101.

### The Ovsyannikovs: a possible Tula–Oryol bridge, but not a "move from Krapivna"

One of the late genealogical stories calls the Ovsyannikovs natives of
Krapivna and dates their move to Oryol to 1593. The basis of the dating turned out
not to be a resettlement record: in 1593 a regiment set out from Krapivna under the voivodes
Zhdan Saburov and Prince Semyon Zvenigorodsky. The regimental formula contains no
Ovsyannikovs and no report of families being transferred to Oryol. The author of the story
himself warns plainly that he is building a hypothesis. A military departure from a town cannot
be turned into a family route. Nor is the auxiliary date confirmed:
a dedicated study of Krapivna notes the absence of a reliable document about
its move to a new site in 1587. New Krapivna certainly
existed by 1627, but its location at the turn of the 16th–17th centuries
can be established only tentatively.

However, the check of the primary books gave a more substantive lead. In Tula
district in 1587–1589 are recorded **Roman Maksimovich, Lyova Elizaryevich, Maksim
Vasilyevich and Grigory Maksimovich Ovsyannikov**. Lyova held his father's
pomestye outright; Grigory held an old pomestye; Maksim Vasilyevich can be traced in
the Nyukhovsky and Verkoshsky stans. In Oryol district in 1594/95 is recorded
**Vasily Grigoryevich Ovsyannikov**. By date and patronymic he could be the son of
the Tula Grigory Maksimovich: this gives a compatible chain Maksim —
Grigory — Vasily. But no entry so far calls this Grigory the father
of the Oryol Vasily specifically, or speaks of a move from Tula.

The Oryol book itself also contains proven internal kinship: **Isay
Sofronovich Ovsyannikov** is called the uncle of **Yakushka Samsonovich Ovsyannikov**.
Next to them is recorded **Filimon Sofronovich Ovsyannikov**, probably Isay's brother, but without
a direct formula of brotherhood. Another Filimon Ovsyannikov is already named as the former
holder of the clearing (pochinok) of Ovsyannikov on the Mokry Bensk. These men show
a developed Oryol family circle, but they do not prove Krapivna.

A research card has been created:
`PUB-OVSYANNIKOV-TULA-OREL-1587-1595-CROSSCHECK`. No profiles or map line have been
added: the surname serves as a control example of the general Oryol core, while
the identity of the generations and the move itself are not yet confirmed. The full roll of noviki
of 1596 has also been checked: the surname Ovsyannikov does not appear in it, neither in the Tula, nor in the
Oryol, nor in the other sections. This does not refute the family's existence;
it is the absence of the needed intermediate link in one particular enrolment campaign.

### The "Otvodnaya kniga po Orlu" of 1591/92: a systematic confusion with Ufa uncovered

In 2009 M. I. Lavitskaya reported the first mass granting of pomestyes to Oryol
servicemen in 1591–1592 and cited the «Отводную книгу по Орлу» ("Land Allotment Book for Oryol")
of 1591/1592–1629: РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 2, d. 15108. From it the author also gave
four names. The syn boyarsky **Yakov Savenkov** supposedly received in 1591
ploughland formerly worked by **Stepan Mikhaylov**. In 1594 the zhilets
**Matvey Vasilyev** received a grant part of which had formerly been used by
the streltsy sotnik (company commander) **Ostafy Martynov**.

This find looked like a direct path to the first Oryol land
distribution, so the reference was checked not against retellings but against the digital
scans of the official inventory 2 of fond 1209. On printed pages 75–76, in the section
**«31. Орел и Болхов»** ("31. Oryol and Bolkhov"), books nos. **10052–10072, 12850 and 12852** are listed.
The Oryol materials of this section begin only in **1617**. Then
the summary index on scans 0432–0436 was checked: it assigns by town
all **1,303 units** of this part of the inventory. No. 15108 does not appear in it at all, not only
in the Oryol section. Consequently, the stated modern reference is impossible.

The problem is aggravated by the almost exact match of the title with the well-known
**«Отводной книгой по Уфе» ("Land Allotment Book for Ufa") of 1591/92–1626**, published by N. F.
Demidova. The Ufa book is kept in the same fond 1209: **op. 1, kn. 517**;
its study is likewise built around a mass allotment of land in
the early 1590s, earlier land use by declaration (yavka) and state
supply of the garrison. Lavitskaya's Oryol paragraph follows the same logic.

The decisive point was not the stylistic resemblance but **the exact number 417**. Lavitskaya
writes that she had at her disposal 417 vvoznye, otkaznye and razdelnye charters
for Oryol. But the abstract of B. A. Aznabaev's 2006 doctoral dissertation
states plainly that there are **417 otkaznye charters of Ufa district** from the fonds "Disputed
Cases of the General Survey" and "Ufa Prikaznaya Izba". Thus
the title of the book, the years, the logic of the study and a rare exact
number of documents all coincided at once. This can no longer be explained by a simple misprint of an archival number.
The Oryol statistical block and the periodisation built on it cannot be considered
proven without a separate Oryol database.

In particular, Lavitskaya's sentence about Oryol being sharply replenished with dvoryane from **Livny,
Yelets and other towns** relates only to her stage of **1636–1680**, not to
the core of the 1590s. There is no separate reference to a named transfer register attached to this
sentence. After the Ufa statistical source was discovered, it cannot
serve as evidence for the route of the first Oryol settlers.

The earlier paragraph of the article, on p. 115, has also been checked. Lavitskaya writes that the children of no
fewer than **62 old "zhiltsy"** served at Oryol at the end of the 16th and start of the 17th century, and
that the descendants of more than **150 old zhiltsy** formed the backbone of the service town in
the first half of the 17th century. Yet there is no name register of these people. The footnote after
the word «жильцов» ("zhiltsy") only explains the term, and the following list of the first
landholders carries a general reference to f. 1209, op. 1 without book or folios.

An internal control is especially telling: in the printed list of **54 surnames**
of the first landholders there are no Anfilogovs in any variant. Meanwhile the full
primary text of 1594/95 independently records **nine landholders**
of the surname group, several of whom had received their fathers' pomestyes. So
the article's list is obviously selective. The figures 62 and 150 can be kept only as
the author's general model of continuity of early service families; they do not name
an ancestor of Anfilogovs, a former town or a migration route.

Still, the four names cannot simply be discarded. In the genuine Oryol survey
book of 1594/95 **two Yakov Savenkovs** have indeed been found: Yakov Kuzmin
syn Savenkov in the hamlet of Savenkova, Kamensky stan, and Yakov Timofeev syn Savenkov
in the clearing of Greshinsky Elkov, Korchakovsky stan. Lavitskaya gives no patronymic,
so neither can be confidently identified with the recipient of land in 1591.
There are as yet no independent matches for Stepan Mikhaylov, Matvey Vasilyev and Ostafy
Martynov. The names are kept as search keys; no profiles or map route
have been created.

Card: `PUB-LAVITSKAYA-2009-OREL-OTVODNAYA-CITATION-AUDIT`.
[Lavitskaya's article](https://cyberleninka.ru/article/n/proishozhdenie-i-evolyutsiya-dvoryanstva-i-pomestnogo-zemlevladeniya-orlovskoy-gubernii),
[official РГАДА inventory](http://rgada.info/poisk/index2.php?str=1209-opis_2-1),
[study of the Ufa book](https://ufagen.ru/node/31089),
[B. A. Aznabaev's dissertation abstract](https://new-disser.ru/_avtoreferats/01003310047.pdf).

The origin of **the Oryol service population as a whole** is so far outlined only
as a reconstruction: above all a northern Great Russian flow from the Oka basin
through Serpukhov, Kaluga, Belyov, Novosil and Krapivna, supplemented by people from
Mtsensk, Bolkhov, Karachev, the Livny area and Pronsk. This is not a surviving
list of settlers. The documents of 1577 reliably show something else: settlement
and recruitment were still continuing, people were sorely lacking, personal lists
were ordered to be drawn up, and three shifts with Oryol Cossacks actually went out on
service.

The origin of **Anfilogovs in particular** has not yet been narrowed to one town.
The strongest external hypothesis is the northern/Upper Oka corridor. The strongest
family hypothesis is local formation of the surname in Korchakovsky stan from
the personal name Anpilog; a man with that name and a different surname, Anpilog
Ivanov syn Onanyin, has already been found in the same stan. The next evidential step is
to find before 1594 not necessarily an "Anfilogov", but an Anpilog and the whole familiar
circle of surnames in the books of the nine source towns.

### The six-folio razbor in inventory 21: not Oryol but Kolomna, 1599

On the first page of inventory 21 an especially tempting item was **РГАДА, f. 210,
op. 21, d. 4, fols. 1–6** (old numbering 50–55): «Разборная книга
служилых людей (отрывок)» ("Razbor book of servicemen (fragment)"), no earlier than 1597, under the counter-signature (skrepa) of the dyak Andrey
Ivanov. With no town in the title, it could have been taken for a remnant of the Seversk
muster of 1597, which continued the nationwide enrolment of noviki
of 1596.

A. L. Stanislavsky had already made the necessary attribution from internal features.
The full counter-signature reads **«Дияк Андрей Иванов сын Татьянин»** ("Dyak Andrey Ivanov syn Tatyanin"). The fragment
names the okladchiki (assessors) Semyon Birev, Matvey Dubensky, Nikita Ignatyev, Ivan Norov
and Nikita Fomin — the same men appear in the Kolomna desyatnya of 1577. Among
the noviki are Stepan Semyonovich Ignatyev and Nazary Ivanovich Khotunsky,
who later served among the vybor of Kolomna. Finally, the inventory of files saved in
the fire of 1626 directly names a Kolomna book of **the year 107/1599**
of money distribution and novik pomestye enrolment under the counter-signature of Andrey
Tatyanin.

Consequently, d. 4 is reliably excluded: it is a fragment of the **Kolomna desyatnya
of 1599**, not a Seversk or Oryol roll. The negative result is
methodologically useful: a late catalogue shortened the dyak's full name and
hid the town, and the geography was restored through the group of okladchiki. But there is no longer any need to order these
six folios for the origin of the Oryol core. Card:
`PUB-RGADA-F210-OP21-D4-KOLOMNA-1599-AUDIT`.
[Official inventory](http://rgada.info/poisk/index2.php?str=210-opis_21),
[Stanislavsky's scholarly attribution](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Rospis_det_boj_m_o_b/text.htm).

### What the muster of 1597 does give

Although the six-folio candidate turned out to be from Kolomna, the razryad entry of 1597
itself remains an important continuation of the Oryol series. On 15 March Tsar Fyodor
Ivanovich ordered that the deti boyarskie of «всю землю» ("all the land") be granted money pay and
that commissions be sent to the service centres. Pyotr Nikitich Sheremetev and
the duma dvoryanin Elizary Leontyevich Rzhevsky were sent «в Северу во
Брянске и во все северские городы» ("to the Severa, to Bryansk and to all the Seversk towns"); the name of the dyak who followed them in the razryad
entry is lost after the words «да диака» ("and the dyak").

M. G. Krotov established the link between two nationwide measures: the noviki
enrolled with pomestyes in Moscow in 1596 were given supplementary money pay
at the muster of 1597; those who had failed to appear a year earlier could be enrolled with both pomestye and
money. So the Oryol group of 1596 must have gone through the next
administrative review. But the decree does not name Oryol directly and does not list all
the commissions' towns: it cannot yet be decided whether the Oryol men were reviewed by the Seversk, the Tula
or the bereg (Oka river line) commission.

There is also an important negative control. In Krotov's chronological inventory, under
1597, desyatnyas of twelve districts are reconstructed: originals for Murom and
Ryazhsk, extracts for Arzamas, Kashira, Kolomna, Lukh, Meshchera, Nizhny Novgorod,
Pereslavl-Zalessky and Suzdal. In the summary index of towns **Oryol appears
only under 1596**. Consequently, an "Oryol desyatnya of 1597" is not yet a
known archival file but a probable lost product of the general muster. Until
its own heading, alphabet or extract is found, it cannot be cited as
a surviving document.

What must be sought now is not a new general order but the documentary descendants of
the lost Oryol review of 1597: a surname alphabet, an extract of the Razryad
archive, the formula of a later desyatnya «по верстанью 105-го году» ("by the enrolment of the year 105"), or a pomestye
act on the issue of money pay. Such material could separate the old
Oryol core, the noviki of 1596, the absentees and those without pomestyes. Card:
`PUB-KROTOV-1597-SEVERSKY-MUSTER-OREL-BRIDGE`.
[M. G. Krotov's study](https://muscovitearmor.wordpress.com/2018/07/08/%d1%8f%d0%ba%d0%be%d0%b2-%d0%ba%d1%80%d0%be%d1%82%d0%be%d0%b2-%d0%b4%d0%b5%d1%81%d1%8f%d1%82%d0%bd%d0%b8-xvi-%d0%bd%d0%b0%d1%87%d0%b0-xvii-%d0%b2%d0%b2-%d0%ba%d0%b0%d0%ba-%d0%b8/),
[the desyatnya of noviki of 1596](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1580-1600/Desjatnja_novikov/text.php).

### Four Heraldry files: the first family archival bridge

The search for the documentary descendants of the lost desyatnya has for the first time led not to
a general fond but to a precise family complex. In the official catalogue of РГИА
four files of the Senate's Department of Heraldry for Kursk province have been found:

- **f. 1343, op. 16, d. 2345**, «Дело о дворянстве. Анфилоговых» ("File on the nobility of Anfilogovs"),
  1826–1887;
- **d. 2348**, the same spelling of the surname, 1867–1886;
- **d. 2164**, «Дело о дворянстве. Анпилоговых» ("File on the nobility of Anpilogovs"), 1851;
- **d. 2165**, the same spelling, 1859.

The top priority is d. 2345. Its wide date range suggests
the original proof of nobility, Heraldry decisions and later
additions. It is precisely in such proceedings that the archival certificate may have survived
from which the well-known formula about Vasily Dmitrievich Anfilogov comes,
«записанного по городу Орлу Московского стола в десятках» ("entered under the town of Oryol, Moscow desk, in the desyatnyas"). If the certificate
exists, what matters is not only the quotation but its clerical apparatus: the year, the title of the book,
the old reference, the folios and the institution that issued the extract.

The content of the folios is not yet available. So it cannot be claimed that d. 2345
already contains this extract, nor can the four files be automatically merged into one branch,
nor can a "founder" named in the future be considered the biological founder of
the whole surname. In noble-status proceedings the founder could be the
earliest ancestor from whom the applicants managed to prove legally an
unbroken chain.

There is also a practical lead on access. On 30 August 2024 an РГИА reader
asked on a genealogy forum for d. 2345 specifically to be re-photographed and called
it **microfilmed**. This is not an official description of the medium and not a
publication of the folios, but it raises the chance of getting a copy quickly in the reading room.
A search for ready publicly available images gave no result. The list of surnames
and places in the signature of the post's author cannot be passed off as the content of the file.

A separate compilation of entries in the Kursk noble genealogical book clarified the
chronology. Under the form **Anfilogovs** Part VI is given not once but for
**1824, 1830, 1833, 1835, 1849, 1852, 1853, 1886 and 1887**; under the form
**Anpilogovs**, for **1839**. At the same time both forms occur in Part II,
and Anfilogovs also in Part III. So we are dealing not with a single one-off
"recognition of the surname" but with several branches and grounds: antiquity of the family,
military and civil service. The year 1824 is especially important: it precedes
the starting date of d. 2345, so the file may indeed contain not the original
provincial decision but its review or continuation. The compilation is incomplete and does not
replace the Heraldry books, but it gives exact years for searching the rulings.

From these years an immediate predecessor has been found. **РГИА, f. 1330,
op. 18, d. 137 A–B** is the case of the non-commissioned officer Anfilogov and his brother, an odnodvorets,
about land, 26 January 1822 – 25 October 1823. The main part runs to 124
folios, the attached Senate proceedings to another 67. Two full brothers
are designated by different statuses within the old service society; the land
dispute ended immediately before the surname was entered in Part VI. It
may contain ownership extracts and proofs of inheritance, but the catalogue
names neither the brothers, nor the land, nor the outcome. The entry from
the name index has now been located exactly: **РГИА, f. 1343, op. 51, d. 593,
fol. 30**. On this folio are officially indexed **«Анфилогов А. Ф.,
дворянин»** ("Anfilogov A. F., dvoryanin") and **«Анфилогов Р., унтер-офиц.»** ("Anfilogov R., non-commissioned officer"). The card for the whole alphabetical
genealogical book for 1788–1875 is marked by the archive as having
scanned folios and reveals a total of 471 folios; the open card for
fol. 30 shows the names but does not serve the image file. The match of surname
and rank makes R. a strong candidate for the participant in the land case, but it does not
reveal his full name, and proximity to A. F. in the alphabetical book does not
prove kinship.

The first book of ancient nobility itself has now also been officially checked:
**РГИА, f. 1343, op. 51, d. 155**, «Родословная книга дворян Курской
губернии» ("Genealogical book of the nobles of Kursk province"), 1824. The catalogue marks scans as available and indexes in the book,
on exactly **fol. 100**, **«Анфилогова А. М.»** ("Anfilogov A. M."), a titular counsellor of the Kursk
police. A separate official card exists for the folio, although the open catalogue
does not serve the image itself. This is the first
named point in the sequence: the brothers' land case of 1822–1823
→ the entry in Part VI of 1824 → the Heraldry file of 1826–1887. It does not
prove that A. M. was one of the brothers or that it was he who represented
the whole branch of Vasily Dmitrievich: the full name, patronymic and genealogy are not shown in the open
catalogue. A private alphabet of the next book, d. 164 for 1833,
again names A. M. Anfilogov; for now this is a navigational hint, not
a substitute for the official line. No separate profile has been created on initials alone.
[The 1824 book](https://fgurgia.ru/object/2997305),
[folio 100](https://fgurgia.ru/object/6987611),
[alphabet of the 1833 book](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/406/67119/p1926171.htm).

The published staff lists of Kursk province made it possible to remove one false
identification. In Parts II of the mesyatseslovs (official almanacs) for 1824 and 1825 the spreads of the Kursk section were
checked directly. In both editions the Kursk
**politsmeister** (police chief) is the collegiate assessor **Ivan Nesterovich Gendelev**;
A. M. Anfilogov is not among the listed heads and officials of the government
offices. Consequently, the catalogue note "Kursk police" does not allow
Anfilogov to be called the police chief. He may have held a lower or
temporary post not included in the abridged roll. This route did not reveal the full name,
so fol. 100 of the genealogical book remains necessary.
[Mesyatseslov for 1824, Part II](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:%D0%9C%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%8F%D1%86%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B2_%D1%81_%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BF%D0%B8%D1%81%D1%8C%D1%8E_%D1%87%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%BD%D1%8B%D1%85_%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%B1,_%D0%B8%D0%BB%D0%B8_%D0%9E%D0%B1%D1%89%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D1%88%D1%82%D0%B0%D1%82_%D0%A0%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%81%D0%B8%D0%B9%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%BF%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%B8,_%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%BB%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%BE_%D0%BE%D1%82_%D0%A0%D0%BE%D0%B6%D0%B4%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81%D1%82%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B0_1824._%D0%A7%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D1%8C_%D0%B2%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.pdf),
[Mesyatseslov for 1825, Part II](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:%D0%9C%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%8F%D1%86%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B2_%D1%81_%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BF%D0%B8%D1%81%D1%8C%D1%8E_%D1%87%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%BD%D1%8B%D1%85_%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%B1,_%D0%B8%D0%BB%D0%B8_%D0%9E%D0%B1%D1%89%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D1%88%D1%82%D0%B0%D1%82_%D0%A0%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%81%D0%B8%D0%B9%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%BF%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%B8,_%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%BB%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%BE_%D0%BE%D1%82_%D0%A0%D0%BE%D0%B6%D0%B4%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81%D1%82%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B0_1825._%D0%A7%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D1%8C_%D0%B2%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.pdf).

A fresh independent search compilation of extracts from ГАКО files, updated on 9 August
2026, added a testable hypothesis. It names **Vasily Rodionov
Anpilogov**, for whom a family composition is noted within an approximate range of
1840–1870, **Mikhail Mironov Anpilogov**, a clerk (kantselyarist) with a service
record for 1820–1850, and **Lyubov Anpilogova**, a lieutenant's daughter. Vasily's
patronymic suggests that the non-commissioned officer's initial "R." might stand for
Rodion, and Vasily might be his son. But the private compilation does not publish an archival
reference, an exact date, a place or a kinship formula; so "Rodion
Anfilogov" remains a search hypothesis, not an established person.
The next targeted order is a full extract about Vasily Rodionov's family.
[Compilation of ГАКО extracts](https://forum.vgd.ru/9304/174610/0.htm).

Eleven years earlier still, two Senate descriptions name a specific
person: **the collegiate registrar Yakov Anfilogov**, killed near the village of
Bolshie Shchigry. The ruling in the case is dated 11 December 1811, the extract
from the journal 7 February 1812. Bolshie Shchigry later appears as a large
nest of Anpilogovs; so Yakov's heirs could link the early
Shchigry circle with the brothers of 1822 and the noble-status proceedings. So far
his patronymic and kinship are unknown. For Yakov a profile `P9678`, a Record
`PUB-RGIA-1330-23-143-YAKOV-ANFILOGOV-1811` and an event point in Bolshie
Shchigry have been created. Card for the land case:
`PUB-RGIA-1330-18-137-ANFILOGOV-BROTHERS-LAND-1822`.

The local archival duplicate has also been checked. The official inventory of **ГАКО, f. 1436,
Kursk Assembly of Noble Deputies** contains only 19 surviving files
for 1760–1864; there is no separate file of Anfilogovs or Anpilogovs in it.
This does not prove that local proceedings never existed, but it makes
the РГИА Senate files the main surviving complex.

The full sections "Names and meaning", the limit of proof and the reading order
have been entered in the card
`PUB-RGIA-1343-16-ANFILOGOV-NOBILITY-DOSSIERS` and the working lead
`LEAD-RGIA-1343-16-2345-ANFILOGOV-NOBILITY`. The four catalogue
headings themselves create no people and no routes; the only new named event
on the map relates to Yakov Anfilogov in Bolshie Shchigry.
[Main РГИА file](https://fgurgia.ru/object/2374618593),
[land case, part A](https://fgurgia.ru/object/5932449),
[Senate document on Yakov](https://fgurgia.ru/object/3884669),
[compilation of the Kursk genealogical book](https://goldarms.narod.ru/kursk.htm),
[post about the microfilm](https://forum.vgd.ru/2/60627/610.htm),
[official ГАКО inventory](https://archive.rkursk.ru/sites/default/files/Opisi/1_-_dorev/f1436.pdf).

### Mixed inventories of fond 210: the early outer dates jump over 1577

Two inventories that in the catalogue looked like a possible
refuge for lost Oryol lists have also been checked. The combined inventory **15/16/23** begins
in 1545 and includes heterogeneous scrolls (stolbtsy), among them some of the Pomestny desk.
However, the early unit no. 29 «Отрывки разрядов» ("Fragments of razryads") combines razryads of 1550–1553 and
1580–1582: there is no 1577 in its description.

Even more convincing is the structure of **inventory 21**, with outer dates 1512–1711.
On the first page of the inventory proper there stand in sequence: d. 1, a list of
participants in the Smolensk campaign of 1512; d. 2, a late copy of a razryad
book for 1537–1552; d. 3, a late copy of a razryad book for 1556–1561;
the next unit, d. 4, is already dated 1597. The lower limit of the
inventory is created by a few ancient books, not by a continuous run of files.

Thus no separate file for 1577 turned up in the two earliest
mixed inventories either. This does not prove the physical loss of every named
list: a sheet may have been inserted into a later bundle and not made it into a heading.
The practical conclusion is already reliable: these inventories should not be ordered wholesale just because of
the dates 1545 or 1512; they are worth returning to with a specific
archival reference. The result has been entered in the cards
`PUB-RGADA-F210-EARLY-OREL-CATALOG-AUDIT` and
`LEAD-210-6-1-OREL-NAMED-LISTS-1577`.
[Inventory 15/16/23](http://rgada.info/poisk/index2.php?str=210-opis_15,16,23),
[inventory 21](http://rgada.info/poisk/index2.php?str=210-opis_21),
[first page of the chronological series](http://rgada.info/opisi2/210-opis_21/0010.jpg).

### The full early corpus of desyatnyas: seven files and not one separate Oryol file

The official **inventory 4 of fond 210** has now been checked not from the viewer's
reduced pictures but from all 31 full-size archival images. Two
independent recognition passes and a page-by-page comparison gave one and the same
closed series of files before 1596:

- d. 17 — Kolomna, 1577, razbor desyatnya, money distribution and enrolment of
  noviki, 106 folios;
- d. 94 — Ryazhsk, 1579, money distribution, 80 folios;
- d. 221 — Epifan, 1585, enrolment of Cossacks as deti boyarskie, 13 folios;
- d. 13 — Pereslavl-Zalessky, 1590, enrolment and money distribution,
  8 folios;
- d. 95 — Ryazhsk, 1591, enrolment and money distribution, 156 folios;
- d. 222 — Epifan and Venyov, 1591, money distribution, 57 folios;
- d. 96 — Ryazhsk, 1594, muster, 53 folios.

The full published texts of all seven books have been checked for the forms Анфилог-,
Анпилог-, Онфилог-, Онпилог-, Ампилог- and Амфилог-, and also for the rare family
signature **Mikula — sons Vasily, Timofey and Karp**. Neither the target surname nor such
a trio is in them. This is an important negative result: the early part
of the specialised inventory has been exhausted entirely, so there is no need to repeat a general search
of it. The Oryol intake should be sought in lost books,
inserted lists, later ownership extracts and petitions about rewriting
service records.

Three full-size fragments of the official inventory are kept in the public card
`PUB-RGADA-F210-EARLY-OREL-CATALOG-AUDIT`; the card is now open as a Record and
explains the names, the method of checking and the limit of the negative conclusion. The map does not
change: absence of the surname from the corpus is not a migration event.

### The Kolomna desyatnya of 1577: a full name-by-name check gave no move to Oryol

The official inventory of fond 210 confirms the exact reference of the earliest file in
the specialised collection of desyatnyas: **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 4, d. 17**. This is
the Kolomna razbor desyatnya of 7085/1577, money distribution and pomestye
enrolment of noviki, 106 folios. The razbor was conducted by the boyar **Pyotr Vasilyevich
Morozov** and the dyak **Vasily Nizovtsov**. There is no further separate early Oryol
desyatnya in the inventory; Oryol is first directly included in a surviving enrolment
desyatnya only in 1628.

The full publication of the Kolomna desyatnya was compared with the owners'
headings of the Oryol book of 1594/95. In the original Kolomna series
the okladchiki, the vybor, the dvorovye and the gorodovye are distinguished — about 301 men in all.
Ten shared surname forms were found: **Vasilyev, Esipov, Zakharov, Kishkin,
Parfenov, Protasov, Sofonov, Sumorokov, Tishkov and Fedorov**. But in no pair
did surname and patronymic match at once; nor is there an exact three-part name.
Most of the forms are patronymic and could have arisen independently.

The control on the Sumorokovs is especially telling. In Kolomna in 1577 are named
**Mitya Vereshchagin syn Sumorokov**, **Ivashka Turabyev syn Sumorokov** and
the guarantor **Istoma Stepanov syn Sumorokov**. In Oryol in 1594/95 lived
**Istoma Petrov syn Sumorokov**, who received land after his brother Ivan. The given name and
surname match, but the patronymics differ: this is not the same man. A pair that arose in
the first machine comparison with **Neudacha Petrov syn Sumorokov**
turned out to be false: Neudacha is printed in a late Kolomna list of 1648,
placed in the commentary to the same publication, not in the desyatnya of 1577.

Consequently, Kolomna remains a possible part of the general Oka corridor, but
is not yet a proven source of the Oryol family. No route has been drawn on the map.
Card: `PUB-KOLOMNA-1577-OREL-1594-CROSSCHECK`.
[Kolomna desyatnya, part 1](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text1_1.php),
[part 2](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text1_2.php),
[official РГАДА inventory](http://rgada.info/poisk/index2.php?str=210-opis_4).

### Epifan, 1585: four possible pairs of brothers with Oryol at once

The full list of Epifan proved far more substantive. Its heading says outright
that this is «десятня детей боярских епифанцов, которые верстаны ис казаков» ("the desyatnya of the Epifan deti boyarskie who were enrolled from the Cossacks")
by the boyar Prince **Dmitry Ivanovich Khvorostinin** and the dyak **Vasily
Sherapov** in the year 7093. The summary of the publication is more precise still: in **1585**
**300 men** from the Epifan Cossacks were enrolled as deti boyarskie. Seventy
received pomestye oklads of 40 chetverti each, the remaining 230 of 30.

The source thus shows the social mechanism itself: the Cossack population
of a new frontier town did not vanish and was not replaced by a ready-made nobility, but
was turned by one razbor into pomestye-holding deti boyarskie. For the first Oryol men
this is an especially important parallel: the verdict of 1577 also describes the local
Oryol Cossacks with small pomestyes and the selection of fit men.

The full 300 Epifan names were compared with the owners' headings of
the Oryol survey book of 1594/95. Four pairs were obtained in which
both patronymic and family name match, but the given
names differ:

- **Ivanko Yuryev syn Vorobyev**, no. 15 in Epifan — **Fetko Yuryev syn
  Vorobyev** in the Oryol clearing of Detkil;

- **Fomka Kuzmin syn Ivanov**, no. 205 — **Maksim Kuzmin syn Ivanov** on
  an Oryol zaimishche by the Oka;

- **Stepanko Vasilyev syn Koptev**, no. 151 — **Foma Vasilyev syn Koptev** in
  the Oryol hamlet of Bruseni;

- **Okinsha Ivanov syn Novikov**, no. 175 — **Pavel Ivanov syn Novikov** in
  the Oryol hamlet of Smorodichnaya.

Each formula is compatible with full brothers: different given names, but one
named father and one family element. However, the word «брат» ("brother") has not yet been found in any of these
pairs, so the kinship cannot be considered established.

**Names and meaning.** Ivanko is Ivan, Fetko a colloquial form of Fyodor, Fomka is
Foma, Stepanko is Stepan. Okinsha is a rare everyday form, probably linked to
the calendar name Akinf/Iakinf or a similar form. What matters is not modernising
the names but the identical documentary construction: for example, both Koptevs are called
«Васильев сын Коптев» ("Vasilyev syn Koptev"), that is, each names Vasily as his father and belongs to
the Koptevs.

The Koptev pair is especially strong. **Stepanko Vasilyevich Koptev** in 1585
still belonged to the Epifan Cossack circle, which was then enrolled as deti
boyarskie. **Foma Vasilyevich Koptev** already held land at Oryol in 1594/95
and under the same full name is recorded among the Oryol noviki of 1596. Earlier,
Timofey Grigoryevich Koptev had been found in the Tula watch service of 1577 and
an old Tula pomestye, Artemy Grigoryevich in Mtsensk and Ivan Grigoryevich at
Oryol. So what we have now is not the direct line of a single migrant but a possible
family network **Tula — Mtsensk — Epifan — Oryol**.

The Novikovs give another important detail. Of the Oryol **Pavel Ivanovich Novikov**
the scribe says «отца его поместье» ("his father's pomestye"). So Pavel's father Ivan Novikov was connected
with Oryol land before 1594/95. If the Epifan man Okinsha really
was Pavel's brother, the family did not "come from Epifan" as a whole: its sons were already
distributed between two towns. This is exactly the model of family homesteads and
regulated separation of brothers that the case of the Oryol
Cossacks Logachev of 1592 later shows directly.

A line dangerous for surname searching was checked separately: **Persha Onanyin syn
Levontyeva**, no. 125. Here «Онаньин» stands in the place of the patronymic — Persha was the son of
Onanya — and the family element of the entry is **Levontyev**. This is not an Epifan
man surnamed Onanyin and no basis for linking him with Anpilog Ivanov syn
Onanyin of Oryol district.

As a control, the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1579 was analysed in the same way: in its 461
service blocks there is not a single match with Oryol on both patronymic and
family element. So the four Epifan pairs are noticeably more substantive than the general
surname background.

The repeat Epifan desyatnya of **30 October 1591** made it possible to check
the fate of the four men themselves. **Ivanko Yuryev syn Vorobyev** continued to serve
at Epifan under no. 10; Andrey Vlasov, Mitrokha Koshkarov,
Semyon Medvedev and Moisey Efanov stood surety for him. **Okinsha Ivanov syn Novikov** remained there
too, under no. 68; his guarantors were Maksim Uvarov, Moisey Efanov, Sidor Larin
and **Sava Koptev**. **Stepanko Vasilyev syn Koptev**, under no. 302, is marked with one
word: «умре» ("died"). **Fomka Kuzmin syn Ivanov** is in the editor's list of 39
men of 1585 who are no longer in the desyatnya of 1591; no reason is given.

This substantially changes the meaning of the comparison. Ivanko and Okinsha are certainly not
the Oryol Fetko and Pavel, and Stepanko could not have moved to Oryol later at all.
Consequently, if identical formulas really link the families, what we have is
not four personal moves but possible **brothers or branches divided
between Epifan and Oryol**. Fomka's absence does not permit declaring him
a migrant: a man could die, be taken captive, become unfit, fail to
come to the distribution or drop out of the list for another reason.

A full comparison of the 1591 list added two more parallels. The Epifan
**Sava Ivanov syn Yurov** and the Oryol **Nikifor Ivanov syn Yurov** are compatible with
brotherhood. The eight-year-old **Sukhanko Ivanov syn Grigoryev** formally fits
the Oryol Maksim and Ignaty Ivanovich Grigoryev, but the name Grigoryev is
too broad and the age gap large; this is a weak hypothesis.

The structure of the sureties also matters. Sava Koptev stood surety for Okinsha Novikov,
that is, two of the family groups found belonged to one Epifan service circle.
Another Epifan man, **Ivan Stepanov syn Koptev**, later had an unusual
guarantor from another town — the Tula guba elder (gubnoy starosta) Andrey Maslov. These entries
strengthen the picture of an inter-town Koptev network, but still do not name
parents and brothers.

The archival reference is kept exactly in the form of the publication: **Moscow Archive of
the Ministry of Justice, desyatnya book no. 221, printed inventory no. 83** for 1585
and **book no. 222, printed inventory no. 84** for 1591. The modern РГАДА references
have not yet been established and are not substituted by guesswork. Card:
PUB-EPIFAN-1585-OREL-1594-FAMILY-CROSSCHECK. No route has been drawn on the map:
there is no direct move, and the Koptevs, Novikovs, Vorobyevs and Ivanovs do not belong to
the surname group under study.
[Epifan desyatnya of 1585](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text4.php),
[Epifan desyatnya of 1591](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text5.php).

### Ryazhsk, 1591: direct transferees from Ryazan and a trace of the Bazdyrevs

Unlike the Oryol survey book, the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1591 is
a service book of money distribution and razbor. So it preserves exactly the
