formula one would like to find for the first Oryol men. Six entries
are grouped by the editor with a direct explanation: they **«переписались на службу с Рязани
с Каменскаго и Пехлецкаго станов»** ("transferred their service from Ryazan, from the Kamensky and Pekhletsky stans").

All the men and their former places are named:

- **Ivan Petrov syn Lunin**, no. 507 — served from Ryazan in the Kamensky stan;

- **Fyodor Ivanov Potulov**, no. 508 — from the same place;

- **Grigory Petrov syn Lunin**, no. 509 — from the same place;

- **Petrusha Nikiforov syn Bazdyrev, Sviridov tozh** ("also called Sviridov"), no. 518 — served from
  Ryazan in the Pekhletsky stan; at Ryazhsk he is recorded among the siege men with an oklad of
  40 chetverti;

- **Grisha Ivanov syn Mosolov**, no. 522 — from the Kamensky stan;

- **Fedka Alekseev syn Buzheninov**, no. 529 — served from Ryazan in the Kamensky
  stan from 7096/1587–1588.

This is not a reconstruction and not a surname match but a documented named
transfer. The desyatnya shows that the chancery really did use exact
formulas of former service: the man, the old town, the stan, sometimes the year. The absence of
such words in the Oryol book is now explained above all by the type of source:
the Oryol book described land, the Ryazhsk one a service razbor.

#### Ryazhsk, 1594: the same group grew to twelve men

The full facsimile of the next Ryazhsk desyatnya gave a second, even stronger
check of the mechanism. In March 1594 a separate rubric, nos. 635–646, reports
that the okladchiki said of these Ryazhsk men **«что они служат с Рязани»** ("that they serve from Ryazan"). In the summary
analysis of the book V. N. Storozhev names them directly: **«переписавшихся на службу
с Рязани — двенадцать человек»** ("twelve men who transferred their service from Ryazan").

Named are Prince Grigory Fyodorovich Dulov; Ivan and Grigory Petrovich Lunin;
Fyodor and Konstantin Ivanovich Potulov; Grisha Ivanovich Mosolov; Ivashko
Alekseevich Buzheninov; Dmitry Mikhaylovich and Pyotr Sidorovich Sozonov; the brothers
Bogdashko and Fedka Nikiforovich Bykovsky; Khrenka Mikhaylovich Dubovitsky.
The entries preserve oklads from 400 to 100 chetverti, the Kamensky stan and a separate
ataman service.

Four men indisputably repeat the transferees of 1591: the two Lunin brothers,
Fyodor Potulov and Grisha Mosolov. Konstantin Potulov, with the same father Ivan,
forms a probable pair of brothers with Fyodor, and Bogdashko and Fedka are directly called
Nikifor's sons, Bykovsky. An important answer about the structure of the source has been obtained:
the note of the former town could be kept more than once, and the transferring group
remained distinguishable across several successive desyatnyas together with
its family ties.

For Anfilogovs there is no direct match. None of the twelve corresponds to
the seven Oryol paternal lines. The Sozonovs merely sound like Okinsha Sozonov
syn Sotnikov of Oryol, but the different second name excludes identity.
So the map again does not change. On the other hand, we now know what quality
the lost Oryol service list could have had: the former town, the land stan,
the oklad, repetition across years and brothers in one rubric. Photocopies of printed
pp. 354–355 and the full name-by-name analysis have been added to the card
`PUB-RYAZHSK-1591-OREL-1594-BAZDYREV-NETWORK`.

#### Petrusha Bazdyrev and a possible father at Oryol

The most important name is **Petrusha Nikiforov syn Bazdyrev, Sviridov tozh**.
The Bazdyrevs are not a random common surname: in Oryol district it was from this name that
the hamlet took its name where in 1594/95 Belyay and Osip Vasilyevich
Anfilogov and the three Mikulich Anfilogov brothers held land.

In the same hamlet the scribe recorded **Nikifor Oksyonov syn Bazdyrev**. He held
«отца его поместье» ("his father's pomestye"), that is, the land had formerly belonged to his father **Oksyon
Bazdyrev**. This gives a proper three-generation construction:

- Oksyon Bazdyrev — former holder of the Oryol pomestye;

- his son Nikifor Oksyonovich Bazdyrev — Oryol pomeshchik of 1594/95;

- a possible son of Nikifor — Petrusha Nikiforovich Bazdyrev, who served first
  from Ryazan in the Pekhletsky stan, then transferred to Ryazhsk.

The rare surname, the name of the named father and the chronology all match. So this is
the strongest hypothesis found of kinship between a man with a documented
former town and a landholder from the immediate Oryol neighbourhood
cluster. But the document does not say "Petrusha, son of the Oryol Nikifor", so
the link cannot yet be put down as proven.

There is a fundamental caveat about direction. Even if Pyotr turns out to be the son of
the Oryol Nikifor, this is not necessarily a route Ryazan — Oryol. Nikifor held
**his father's Oryol pomestye**, whereas Pyotr served from Ryazan and moved
from there to Ryazhsk. Early dispersal from Oryol, simultaneous service of family
members in different towns, or a more complex sequence are all possible. What has been found exactly is
Pyotr's route **Ryazan — Ryazhsk**, not his father's route.

The double name «Баздырев, Свиридов тож» ("Bazdyrev, also called Sviridov") also calls for caution. At
Ryazhsk there was a large circle of Sviridovs. One form could be the family
name, the other a nickname or membership of another branch. Without
a surety or land record the order in which the surnames arose cannot be established.

#### A new find in an open scan: Pyotr's lot in Myakishevo

A check of the full facsimile of the payment book of the Pekhletsky stan corrected the earlier
negative result. On printed p. 134 it is recorded:

> «Въ той же д. жеребей за Петрушкою за Микифоровымъ сыномъ Свиридова; на
> его жеребей сошного письма въ живущемъ и въ пустѣ пол-полчети сохи, да не
> дошло 5 ч. пашни; а платити ему зъ живущего съ 3 ч.»

> *Translation:* "In the same hamlet, a lot held by Petrushka, Mikifor's son, Sviridov; on
> his lot, by sokha assessment, inhabited and empty, a half of a half-quarter of a sokha, short by
> 5 chetverti of ploughland; and he is to pay on the inhabited part for 3 chetverti."

The previous page expands the abbreviation "in the same hamlet": it is the hamlet of
**Myakishevo on the Prudishchi, the Sofyevskie selishcha**. The name Petrusha, the father Nikifor,
the name Sviridov and the Pekhletsky stan all match at once with Pyotr
Nikiforovich Bazdyrev, **«Свиридовым тож»** ("also called Sviridov"), of the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1591.
The combination of features makes the identification highly probable, although the payment entry
contains no separate formula "the same man" and the byname Bazdyrev is
omitted in it.

This changes the biographical model. Pyotr did not simply vanish after transferring his
service to Ryazhsk: the book of 1594–1597 continues to record a Ryazan
land lot in his name. What we have is most probably not a simple final
move but a distribution of service and holdings between neighbouring town
corporations. At the same time the find shows a methodological trap: a search
only for the form **Bazdyrev** gave a false zero, because the second source knows
the man only as **Sviridov**.

Open photocopies of printed pp. 133–134 of the 1898 edition have been kept;
the transcription and the assessment of identity have been added to the card
`PUB-RYAZHSK-1591-OREL-1594-BAZDYREV-NETWORK`. No profile of Pyotr and no point on
the family map are created: he belongs to the neighbourhood, not to the surname group
under study, and the Bazdyrevs' route to Oryol is still unproven.

In the Ryazhsk desyatnyas of 1594 and 1597 Pyotr Nikiforovich and the form Bazdyrev are no longer
found. This is not proof of departure to Oryol: he may have died, been taken captive,
changed his service or not been entered in a particular list.

#### The Ryazhsk desyatnya revealed the recruitment mechanism itself — and two full namesakes

A repeat check of the paternal pairs of the Oryol core yielded not ancestors but an important
check on method. Two full pairs matching Oryol ones turned up at once
in the Ryazhsk corpus:

- **Belyay Vasilyev syn Korostelev** stood surety several times for servicemen
  in the desyatnya of 1591. In the March desyatnya of 1594 he stands under no. 687 in
  the section of the dead, and the book of 1597 specifies that he died in the year 7101 —
  between 1 September 1592 and 31 August 1593. This is a different man and a different
  family line, not the Oryol Belyay Onfilogov;

- **Osipko Vasilyev syn Pokhotinskoy** is recorded in March 1594 under no. 461
  among the «атаманов рязских Васильева прибору Биркина» ("Ryazhsk atamans recruited by Vasily Birkin"). These atamans had earlier
  been taken into service but not yet enrolled with the sovereign's pomestyes; Trubetskoy and
  the dyak Sapun Avramov gave Osip 100 chetverti. In 1597 he is repeated under
  no. 524 with the same pomestye oklad and for the first time received four roubles of money
  pay.

Osip's entry is especially valuable not as a candidate but as a model of recruitment almost
contemporary with Oryol: **Vasily Birkin's recruitment (pribor) around 1591/92 → pomestye
enrolment in 1594 → money enrolment in 1597**. Exactly this kind of
sequence may have existed for early Oryol too, but its razbor
desyatnya has not survived or has not yet been found. At the same time Korostelev and
Pokhotinskoy prove that even a rare name with a matching patronymic is not
a sufficient family signature. For the Oryol Belyay and Osip what remains distinctive is
the whole complex: the surname form, Bazdyrevo, the neighbours, the former holder of the land and
the service sequence. No profiles or map points are created for the unrelated Ryazhsk
namesakes.

#### An exact Sofon — Timofey pair turned up in Shatsk district

In an extract of 1712 from the survey book of Fyodor Chebotov for 1623 one more
full namesake of an Oryol paternal line has been found. Among the 151 court
peasant households of the hamlet of **Temeshevo on the Tsna**, on fol. 101, under no. 146 in
the transcriber's modern numbering, stands **«Софонко Тимофеев сын»** ("Sofonko, Timofey's son").
The reference has been established exactly: **SPbII РАН, coll. 115, op. 1, d. 173, fol. 101** —
a file of 1712 on the survey of the Naryshkins' lands, containing an extract from the Shatsk
book of 1623. In the scholarly literature the parallel complex is also cited
as РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 4, d. 6034.

The full open PDF of issue 37 of the *Proceedings of the Tambov Scholarly Archival
Commission* for 1893 has been checked against the images. On p. 77 there is indeed
printed the economic entry «Сельцо Темешево на реке на Цне» ("The hamlet of Temeshevo on the river Tsna"), but
the publisher omitted the household list of 151 names. A photocopy of this page
has been kept, but it confirms the place and the entry, not the name itself. The exact
name line is for now being checked against an open folio-by-folio transcription of fol. 101;
final verification needs an image of this SPbII РАН folio.

The chronology allows identity: the Oryol Sofon was an adult in 1594/95,
and the Shatsk entry is 28 years later. But the social and land picture
contradicts it. At Temeshevo a court peasant without a surname is named, whereas
around 1625 the Oryol register still knows **«Сафоново поместье
Тимофеева сына Анфилогова»** ("the pomestye of Safon, Timofey's son, Anfilogov"). The latter formula might preserve the name of a former
holder and does not prove that he was alive, but without a record of loss of the pomestye,
change of status and a move to Shatsk the men cannot be joined. At present this is an exact
late namesake or a weak biographical candidate. He gets a separate
Record, but no profile and no route on the map.

#### Bazdyrevs were also in the Dedilovo Cossack layer

The pripravochnaya (reference) book of Dedilovo district for 1588/89 adds **Olfim
Bazdyrev**. He is recorded among the Cossacks of Lutyuva's hundred, who along the upper reaches of
the Shivoron below the Cheremkhov moss were each given five chetverti of fallow and wild steppe
«за послугу» ("for service"). The printed entry is on p. 1271.

So the surname turns up at the same time:

- in a Cossack hundred of Dedilovo;

- in the Ryazan Pekhletsky stan and among the transferees to Ryazhsk;

- in the Oryol pomestye nest below the Minov forest.

This is no longer a single similar surname but a real Upper Oka service
network.

#### Four more Ryazhsk — Oryol family bridges

A comparison of the 580 three-part names of the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1591 with the Oryol
landholders revealed four more groups sharing a patronymic:

- the Ryazhsk **Ondryusha and Stepanko, Ivan's sons, Ovdokimov** — the Oryol
  **Ivan and Gavrila, Ivan's sons, Ovdokimov**;

- the Ryazhsk **Onashka Vasilyev syn Sviridov** — the Oryol **Kurdyum Vasilyev
  syn Sviridov**;

- the Ryazhsk **Senka Ivanov syn Novikov** — the Oryol **Pavel Ivanov syn
  Novikov**;

- the Ryazhsk **Rodka Dmitreev syn Pakhomov** — the Oryol **Danilo Dmitreev syn
  Pakhomov**.

No pair is called brothers, but the Novikovs form an especially clear
three-town construction. **Okinsha Ivanov syn Novikov** continued to serve at
Epifan in 1591, **Senka Ivanov syn Novikov** is consistently recorded at
Ryazhsk in 1591, 1594 and 1597, and **Pavel Ivanov syn Novikov** held his father's pomestye at
Oryol. Three different men at the same time named one
father, Ivan, and bore one surname. This may be a family of three brothers
divided between Epifan, Ryazhsk and Oryol.

#### What this changes for Anfilogovs

The Ryazhsk books contain no Anfilogovs or their variants. Yet for the first time
a former town has been found for a member of **the family that gave its name to the hamlet of
Bazdyrevo** itself. So Ryazan district, especially **the Pekhletsky stan**, becomes
a targeted direction of search for Anfilogovs, Trunovs, Nemtsovs and Krivolapovs,
and not just another point on the general map of the Upper Oka.

The evidential limit remains strict:

- Pyotr Bazdyrev really did move from Ryazan to Ryazhsk;

- his kinship with the Oryol Nikifor Bazdyrev is very plausible, but not
  proven;

- a move of the Bazdyrevs or Anfilogovs from Ryazan to Oryol has not yet been found.

Card: PUB-RYAZHSK-1591-OREL-1594-BAZDYREV-NETWORK. No route has been drawn on the family
map: the documented move belongs to an unrelated
surname, and movement to Oryol remains a hypothesis.
[Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1591, part 1](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text8_1.php),
[part 2](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text8_2.php),
[Ryazhsk 1594](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text9.php),
[Ryazhsk 1597, part 1](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text10_1.php),
[part 2](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text10_2.php).

#### Pavel Terentyev syn Ievlev: the strongest candidate for a pre-Oryol biography

In the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1591, twice, in the surety groups at no. 142 and no. 290,
**Pavel Terentyev syn Ievlev** is named. His name and patronymic match to the letter
the Oryol novik of 1596 **Pavel Terentyev syn Anfilogov**. This is
the closest personal match found in the full early corpus of
desyatnyas.

Both entries have been checked not only against the online publication but also against the scan of the 1891
volume. On printed **p. 272** Pavel stands among the guarantors for the service of Shestachko
Ivanov syn Stroilov (entry no. 142); on **p. 294**, among the guarantors for
Posnik, also called Poznyak, Savin syn Goltsov (entry no. 290). Two independent
sureties matter more than a single mention: by 1591 this is an adult Ryazhsk
serviceman trusted to answer for the service of other deti boyarskie.
Photocopies of both pages are kept in the card of the Oryol Pavel.

There is no full identification yet. The Ryazhsk line gives a different third name —
**Ievlev** — does not report a move to Oryol and does not give Anfilogov as a second
byname. But the exact form of Pavel is not repeated in the Ryazhsk desyatnyas of 1594 and
1597, and five years later a Pavel with the same name and
patronymic appears at Oryol. This gives a coherent, though unproven, sequence: Ryazhsk
1591 → absence from the two subsequent Ryazhsk snapshots → Oryol 1596.

The meaning of the word **novik** has been substantially revised. Earlier the Ryazhsk candidacy
was weakened by the assumption that a guarantor who had served before could not again be
a novik. But the Oryol rubric itself contains a control: Foma Vasilyev syn Koptev
already had land according to the survey book of 1594/95 and yet is called a novik in
the enrolment of 1596. Consequently, "novik" describes a man's position in
a particular enrolment and does not guarantee that he had never served or
held land before. Pavel's early Ryazhsk service is no longer an automatic
objection to identity.

Entry no. 299 has been checked separately, now against a photocopy of printed p. 296: «Панка
Орбеев, а Терентьев сын Иевлев тожъ» ("Panka Orbeev, also Terentyev syn Ievlev"). This is not an OCR error — the word **Терентьев**
really is printed. A complete check of all fourteen constructions with
«тож/тоже» ("also") in the book revealed direct parallels of a double father's name:
no. 110, «Фетка Матвеев сын, а Тимофеев тож, Облов» ("Fetka Matveev syn, also Timofeev, Oblov"); no. 68,
«Юшко Семенов сын Ретюнской, а Селиванов сын тож» ("Yushko Semenov syn Retyunskoy, also Selivanov syn"). So Orbey/Arbey and Terenty are most
probably two names of Panka's father, and Ievlev the family name. This substantially
strengthens brotherhood with Pavel Terentyev syn Ievlev. In 1594 and 1597
Panka continues to serve at Ryazhsk as **Panka Arbeev syn Ievlev**, so
he cannot be the Oryol Pavel himself. There is still no direct formula "brothers", no shared pomestye
and no inheritance; the kinship remains a strong hypothesis.

An early namesake candidate has been checked separately: **Trenka Semyonov syn Ievleva**,
no. 362 of the desyatnya of 1579. Printed pp. 244–245 place him under the heading
of the dead or killed after whom «не осталось жон и детей, а поместья лежат
пусты» ("no wives and children remained, and the pomestyes lie empty"). So he cannot automatically be declared the father with two names,
Orbey/Terenty. Adult sons who had already set up on their own might theoretically not
be counted as remaining with the pomestye, but the source does not say so, and
an empty pomestye shows no inheritance by Pavel or Panka.

The possibility of a move together with the surety circle has been checked. Pavel's two entries
name 23 other Ryazhsk servicemen. None of them recurs in the full
Oryol survey book of 1594/95 as the same man by the combination of name,
father and third name. Other Goltsovs and Pakhomovs occur at Oryol, but not
the Ryazhsk **Sofon Ivanovich, Poznyak Savinovich, Shestoy Vasilyevich and Pyotr
Menshovich Goltsov** nor **Sava Grigoryevich Pakhomov**. There is no sign of a group
move; this does not exclude Pavel's individual path through kinship or a new
enrolment.

The Ryazhsk desyatnya of **1579** confirms that Ievlev is a stable local
family name: **Martynko Grigoryev syn Ievlev** is recorded under no. 298 among the foot
servicemen from the land, and **Trenka Semyonov syn Ievleva** under no. 362 among the dead
or killed, after whom no wives and children remained. The latter line does not
allow Terenty to be declared Pavel's father: an adult son living on his own might theoretically
not be counted as an heir remaining with the pomestye, but the source does not
say so.

Two false paths sharing a patronymic have also been checked. At Tula, Terenty Rataev syn
Rostopchin of 1587–1589 remains within the Rostopchin family: next to him are named his father
Ratay, his brother Besson, his nephew Aleksandr, and in the Tula desyatnya of 1596,
Belyanin Terentyev syn Rostopchin. At Oryol in 1594/95 Zakhar Terentyev syn
Bobynin held the pomestye of his brother Bogdan, taken captive in 1591. Both lines
are explained by their own family names and are not for now attached to Pavel Anfilogov.

The final status has been raised from "weakened match" to **the strongest
personal candidate for a pre-Oryol biography of Pavel Anfilogov**. This is still
not a proven identity. What is needed is a formula «Иевлев, Анфилогов тож» ("Ievlev, also Anfilogov"), a record of
departure or transfer from Ryazhsk, shared land, inheritance or a recurring
kin-based surety circle. Until such a find no separate profile is created for Pavel Ievlev,
and no Ryazhsk — Oryol route is drawn on the map.

[Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1579](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text7.htm).
[Open scan of the 1891 volume](https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=47506922).

#### The payment book of the Pekhletsky stan confirmed four links

The next check was made not against a desyatnya but against the images of the full
edition of the **payment books of the towns of Ryazan, Pronsk and Ryazhsk and of the
Okologorodny, Kamensky and Pekhletsky stans, by the survey and measurement of Tretyak
Grigoryevich Velyaminov and his colleagues, 1594–1597**. In the edition of the Ryazan
Scholarly Archival Commission this complex occupies pp. 1–156, and the
Pekhletsky stan proper pp. 94–156.

The check linked four names of the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1591 with specific
Pekhletsky pomestyes:

- **Petrushka Mikiforov syn Sviridova**, p. 134, held a lot in the hamlet of
  **Myakishevo**; the name, patronymic, second name and the Pekhletsky stan make
  his identification with Pyotr Nikiforovich Bazdyrev, «Свиридовым тож» ("also called Sviridov"),
  highly probable;

- **Radko Dmitreev syn Pakhomov**, p. 125, held a lot in the hamlet **na
  Rogu in the Tolbuzovskoe selishche**; his share came to 20 chetverti
  of inhabited and empty ploughland;

- **Senka Ivanov syn Novikov**, p. 132, held a lot in the hamlet of
  **Novikova on the Istobensky verkh below the navolok**;

- **Onashka Vasilyev syn Sviridov**, p. 136, held a lot in the hamlet of
  **Knyazhaya in the Sobino selishche**; payment was reckoned on four chetverti of inhabited
  ploughland.

The readings have been checked directly against the page images, not only against OCR.
Thus the desyatnya reports their service, and the payment book independently
shows their land. These are not four random similar names but established
Ryazhsk–Pekhletsky people of the late 16th century.

The Oryol correspondences become more substantive as a result, but do not change
their evidential status. The Oryol **Danilo Dmitrievich Pakhomov, Pavel
Ivanovich Novikov and Kurdyum Vasilyevich Sviridov** may have been brothers of three of the
named Pekhletsky holders. No book, however, uses the word
«брат» ("brother") or reports a move to Oryol.

The change of name is especially important. The form **Bazdyrev** was not
found in the payment book, but Pyotr was found under the second form from the desyatnya —
**Sviridov**. This shows how one and the same man could vanish from a search by the main
surname while remaining in the book under another byname. Even so, this entry does not
prove a move to Oryol: it continues his Pekhletsky land connection.

Likewise, **Анфилог, Анпилог, Онфилог, Ампилог and Амфилог** were not found.
Panfilov and Panfilovskoe occurred, but that is a different name series. The zero
result was obtained by full recognition of the old printed text, not by manual
letter-by-letter proofreading of all 156 pages, so it is strong but not absolute.

After this a second public copy of the same scan was found and **the whole volume up to
p. 416** was checked. The table of contents shows that the earlier range is
followed by the payment books of Zaraysk and six stans (pp. 157–254), the pripravochnye
books of Pronsk and the Kamensky stan (pp. 255–370), then of Ryazan and
the Okologorodny stan (pp. 371–416). A full machine search of the extended
corpus also gave no forms **Анфилог, Анпилог, Онфилог, Онпилог, Ампилог,
Амфилог**, and in the added sections revealed no reliable Bazdyrevs,
Krivolapovs, Trunovs or Nemtsovs. This is a useful narrowing of the published
corpus of 1594–1598, but no substitute for the lost sovereign's books
of the 1560s–1570s. The title page and table of contents were checked against the images; the remaining
pages went through search recognition, not manual letter-by-letter proofreading.

Conclusion: the Pekhletsky stan remains a targeted zone of family parallels with Oryol,
but has not yet yielded a direct source for the Oryol core or Anfilogovs.
The family map does not change: all the confirmed movements and land
points here belong to unrelated surnames.
[Digital copy of the edition and table of contents](https://62info.ru/history/node/10453),
[second public copy of the full volume](https://62info.ru/history/node/10609),
[scan of volume I, issue 1](https://yadi.sk/d/vyqQMlNG1wi44).

### Before 1591: a real mechanism of transfer and hereditary recruitment found

A check of the chronology of the Ryazan survey books changed the very framing of the search.
The official РГАДА catalogue states that as early as **the 1560s** a separate
corporation of servicemen centred on Ryazhsk existed in the Pekhletsky
stan. Yet not a single original survey book of Ryazan district of the 16th century
has survived in full. The books especially important to us, those of **Mikhail Ozerov and the podyachy
Krestyanin Petrov of 1573/74** for the Kamensky and Pekhletsky stans, are known
only from extracts and later references.

However, one genuine act predates the first large Oryol roll.
**On 13 July 1571** the Ryazan scribes Zakhary Trakhaniotov and Matvey Chemodanov
gave **Fyodor Fyodorovich Lezhnev** and **Elizar Semyonovich Golitsyn** 100
chetverti in the hamlet of Ukhorskaya Slobodka in the Kamensky stan. The formula of transfer
is literal: the new holding «**дано им против старого их поместья, что за ними
было в Пехлецком стану в деревне в Серезеве**» ("was given to them in exchange for their old pomestye, which they held in the Pekhletsky stan in the hamlet of Serezevo"). The land had become free after
Elizar Timofeevich Karamyshev: he was killed by the Tatars, and no wife or children remained.
Reference of the original: **РГАДА, f. 1209, stolbtsy for Ryazan, no. 854/41287, fol. 29**;
two black-wax seals survive on the extract. Publication: АСЗ, vol. II, no. 96.

This is not a move to Oryol, but for the first time a document has been found of almost the right time and
the right type: it names at once the servicemen, the old pomestye,
the new pomestye and the reason the land became free. This is exactly what
the lost extract of the first Oryol pomestye grant must have looked like.

A charter of **1587** restores the chancery chain even more fully. For
the allotment to Bogdan Obryutin syn Skobeev of 50 chetverti in the hamlet of Nazemnaya
in the Pekhletsky stan, an extract from the books of Mikhail Ozerov was sent **«слово в слово»** ("word for word")
to the Ryazhsk zaseka steward (zasechny prikazchik) Ivan Tyutchev. The steward was ordered
to record the homesteads, people, ploughland, hay, forest and appurtenances, and to send the new book
immediately to Moscow, to the Pomestny Prikaz. Original:
**РГАДА, f. 1209, stolbtsy for Ryazhsk, no. 36/42020**; АСЗ, vol. III, no. 389.
No. 389 itself is a charter of August 1587, not a surviving leaf of the book
of 1573/74. It names the former holder who died childless,
**Andrey Ivanov syn Elchin**, the co-holder of another part of Nazemnaya,
**Vasily Bolshov**, and the new recipient, **Bogdan Obryutin syn Skobeev**.
The text and note are printed on pp. 314–315 of vol. III; checked against
[images 315](https://djvu.online/jpg1/X/S/h/XShJZdkt8XXun/315.webp) and
[316](https://djvu.online/jpg1/X/S/h/XShJZdkt8XXun/316.webp) of the digital copy.
Thus the expected chain for Oryol looks like this:

`старая писцовая выпись → государева грамота → местный отвод → новая книга → Поместный приказ`.

(That is: old survey-book extract → sovereign's charter → local allotment → new book → Pomestny Prikaz.)

The fire in the Pomestny Prikaz in 1626 may have destroyed precisely the last,
central end of this chain. So one must look not only for an "Oryol
desyatnya", but also for late vvoznye charters, lawsuits and lists quoting the earlier
separate (otdelnaya) book.

Finally, a charter of **16 June 1590** shows a hereditary way of
replenishing the service corporation. After the death of Boris Ekimov his 150
chetverti were confirmed to his sons — Ryumka aged 11, Ivan aged 7 and Ignat aged 3 —
with an explicit condition: when each turned fifteen, he was to
begin the sovereign's service from his father's pomestye. Reference:
**РГАДА, f. 1209, stolbtsy for Ryazan, no. 133/40969**; АСЗ, vol. I, no. 90.

For Oryol this is fundamental: the people of the 1594/95 book may have been not only
migrants of the first intake but already **a second generation**, grown up in the families
of the settlers of 1566–1571. The Mikulich Anfilogov brothers, who held their father's
pomestye, fit this model especially well, although the source does not give the former town of their
father Mikula.

In the full text of the four volumes of АСЗ no Oryol otdelnaya extract or formula of
a man's transfer to Oryol has been found. The extracts from Mikhail Ozerov's books likewise
do not name Anfilogovs, but only four local fragments have come down, so
this is not a negative check of the whole book.

A bibliographical re-check against the official РГАДА catalogue refined this
count. Four local holdings do not mean four acts: Ozerov's dozornaya (inspection) book
itself is attested by exactly three references — **АСЗ, vol. I, no. 293; vol.
III, nos. 259 and 389**. АСЗ I no. 90 describes the inheritance of a pomestye in 1590 and
remains important for the mechanism of replenishing the service, but it does not mention Ozerov's book.
So the next search is narrowed to three real documentary
chains: the full texts, editorial notes, versos and
neighbouring archival leaves of nos. 293, 259 and 389 must be checked, rather than searching for a non-existent fourth
attestation.

At the same time the Ryazan direction has an independent onomastic background. In an act
of 1483–1503 an adult prince's man **Antipa Anfilogov syn** is named,
that is, the son of an Anfilog who lived as early as the 15th century; in an inquest (obysk) of the 1530s
the grand prince's peasant **Anfilog Ivanov syn Konstyantinova** takes part directly.
Both appeared in the Ryazan land before Oryol was founded. This strengthens
the plausibility of the Ryazan direction for the name itself, but does not turn
a patronymic and a personal name into a hereditary surname and does not prove their kinship with
Oryol Anfilogovs.

A full check of the open name indexes to all four volumes of A. I. Tsepkov's
Compendium has now outlined the limit of this trace. The editor of vol. III
normalised the line as **«Анпилог (Анфилог) Иванов сын Константинова,
крестьянин»** ("Anpilog (Anfilog) Ivanov syn Konstantinova, peasant"). For the stems Анфил-, Анпил-, Онфил-, Онпил-, Амфил- and Ампил-
this is the only target entry in the whole four-volume index. In volumes I,
II and IV there are no other bearers of the series; in vol. III there is no second such man
and no named sons with the patronymic Anfilogov/Anpilogov. Consequently,
the Ryazan candidate is confirmed as a separate early individual, but in the published corpus
he did not form a visible family chain. This reduces the weight of the simple
version "the ready-made family of Anfilog Konstantinov moved to Oryol", without
excluding another mechanism: his sons may have been recorded under a new name or
enrolled into service.

A geographical check refined the addresses of this search. Ruposovo in the act is not
a place that vanished without trace but present-day **Rubtsovo in Ryazan district**:
the Ryazan toponymic dictionary directly traces the chain Ruposovo → Rubtsovo.
It was at Ruposovo that the sovereign's inquest took place, so Anfilog's card now
has a verified map point in present-day Rubtsovo. Podkletnoe also
turned out to be a real separate settlement, not merely the word «подклетное» ("podkletnoe") in
the sense of a category of court village: a local-history localisation places it between
present-day Strakhovo and Tyapkino on the left bank of the Sknizhka. Starotumovo remains
unlocated; the publisher allows the reading **Starodumovo**. An essential
caveat: after the two villages the act names two peasants — Anfilog and Isay — but does not
assign them to the settlements. So it cannot yet be claimed that Anfilog was
from Podkletnoe specifically. The next targeted search is the household composition and departed
people of both settlements in the books of the 1550s–1570s.

[Name index, vol. I](http://62info.ru/history/node/8900),
[vol. II](http://62info.ru/history/node/9173),
[vol. III](https://62info.ru/history/node/9353),
[vol. IV](http://62info.ru/history/node/9676),
[full text of the Ryazan act](https://m.62info.ru/history/node/10631),
[toponymic dictionary of Ryazan Oblast](https://info.rounb.ru/elbibl/kray/top_slov.pdf),
[localisation of the vanished Podkletnoe](https://geocaching.su/cache/19565).

Card: `PUB-RYAZAN-1571-1590-PRE-OREL-RECRUITMENT-MECHANISM`. The map does not
change: the confirmed route Pekhletsky stan — Kamensky stan belongs
to unrelated surnames.
[Chronology of the survey books of Ryazan district](https://studfile.net/preview/11916075/),
[official РГАДА catalogue](https://www.rgada.ru/sprav/sprav/zenchenko_miu_ivanova_ga_otv_sost_pistsovye_knigi_riazanskoi.pdf).

### The Oryol Budget Roll (Smeta) of 1642 Separates Men Present from Those «сошедших с Орла» ("Gone from Oryol")

The file **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 4, d. 260** turned out to contain a useful control
structure. The main list of the Oryol deti boyarskie occupies fols. 369 v.–429,
and on fol. 431 a separate heading begins: **«Дети боярские сошли с Орла,
живут по розным городам»** ("Deti boyarskie gone from Oryol, living in various towns"). It records men who had ended up in Kursk,
Mtsensk, Efremov, Livny, Belyov, Chern, Chuguev and other places. Then come
separate lists of captives, of the dead and of those killed.

All seven bearers of the surname series appear earlier, in the main list of men
present: **Vasily Dmitriev syn Anpilov** (Vasily, son of Dmitry, Anpilov) on fol. 374 v.; **Aleksey Vasilyev syn
Anfilogov** on fol. 375; **Ivan Vasilyev** on fol. 388; **Afanasy Osipov** on
fol. 392 v.; **Kuzma Karpov** on fol. 393 v.; **Grigory Vasilyev** on fol. 402;
**Fyodor Vasilyev Anfilogov** on fol. 403 v. The surname is absent from the published lines
of the heading «сошли с Орла» ("gone from Oryol").

This is an important limit on the conclusion. By 1642 several branches were not merely keeping
the Oryol designation out of old habit but were still being counted in the corporation of servicemen
actually present in the town. Nikifor Dmitrievich of Belgorod and the Kursk
Anpilogovs therefore cannot automatically be declared men who had recently left
Oryol: their route needs its own transfer entry. At the same time
the budget roll says nothing about the origin of the older Oryol generations of the sixteenth
century, so no route back to 1566 can be built from it.

Card for the inventory and the structure of the file: `RGADA-210-OP4-OPIS-D260`.
[Line-by-line index and headings of the book](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/5703/135781/p5369181.htm).

### Pereyaslavl-Zalessky, 1590: a Whole Town Gave a Null Result

For comparative control, the only known sixteenth-century desyatnya (service roll) of
Pereyaslavl has been worked through. The heading stresses that Prince **Nikita Romanovich
Trubetskoy** and the dyak (clerk) **Postnik Dmitriev** reviewed in Veliky Novgorod the deti
boyarskie of **«всего города»** ("the whole town"). The editor counted 107 Pereyaslavl service
men. The old archival reference: МАМЮ, book of desyatni No. 13, printed inventory No. 219.

From the list, 89 complete constructions "first name — patronymic — family
designation" were extracted; the remaining entries are abbreviated or damaged in the printed layout.
Not a single complete construction matched Oryol in both patronymic and
surname at once. There is no exact three-part name either.

**Elizarey Osipov syn Onanyin** (Elizarey, son of Osip, Onanyin), No. 97, is indeed recorded with the family name
Onanyin: he was a non-serving novik (novice) of the first category, had come for the first time to
the sovereign's service and received a pomestye oklad (land entitlement) of 250 chetverti. But his first name and
patronymic do not match any Oryol Onanyin. This is evidence of
the wide spread of the surname, not of a move to Oryol.

Pereyaslavl remains part of the common northern world of service men, but it is not
confirmed name by name as a source of the Oryol core. Against such a null background the Epifan
and Ryazhsk family bridges become more significant. Card:
PUB-PEREYASLAVL-1590-OREL-1594-CROSSCHECK. No route has been drawn.
[The Pereyaslavl desyatnya of 1590](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Desjatni_XVI_v/text2.php).

### Serpukhov 1556 and Meshchera 1580: Early Surname Parallels without First Names

Along the northern and north-eastern corridors a source was found that is earlier
than Oryol itself. M. G. Krotov reconstructed lost desyatni from old
folio-by-folio alphabetical indexes and genealogical extracts of the Razryad-Senate Archive.
This is not a retelling of a late genealogy: the indexes record surname headings and
folios of the genuine service books, although they almost always lose the personal names.

In the Serpukhov part of **1556**, compiled ten years before the founding of
Oryol, the surname **Nemtsov** stands on fol. 11. The index has not preserved the first name. In
Oryol's Bazdyrevo of 1594/95 a **Gavrilka Fyodorov syn Nemtsov** (Gavrilka, son of Fyodor, Nemtsov) is known.
The coincidence provides an exact archival address for testing the Serpukhov
direction, but it does not prove kinship: there is no first name, no patronymic, no formula
of transfer.

In the Meshchera desyatnya of **1580** the surname **Onanyin** is noted on fols. 35,
36 and 67. The first two positions are in the fifth category of old service men
with a pomestye oklad of 100 chetverti; the third is among the non-serving noviki
who had come to be enrolled. Without the original, three folio positions cannot be counted as
three different men. Their first names have not survived either, so none of them can yet
be connected with the Oryol Kondraty Vavilovich, Samoyla Yuryevich, the
children of Trofim, Timofey Lamanovich, Daniil Olimpievich or the earlier
Anpilog Ivanovich Onanyin.

The source apparatus of the reconstruction has also been checked. When a full name
is restored from a genealogical extract of the Razryad-Senate Archive,
Krotov gives the exact reference: for example, the heading «Мещера» ("Meshchera") and the entry for Pyotr
Vasilyevich Bazarov are taken from **РГАДА, f. 388, kn. 883, fol. 177 v.**, the information
on T. N. Bogdanov from kn. 872, fol. 65, on Bazhen and Dmitry Sleptsov from
kn. 774, fol. 388. For the three Onanyin positions there is no such reference. Consequently,
the extracts of fond 388 known to and used by the author did not reveal their names;
the surnames were restored only from the index **f. 210, kn. 7, fols. 177–181**.
A new precise request to РГАДА is not a second look at extracts already cited, but a search
for a standalone genealogical certificate of the Onanyins/Ananyins and
spelling variants in the inventory of fond 388. The absence of a reference in Krotov does not
prove that no such unidentified certificate exists.

A dangerous outward resemblance has been examined separately. In Meshchera the
**Trunins** are recorded many times, including **Istomka Nikitin syn Trunin** (Istomka, son of Nikita, Trunin). These are not the Oryol
Trunovs. Until there is a manuscript chain of variants, Trunin and Trunov cannot
be merged into one family.

Thus Serpukhov and Meshchera have for the first time been checked against early registers and yielded
two real surname parallels — Nemtsov and the Onanyins. There are no Anfilogovs and no
exact personal matches. The map does not change: the source contains no confirmed route of a
bearer of the researched surname series. Card:
`PUB-KROTOV-1985-SERPUKHOV-MESCHERA-OREL-CROSSCHECK`.
[Study and source commentary](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1540-1560/Desjatni/pred.php),
[reconstructed text of the desyatni](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1540-1560/Desjatni/text.php).

### The First Meshchera–Oryol Family Bridge: the Sons of Yakov Bogdanov

The next Meshchera source has preserved full names. The money desyatnya (pay roll)
was compiled on 13 January 1590; what has come down to us is a seventeenth-century copy of the official
document, made for the Lyubovnikov family and kept in the manuscript
miscellany of the Rumyantsev Museum No. 3277, fols. 180–212. The numbering ends at
No. 162, but entry 18 includes another brother under No. 18a, so Yu. V. Gautier
counted 163 men.

From the full Meshchera list 151 standard formulas
"first name — patronymic — surname" were extracted; non-standard and short lines were checked
separately. Compared with the Oryol book of 1594/95 there turned out to be no complete personal
matches. On the other hand, a single match in both patronymic and surname at once was found:
**Yakovlev syn Bogdanov** (son of Yakov, Bogdanov).

- Meshchera, 1590, No. 149: **Bogdan Yakovlev syn Bogdanov**. He stands in the
  final section of men without a money oklad.
- Oryol, 1594/95, p. 947: **Ontipa Yakovlev syn Bogdanov** and immediately after him
  **Grigory Yakovlev syn Bogdanov**. Both hold zherebya (shares) in the same village of
  Istobnaya on the river Orel.

The names here give a possible family construction: Bogdan, Ontipa and Grigory
could have been sons of one Yakov Bogdanov. Ontipa is a documented form of
the name Antipa, not a misreading. Two Oryol men living side by side in one village
strengthens the probability that they were brothers, but the source does not say "brothers" outright.

It is especially important that Bogdan of Meshchera himself did not disappear. In the desyatnya of 1615 he is
recorded under No. 105 with an oklad of 500 chetverti and 14 roubles; the okladchiki (assessors) reported
that he was **«справлен на Москве»** ("verified in Moscow"), that is, his oklad had passed the central
prikaz check. Consequently, Bogdan went on serving in Meshchera and was not
a man who had moved to Oryol. If kinship is confirmed, it will be a matter
not of one man moving but of one family being divided between two
service towns.

This is the first addressable bridge **Meshchera — Oryol** based on a combination of patronymic
and surname, and not just on a shared surname form. It concerns the Bogdanovs,
not Anfilogovs, and for now remains a probable family connection. Therefore no profiles
and no line on the family map are created. Card:
`PUB-GAUTIER-1911-MESCHERA-OREL-1590-1595-BOGDANOV`.
[Full text of the desyatni of 1590 and 1615](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1580-1600/Desjatni_Wladimir_Mesera/text.php),
[the Oryol book of 1594/95](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n947/mode/1up).

### Meshchovsk and Bryansk, 1584: Four Family Bridges

A. L. Stanislavsky published a roster of the deti boyarskie of Meshchovsk, Opakov
and Bryansk that arose around December 1584. It was based on the desyatnya
of 1583/84 compiled in Kaluga. The original is kept in РГАДА:
f. 210, Stolbtsy (scrolls) of the Moscow desk, No. 1094, stolpik 3, fols. 1–20.
The list is incomplete: it has 360 men of Meshchovsk against no fewer than 400 in 1604,
and nine men of Opakov instead of the fifteen known from the battle of Molodi.

A complete comparison of names with the Oryol cadastral survey book did not produce a single
full match of one person and found no Anfilogovs or variants of
the researched surname. On the other hand, four matches in both
patronymic and family designation at once were found:

- **Zhdan Yakovlev syn Bogdanov** served in Meshchovsk in 1584. Together with
  **Bogdan Yakovlev syn Bogdanov** of Meshchera in 1590 and the Oryol
  **Ontipa** and **Grigory, the sons of Yakov Bogdanov**, of 1594/95, he
  extends the earlier construction to four possible sons of one Yakov
  Bogdanov in three service centres.
- The Meshchovsk men **Nekras** and **Vaska, the sons of Ivan Vorontsov**, are compared with
  the Oryol **Ignat Ivanov syn Vorontsov**, who held a zherebye in the
  village of Ploty in Nugorsk stan. This is a probable group of three brothers, but
  the word "brothers" is absent from the documents.
- **Roman Grigoryev syn Gridunov** of Meshchovsk is compared with the Oryol
  **Konan Grigoryev syn Gridunov** of Bulakovo in Taychukov stan.
  The rarity of the combination strengthens the hypothesis of brotherhood without turning it into a proven
  transfer.
- **Sergey Ivanov syn Kortashev** of Bryansk is compared with the Oryol
  **Gavrila Ivanov syn Kortashev**. The last entry is especially valuable:
  recorded together with Gavrila is his **nephew Matyushka Obramov syn Kortashev** (Matyushka, son of Obram, Kortashev).
  The document thereby directly proves the existence of a second paternal branch:
  Abram Kortashev was Gavrila's brother. Sergey remains a probable but not
  proven brother of Gavrila.

As a control, two false matches have been discarded. Ovonka Vasilyev syn Mikolaev
stands not in Oryol but in the Tula part of the large edition. Pavel Ivanov syn
Danilov-Lunev has a compound designation and cannot automatically be
compared with Kondraty Ivanov syn Danilov of Bryansk.

The most tempting pair for the researched family itself was checked separately.
On fol. 10 **Ivan Nechaev syn Kuleshev** is recorded; the editor corrected this reading
from «Кумшев» ("Kumshev"). Eleven years before the Oryol exemption grant the full pair
Ivan + Nechay matches **Ivan Nechaev syn Anpilogov**. But on fol. 6
of the same roster there already stands **Fyodor Nechaev syn Kuleshev**. Two sons of Nechay with one
family designation point to an independent Meshchovsk line of Kuleshevs.
Therefore Ivan Kuleshev is kept as a useful namesake control, but is not
considered an early record of the Oryol Anpilogov. The different family designations
outweigh the match of first name and patronymic; a scan of fol. 10 is needed only to check
the editorial correction Kuleshev from Kumshev. No profile and no route
Meshchovsk → Oryol is created.

The result is the first series of four independent family bridges from one
early list to Oryol. It supports the model of **distributed family
recruitment**: individual brothers or branches could be detached to a new town
while other family members were left in their former service. But this is not yet a discovered
decree on mass transfer, nor a proven origin of Anfilogovs. Profiles
of other surnames and routes on the family map are not created. Card:
`PUB-STANISLAVSKY-1974-MESCHOVSK-BRYANSK-OREL-1584-1595`.
[Full text of the roster of 1584](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Rospis_det_boj_m_o_b/text.htm),
[the Oryol book, p. 1027](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n1027/mode/1up),
[p. 1059](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n1059/mode/1up),
[p. 1068](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n1068/mode/1up).

### Two Onanyins in the Southern Service Belt: Kromy and Novgorod-Seversky

The next check followed an address given by A. L. Stanislavsky himself: the
book of pay distributed to the defenders of Novgorod-Seversky. On 31 January
1605 the stolnik Prince Pyotr Chyumakov-Cherkassky and the voivodes rewarded
the men who had withstood the siege by the army of False Dmitry I. The document is published as
No. 42 in the first volume of «Акты Московского государства» (Acts of the Muscovite State), pp. 65–77; the old
reference of the manuscript is «Десятня роздачи бояром золотых № 209» (Desyatnya of the distribution of gold coins to the boyars No. 209), fols. 1–32.

The book is especially valuable because it divides people by town and type of service:
deti boyarskie of Bryansk and Novgorod-Seversky, Moscow streltsy (musketeers),
Novgorod-Seversky pushkari (gunners), zatinshchiki (fortress gunners) and Cossacks, Cossacks of Belyov, Trubchevsk and
Kromy. In two separate sections one surname
form was found:

- p. 73, under the heading **«Новогородка Северскаго казаком»** ("to the Cossacks of Novgorod-Seversky") —
  **Bogdashko Onanyin**. In a group of 76 Cossacks he received doroga — an imported
  silk fabric, four arshins of good broadcloth and three roubles;
- p. 76, under the heading of the Kromy Cossacks — **Tishka Onanyin**. He too
  took part in the Novgorod-Seversky siege and was rewarded on top of the main
  roster.

Bogdashko is the everyday form of Bogdan. The full form of Tishka's name has not been established:
it may go back to Tikhon or a similar name, but the source does not
explain this. In both lines **Onanyin** occupies the surname position after the
personal name. This differs from the Kolomna «Федко Онаньин сын
Новокрещенов» ("Fedko Onanyin syn Novokreshchenov"), where Onanyin is the patronymic and the family designation is
Novokreshchenov.

Now, next to the Oryol nest of 1594/95, a later network becomes visible:
**Oryol — Kromy — Novgorod-Seversky**. The Oryol book knows Kondraty
Vavilovich, Samoyla Yuryevich, Yakov, Uvar and Nikita Trofimovich, Timofey
Lamanovich and Daniil Olimpievich Onanyin; the former holder of one
zherebye was Kondraty's grandfather **Anpilog Ivanov syn Onanyin**. A few
years later the surname form is already documented in two neighbouring southern Cossack
services.

This is stronger than distant isolated surname matches: the source itself preserves
two town affiliations within a common frontier garrison. But
the patronymics of Bogdashko and Tishka are not given, so they cannot be declared brothers,
descendants of Anpilog or relatives of Oryol Anfilogovs. The surname could have
arisen independently from different men named Onanya.

A control of the Bryansk part confirmed the quality of the comparison. Goryain Davydov
syn Golyanishchev of 1584 is almost certainly repeated as Goryain Golyanishchev;
Boris Ignatyev syn Krektyshev as Boris Krektyshev; Ivan Grigoryev syn
Panyutin has kept the full formula. There are no Kortashevs, Vorontsovs or Gridunovs among
the rewarded men of Bryansk, but this is a list of participants in a particular siege,
not a complete desyatnya of the town.

The service link between Oryol and Kromy is confirmed by a separate document from exactly
the same time as the Oryol cadastral survey book. The «Роспись Кромским сторожам» (Roster for the Kromy watch posts) of
9 April 1595 instructs Oryol and Kromy jointly to send two
men each to four watch posts (storozhi): on the Bystraya Sosna; on the Ruda at the head of the Ochka;
on the Oka at Bystry; on the Svopa at the mouth of the Tureya. On three directions they were
joined by Mtsensk, and on the Bystraya Sosna also by Novosil and Livny. The names
of the watchmen are not recorded, so Tishka cannot be linked to any of these posts.
But the main point for the settlement mechanism is proven: Kromy was not just a neighbouring
town but a garrison sharing field service with Oryol.

The next archival lead has been refined from the official РГАДА register. For Kromy
it gives f. 1209, op. 1, kn. 1037: copies of the cadastral survey book of Rechitsky stan of
1627–1630 and of the books of the Kromy service slobody (settlements) of 1639–1642.
A secondary reference to book 214 that had come up was not confirmed and is not included in the
search plan.

New card:
`PUB-AMG-1605-NOVGOROD-SEVERSKY-KROMY-ONANINY`.
[The distribution book of 1605](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/41-60/42.htm),
[roster for the Kromy watch posts, 1595](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/21-40/38.htm),
[the Oryol entry on Anpilog and Kondraty Onanyin](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n948/mode/1up).
The map does not change: the Onanyins do not belong to the public surname series,
and the document contains no direct route of a bearer of the Anfilogov form.

### Maksim Ivanov syn Anpilogov: the Surname Confirmed in Kromy

In the search book of service men of the Ukrainian (frontier) towns for 7181 (1672/73)
the first Anpilogov from Kromy checked against the original folio has been found.
Archival address: **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6d, d. 63, fol. 35**. By the tsar's
decree the zhilets Mina Semyonovich Shakhov travelled round Belyov, Bolkhov, Karachev, Oryol,
Kromy and Bryansk, searching for military men who had not set out for the regiment of Prince
Yury Nikitich Baryatinsky.

The Kromy section reports that from 21 August 7181 gunners and streltsy
searched, by doezdy (visits), for **netchiki** — men absent according to the regimental list.
Those found were released on surety and sent with bailiffs (pristavy) to the regiment. In the continuation of the
list on fol. 35, the second line reads:

> **Максим Иванов сын Анпилогов**.
>
> *Translation:* "Maksim Ivanov syn Anpilogov" (Maksim, son of Ivan, Anpilogov).

The reading separates all three parts without guesswork: Maksim is the personal name; Ivanov
syn is the patronymic formula, that is, son of Ivan; Anpilogov is the family
designation of the researched series. The word «нетчик» (netchik) proves failure to appear at the muster,
but does not explain its reason, so Maksim cannot be called a deserter.
His specific rank is not stated on the folio either.

For the history of the Oryol core this is a late but important geographical anchor.
In 1595 Oryol and Kromy together supplied men for four watch posts; in
1605 Tishka Onanyin was among the Kromy Cossacks; in 1673 an Anpilogov is already named outright in the same
town. This proves a long-standing presence of the
surname series in the common service network, but does not fill the eighty-year
gap back to Oryol Anfilogovs of 1594/95.

Profile `P9672` and card
`PUB-RGADA-210-6D-63-L35-MAKSIM-ANPILOGOV` have been created. Kromy is added to the map as
Maksim's place of service; no route to the regiment is drawn, because the destination
is not named in the fragment examined.

An open search database gave another important control point. For the exact
form **«Анпилогов Максим»** ("Anpilogov Maksim") four archival lines are found:

- Kromy, 1670 — РГАДА, f. 210, op. 5, d. 35;
- Kromy, 1687 — РГАДА, f. 1157, op. 1, d. 8;
- Kromy, 1689 — РГАДА, f. 210, op. 5, d. 68;
- Oryol, 1697 — РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6g, d. 25.

The earlier hypothesis of a single service biography has now been refuted. The complete
published list of 1686/87 names **Maksim Androsov
Anpilogov**, that is, Maksim Andreevich, whereas the original of 1673
clearly names **Maksim Ivanov syn**. At least two Maksim Anpilogovs existed in Kromy
at the same time. The lines of 1670, 1689 and 1697 without
patronymics cannot be assigned in advance to either of them; the route Kromy — Oryol is not
drawn.

On the other hand, a comparison of the lists of 1670 and 1686/87 gave three more precise
candidates for continuity:

- **Karp Anpilogov** → **Karp Deevich Anpilogov**;
- **Maksim Anpilogov** → **Maksim Andreevich Anpilogov**;
- **Sazon Anpilogov** → **Sazon Nefedovich**, published as
  «Анталогов Сазон Нефедев» ("Antalogov Sazon Nefedev").

In all three cases Kromy, the first name and the surname pattern coincide across an interval of
sixteen years. Sazon's name is especially rare, so the match is strong,
but the form «Анталогов» (Antalogov) requires a mandatory check of the scan. Thus the list of 1670
for the first time reveals a possible generation of fathers — **Dey, Andrey and
Nefed**. The full skazki (sworn statements) of 1686/87 should contain the land and money
oklad, the father's service, one's own service, children and relatives, and arms;
this is the best next set of folios for checking the three identities.

For the ten people whose full forms in the publication read precisely as
Anpilogov, separate profiles `P99452`–`P99461` have been created. For Sazon
Nefedovich no separate profile is created from the late line for now: the surname is
rendered as «Анталогов» and must be confirmed from the original. He remains
an argued entry inside the Record, as the procedure for working with a
doubtful reading requires.

For the five named people of the 1670 list separate cautious
profiles have been created: `P99447` Gavrila, `P99448` Karp, `P99449` Maksim, `P99450` Sazon
and `P99451` Stepan. Two lines with undeciphered names remain without
profiles until the original is read. The card records that the open publication
contains 4540 entries, including 513 men of Oryol and 502 men of Kromy; the original entries usually
give attendance or absence, the reason, the pomestye oklad and additional information.

A separate cross-check strengthened the biography of the departed **Averky
Grigoryevich Anpilogov**. The index to РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6, d. 105 already
contains **Averky Anpilogov**: the file was compiled in 1679 about men wounded
in the Chigirin service of 1678 who received an increase to their pomestye and
money oklads. The rare first name, the same surname and an interval of seven to eight years
make a sequence probable: wounded at Chigirin → reward for the
wound → later departure from Kromy district. But the early index hides
the patronymic and the town, so two separate profiles have been created (`P99462` and
`P99457`), not one glued-together biography.

At the same time a source error has been corrected: the card for file 210-6-105 previously
referred to the web page of a different list — the regiment of Semyon Khovansky,
f. 210, op. 5, d. 50, which lists men of Pskov, Pustorzhev and Nevel and
contains no Anpilogov. The wrong link has been removed; the archival reference and the meaning of the Chigirin
entry have been kept according to the correct index.

#### Aleksey Ivanovich: a Probable Trail Kromy → Kursk, 1686/87–1693

For the second departed man, not a search for one name but a check of the whole
migration group was applied. From the section **«Из Кромского уезда сошли (указано куда и
в каком году)»** ("Gone from Kromy district (stating where and in what year)") all 195 combinations of surname and first name were extracted. They
were compared with the 5504 lines of the muster list of the regiment of Venedikt Zmeev in
Kursk of 1693, **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 5, d. 72**.

Twelve different combinations of surname and first name matched; eight of them have a
Kursk line in the regimental list. Among the control matches are Gavrila
Ryzhkov, Semyon Maslov, Ivan Kononov, Danila Gorelogo, Stepan Chernyshev, Sila
Gribanov and Mikhey Shakhov. Into this same predominantly Kursk group falls
**Aleksey Anpilogov**.

The result is a candidate sequence:

- 1686/87: **Aleksey Ivanovich Anpilogov** is already placed among those gone from
  Kromy district;
- 1693: **Aleksey Anpilogov** is in the Kursk part of Zmeev's regiment.

This is stronger than a chance match of two Alekseys: the check produced a common cohort
trail. But it is not yet proof of one person. The publication of the Kromy book
omitted precisely the decisive words about the place and year of departure, and the Kursk index does not
give the patronymic or the folio. Moreover, Aleksey has not been found in the disclosed skazki of Kursk Anpilogovs
of 1697. Within four years he could have died, left again,
changed his service category or simply not fallen into the extracted group, so
a negative check does not refute the identity.

A separate profile for the Kursk Aleksey, `P99693`, has been created; it is not merged with
the Kromy `P99458`. The cards of both documents are linked by an explicit hypothesis. No route
is drawn on the map yet.

An attempt to continue this chain still further back, to Oryol, gave an important
**negative result**. The index does name an Aleksey
Anpilogov in Oryol in 1641/42 and an Aleksey Onpilogov there in 1648.
But the first man has already been checked against primary fol. 375 of file 210-4-260: he is
**Aleksey Vasilyevich Anfilogov**, who served continuously in Oryol from 1620/21 to
1645. The man who left Kromy is **Aleksey Ivanovich**. The difference in patronymics
breaks a neat but false chain Oryol 1642 → Kromy 1687. The 1648 line
without a patronymic primarily continues the Oryol Vasilyevich; it is linked
to his profile as the leading identification, but still awaits the original.

A scholarly article by K. V. Petrov clarifies the meaning of the formula «сошёл» ("left") itself. In the seventeenth
century leaving a service town was not necessarily an official transfer and did not
always immediately deprive a man of his former pomestye. The Kursk skazki of 1697
contain a direct control: Kirey Durakov said that he had left precisely from Rechitsky
stan of Kromy district, and that his father's pomestye there had been left empty. Therefore in
the original entry for Aleksey one must look not only for the new town but also for the fate of
the old Kromy pomestye, the service category and the formula of admission to Kursk.

[Muster list of Zmeev's regiment, 1693](https://dompredkov.ru/smotrennyj-spisok-polka-venedikta-zmeeva-v-kurse-1693/),
[review book (razbornaya kniga) of Kromy, 1686/87](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/2172/74786/p2847447.htm),
[K. V. Petrov on transfers between service towns](https://inslav.ru/sites/default/files/2021_shveykovskaya_petrov.pdf).

### Bazdyrevo, the Next Generation: the Register of 1625

The name of the scribe has been corrected. A late historical inventory and its modern
publication give «Иван Коптев» ("Ivan Koptev"), but this is a secondary error. P. P. Smirnov, on
p. 4 of his 1910 study, prints **Ivan Kutuzov**; the same reading is given by
the official РГАДА catalogue of 2013 and by a literal quotation from a scribes' instruction (nakaz) of
1681: «с орловских дозорных книг письма и меры Ивана Кутузова да
подьячего Ивана Коровина 134-го» ("from the Oryol inspection books of the description and measurement of Ivan Kutuzov and the
under-clerk Ivan Korovin of [7]134"). Therefore related documents must be searched
under Kutuzov, and «Коптев» (Koptev) kept only as an erroneous form of the historical
register.

The public transcription of the register of the cadastral survey book of Oryol district, of the description and
measurement of Ivan Kutuzov and the under-clerk (podyachy) Ivan Korovin of 7133–7134, has preserved on
fols. 200–200 v. four Anfilogov entries at once in the village of Bazdyrevo:

- Ivan Vasilyev syn Anfilogova — Bozdyrevo and the pustosh (deserted land) Tokarna;
- Vasily Dmitriev syn Anfilogova — a separate holding in Bozdyrevo;
- Aleksey and Grigory, **Vasilyevy deti Anfilogova** (the sons of Vasily Anfilogov);
- Martyn and Kuzma, **Karpovy deti Anfilogova** (the sons of Karp Anfilogov).

The last two formulas for the first time give not a presumed coincidence of patronymics
but two direct brotherhoods. Aleksey and Grigory were sons of one Vasily;
Martyn and Kuzma were sons of one Karp. Kuzma Karpovich can then be confidently
traced in the Oryol books of 1641/42 and 1645. The strongest
hypothesis is that his father is identical with Karp Mikulovich Anfilogov, who held
his father's pomestye in the same Bazdyrevo in 1594/95. The exact geography,
the rare surname and the chronology are consistent, but the 1625 register does not repeat
Karp's patronymic and does not state a transfer of land, so the kinship cannot yet
be marked as proven.

The name Martyn likewise cannot automatically be corrected to Marko. Marko Karpovich
is documented in the service lists of 1626–1628; until the original of the
register has been examined, this is either another brother or an error by a late copyist/compiler.
The exact next source is now known: РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1,
kn. 330–331, fols. 200–200 v., followed by the otkaznye (land transfer) and otdelnye (allotment) books between
1595 and 1625, where the transfer of Karp's zherebye to his children may be recorded.

The priority has been widened from one register spread to the full text of books
330–331. Smirnov established that Kutuzov and Korovin received for «приправки» (pripravka, checking against the old description) a
copy of the book of Dementy Yakovlev and Leonty Sofonov of 1594/95. The full text
of the 1625/26 description may have preserved comparisons with the old description,
the oklad list and earlier grants that did not make it into the brief register of
holders. This is an especially valuable channel because the work began before the fire
of the Pomestny prikaz of 1626, and the pripravka copy of the old Oryol book
was in the hands of the travelling scribes. The surviving books 330–331 are copies of
1669–1674 of the description of 1624–1626.

### Two Independent Place Names: Kursk and Kozelsk

Two new Anpilogovs must be strictly kept apart. In Kursk district an entry of 1766
on fol. 535 of the file РГАДА, f. 1209-DML, Kursk-15724, kn. 7, d. 35 recounts
the otkaz (land transfer) of Captain Mitrofan Kretov of 7198 (1689/90). In the village of Anpilogova
on the little river Kuritsa lay the pomestye of **Semyon Yakovlevich Anpilogov**,
registered to his grandsons **Fyodor and Prokofy Dementyevich
Anpilogov**. The brothers exchanged 80 chetverti with Gavrila Ivanovich Afanasyev.
The grammar directly restores the intermediate generation: their father
was **Dementy Semyonovich Anpilogov**. This is a three-generation Kursk branch and a strong
link between the surname and the village name, but not a proven founder and not a link with
Oryol. The historical settlement later split into 1st and 2nd Anpilogovo on
the Bolshaya Kuritsa; the map shows their common zone without falsely choosing one.

Separately, the official РГАДА catalogue revealed **the village of Anpilogovo on the little river
Medvedka in Podgorodny stan of Kozelsk district**: f. 281, op. 11,
d. 5901/20. The extract of 1682/83 was made from the cadastral survey books of Vasily Tolbuzin and
Ivan Glazov of 1628–1631. The holders listed in the heading — Godunov,
Zhelyabuzhsky, the Rumyantsevs and Vikentyev — do not explain the name. So the place name is
older than the known chain of later holders and may go back to an earlier
Anpilog, but the catalogue does not name such a man. No profile and no map entry are created until
the three folios of the file have been examined.

### The Census of 1664: 33 Service Names and the Bazdyrevo Folios Found

A large file has been found and checked against its archival reference: **РГАДА, f. 1209,
op. 1, d. 9768** — the census book (perepisnaya kniga) of the town of Oryol and Oryol district by Stepan
Ivanovich Kondyrev, dated 30 April 1664. The scholarly description of the file precisely
places Korchakov stan on fols. 311–396 v., and the village of **Bazdyreva on
fols. 349 v.–352**; from fol. 352 v. the village of Krivtsova already begins. Images of all
the Bazdyrevo folios have been found in the open project «Сказочки, сказки и сказищи
Орловщины» ("Little Tales, Tales and Great Tales of the Oryol Land") and saved locally for line-by-line work.

The open alphabetical index to this book gives **33 entries** of the researched
surname, and all of them are assigned to dvoryane and deti boyarskie:

- 16 forms **Anpilogov**: name not read, Akim, Bogdashka, Vaska,
  Demenshka, Efimka, two Ivans, Ivashka, Lyubimka, Ma…, Martin, Safronka,
  Seryozhka, Fomka, Yakushka;
- 17 forms **Anpliogov**: Ageyka, Ananya, Andryushka, Grishka, Gurka,
  Deniska, Ivashka, Isayka, Kuzma, Larion, Merkushka, Mikitka(?), Mikishka,
  Panka, Seryozhka, Stenka, Filimoshka.

The form Anpliogov is regarded as a transposition of letters within the same surname,
not as a separate family. Diminutive forms of names are usual in seventeenth-century chancery
records and do not by themselves mean that a person was a child.

A strict limit is important here. The alphabetical index does not print folio numbers
or the settlements of individual people. The Bazdyrevo folios do
contain a long sequence of service households, but for now all 33 names cannot
automatically be placed in this village. That requires a full
transcription: separating household heads from sons, reading the patronymics, ages, regiment and
losses from the Tatar raid. Therefore the map gets no new falsely
precise point, and the card `RGADA-1209-1-9768-OREL-ANPILOGOVY` honestly
separates the original from the internet index.

Even without a premature distribution by household, the scale of the conclusion changes.
By 1664 there existed in Oryol district not a lone family but a large
service community of bearers of the surname. This fits far better with
hereditary growth of a core that already held land in 1594/95 and 1625
than with a late arrival of one family. However, the census has not yet named the former service town of the older
generation.

**Names and their research significance.** Bogdashka — Bogdan, Vaska — Vasily,
Demenshka — Dementy, Efimka — Efim, Ivashka — Ivan, Seryozhka — Sergey,
Fomka — Foma, Yakushka — Yakov; Ageyka — Aggey, Andryushka — Andrey, Grishka —
Grigory, Deniska — Denis, Isayka — Isay, Mikitka — Nikita, Stenka —
Stepan, Filimoshka — Filimon. Akim usually corresponds to Ioakim, Safronka to
Sofron. The forms Gurka, Merkushka, Mikishka and Panka are for now left without
forced expansion: the original folio is needed here. The ending
`-ка`/`-шка` is a deprecating chancery form, not proof of childhood
age. The two Ivans, Ivashka and the two Seryozhkas show especially clearly why
one must not create a single profile for a matching name without a patronymic and a household. On the other hand,
the rare Ananya and Filimoshka become addressable keys for subsequent
analyses: a repetition of patronymic, age and family can link generations.

A technical limitation that had been thought archival turned out to be an error in our local
copy. On 30 August 2026 three
full spreads, **fols. 349 v.–352**, measuring 4624 × 2608 pixels, were found in the project's open cloud and saved. The right
edges and the continuations of the household formulas are present on them; only
the site's earlier derived fragments had been cropped. The card and the warning have been corrected,
and the old fragments kept alongside as auxiliary enlargements. Now
the obstacle is not technical but palaeographic: names, patronymics, ages and
household composition should be read from the full spreads, without distributing all
33 index names to Bazdyrevo in advance.

### A Step Back from 1664: Where the Census of 1645/46 Lies

After the full folios of 1664 were recovered, the search moved to the previous
neighbouring cross-section. The open cloud holds **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 14,
fols. 212–408**: 205 full-size spreads of the census book of Oryol and its district by
D. M. Nesvitsky of 1645/46. However, the official composition of the archival copy
points to an even more convenient address: **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, d. 10042**.
In it the alphabetical index of Oryol holders occupies **fols. 24–82**, and the Oryol book itself
**fols. 326–523**; the town of Oryol is on fols. 515–521. The open set of file
14 contains only part of the text and does not contain this index.

The ranges give a working correspondence between the two copies in their common main
part. The text of file 14 occupies fols. 212–408, and the Oryol text in file 10042
begins at fol. 326: the folio number of the copy is obtained by adding **114** to
the number in the open original. The control point matches: the town section,
fols. 515–521 of the copy, translates into fols. 401–407 of file 14, and these spreads
are indeed present in the open set. Therefore a reference in the index to
the main text can be translated back: for example, fol. 400 of file 10042
corresponds to fol. 286 of file 14. The last folios 521–523, where the description notes
an inventory of corrections, must be checked separately rather than treated as a mechanical continuation.

The index remains useful for searching for people across the whole district, but the location of
Bazdyrevo was resolved by a detour. In an old thematic discussion
an exact archival reference was found to six consecutive spreads of file 14:
**fols. 240 v.–241, 241 v.–242, 242 v.–243, 243 v.–244,
244 v.–245 and 245 v.–246**. All six full-size frames were downloaded on 30 August
2026 from the open set and saved for line-by-line reading.
The heading of the village begins at the bottom of fol. 241; after its households, on fol. 245 v.,
the next section begins. Therefore the earlier idea of first obtaining the index
is no longer a prerequisite for reading the settlement itself.

The first confident result from the original is on **fol. 243, household No. 441**:
**«Двор того ж помещика Ивана Васильева сына Онпилогова»** ("Household of the same pomeshchik Ivan Vasilyev syn Onpilogov"). This is not an index
name without a patronymic. The rare surname, the full paternal formula and the village
connect the entry with the already documented Ivan Vasilyevich: an Oryol
novik of 1620/21, a holder in Bazdyrevo in 1625, a participant in the musters
of 1626 and a service man in the book of 1641/42. Thus in
1645/46 his separate household was still located within the old family
nest. The card `RGADA-1209-1-14-L243-IVAN-ONPILOGOV` has been added to the profile and
to the map as an observation in Bazdyrevo; no route is created, because
this entry does not describe a resettlement.

The remaining lines of the six spreads are still being worked through. On the neighbouring folios
households of the Trunovs and other old holdings are visible, but the names of dependants and
kinship formulas must not be replaced by guesses. The main new check is
to find among them Aleksey and Grigory Vasilyevich, Vasily Dmitrievich,
Martyn and Kuzma Karpovich from the 1625 register, or to document their
absence specifically in this settlement. The census stands between the name-by-name oath
of 1645 and the large Oryol list of 1647/48 and can show which
branches kept their households and which had moved away by this time.

[Open set of file 14](https://disk.yandex.ru/d/beTOHQ7SI7x1RQ),
[composition of the Oryol books of fond 1209](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/1851/142603/p5037680.htm).

### A Separate Bolkhov Cluster: Ten Lines of 1686–1687

A new search database of service men led to the review book of the men of Belyov and
Bolkhov, **РГАДА, f. 1157, op. 1, d. 7**. For Bolkhov two open indexes
agree on ten lines of the surname series: `Анпилов ..`, Karp, Naum, two
Pershkas; two Vasily Anpilogovs, Isay, Nikola and Fedotka. For the nine named
lines separate provisional profiles have been created; the nameless line is kept
only in the Record. The double Pershka and Vasily for now mean two index
appearances, not proven namesakes: it may also be a repetition of one person.

The main difference from earlier index finds is that a **complete open
set of the original** has been found: the project «Сказочки, сказки и сказищи Орловщины» has posted
524 frames of the Bolkhov part, fols. 1–289. Working previews were downloaded on 27 August
2026 to locate the lines. The indexes do not give folio numbers, and
automatic recognition of chancery cursive (skoropis) is unreliable, so patronymics,
ages, children, pomestya, arms and the start of service have not yet been transferred to the cards.

The names here themselves become search tools. Karp, Naum and Isay are rare;
Nikola is Nikolay, not Nikifor; Fedotka is a chancery form of Fedot and not
a sign of childhood. Pershka is left literally: it must not be expanded into
Persha or Pervusha before the line is read. A match between the Bolkhov Karp and
the Oryol Karp Anfilogov, or between the Bolkhov Vasilys and the Oryol Vasilys,
is not accepted as kinship without a patronymic.

This is an independent group that existed at the same time as the Oryol one. It
makes Bolkhov a mandatory direction of the early search, but does not yet answer
who moved to whom. Only the documented event
in Bolkhov in 1686–1687 has been added to the map; no route Bolkhov–Oryol has been drawn.

Along the way the popular version about a «ливенском воже Анпилогове» ("Livny guide Anpilogov") in
Belgorod in 1599 was rechecked. The complete published list of Livny guides (vozhi) names
Kamenev, the Krivtsovs, Ladygin, Maslov, Telyatnikov, Fursov and Cherny, but
there is no Anpilogov among them. Nikita Efimov syn Anpilogov appears in Sazhnoe
only in a later rendering of the book of 1616, and the surname is noted again in
the cadastral survey book of 1626. Consequently, the match of surnames with the Livny
contingent is a group observation by the article's author, not a personal route of our
bearer; the hypothesis Livny → Belgorod remains withdrawn.

### Rechecking the Aggregate Database: What It Added and What It Did Not Find

On 25 August 2026 the whole public search service «Дом
предков» ("House of Ancestors"), with 421,095 lines, was checked again: the forms Anpilogov, Anfilogov,
Onfilogov, Onpilogov, Anpliogov, Ampilogov and short forms without `-лог-` were queried separately.
No new entry earlier than the Oryol landholding of 1594/95 was found. In the
general list of 1627–1628, which the service links to РГАДА, f. 210,
op. 19, d. 2, there is no line with the surname either.

The earliest non-Oryol line remains the same: **Fyodor Anpilogov,
Kursk, 1605–1618**, but instead of a file the service shows only the conventional
«сборн» ("collect.") and does not name a folio. It is ten or more years later than the Oryol
landholders of 1594/95. Therefore Fyodor cannot be turned into an ancestor
or into evidence of movement from Kursk to Oryol. On the contrary, the reliably
verifiable lines of the 1620s first show an already existing
Oryol core and then a parallel presence in Kursk.

The service is useful as a search grid, but not as a primary source. Its results
still contain two lines already refuted by the complete name index and
the original of the file of 1620/21: «Анпилогов Марк» ("Anpilogov Mark") and «Онпилогов Илюшка» ("Onpilogov Ilyushka").
So the absence of an early find is a meaningful negative result,
and any future positive line must still pass a check against
the folio.

### Ananya Anpilogov: a Personal Skazka as the Best Late Source

For the first time a separate profile has been created for a man who can be traced for almost
twenty years. **Ananya Anpilogov** appears in the Oryol review of 1686/87,
in two different sets of 1697, in the review of 1700 and again in 1705:

- РГАДА, f. 1157, op. 1, d. 9 — review of 1686/87;
- f. 210, op. 12, d. 1583 — personal skazki of 1697;
- f. 210, op. 6g, d. 25 — muster of 1697;
- f. 210, op. 6g, d. 26 — review of 1700;
- f. 1157, op. 1, d. 19 — review of 1705.

The combination of a rare first name, the surname, one town and an unbroken chronology
makes the identity highly probable. But for now this is an index chain: for
a formal merge, a repeated patronymic, age, rank or
family composition is needed.

The main find is the real heading of file 1583. It is not a general list but
**skazki of the men of Oryol, service men of the foreign order (inozemny stroy), on their service, family composition
and property**, submitted at the syezzhy dvor (office) to the stolnik Ivan
Vasilyevich Zolotaryov. That is, Ananya's own account lies inside. His
specific folio is not yet known, but the archival request is now extremely narrow:
only one skazka needs to be found, and the start of service, the father's service,
family, pomestye and former holding copied out. This is the most probable late document
