that could retrospectively connect the branch with the seventeenth-century generations.

An Ananya Anpliogov is also in the index of 1664. He may have been the same man
in his youth or an older relative, but the twenty-two-year gap and
the absence of a patronymic do not allow the entries to be merged. Profile `P9679`
and card `PUB-DOMPREDKOV-1686-1705-OREL-ANANYA-ANPILOGOV` have been created.

### Why the Late "Incomers" Do Not Explain the Core

As a control, the special file **РГАДА, f. 350, op. 2, ch. 2,
d. 2439, fols. 1–90 — «Сказки пришлых людей, жителей города Орла»** ("Statements of incomers, inhabitants of the town of Oryol") **of 1721**
was checked. Full images of the file are also in the open Oryol project.

A study by I. E. Kushelev establishes that in the thirty years before 1721
only **75 incomers** arrived in Oryol. None had his own
household; most lived by unskilled labour, almost half worked on
river boats (strugi) as cooks, and by 1748 only a handful had settled in the town.
In the published list of incomers' surnames the forms Anpilogov, Anfilogov,
Onfilogov or Ampilogov do not appear.

This is a negative but meaningful result. It shows what
genuine late migration records look like — with the former place, the route of
arrival, occupation and the lack of one's own household — and at the same time rules out
the hypothesis that the old family core arose from the urban influx of
1691–1721. The file concerns the town, not all the district deti
boyarskie, and says nothing about a sixteenth-century resettlement; so it serves as
a control, not an answer. A lead has been recorded:
`LEAD-350-2-2439-OREL-INCOMING-1721`.

### Updated Interim Conclusion

The evidential sequence now looks like this:

1. In 1594/95 Anfilogovs already hold land in several related
   groups in Bazdyrevo and the neighbouring villages.
2. In 1596 most of the Oryol noviki are not fresh settlers but
   young men from an already existing landholding milieu.
3. In 1625 Bazdyrevo keeps several family holdings and two explicitly
   named pairs of brothers.
4. By 1664 the census indexes 33 Anpilogovs/Anpliogovs among the dvoryane and
   deti boyarskie — the result of long hereditary growth.
5. In 1686/87–1705 Ananya and other bearers of the surname are traced in
   repeated service reviews; Ananya's personal skazka may explain
   a specific branch.
6. The late influx of "incomers" into the town was small and did not form
   the family core.

Consequently, the main gap now lies not between the 1590s and the eighteenth
century but **before the first cadastral survey book**: where did the older generation come from,
which had already received Oryol zherebya by 1594/95? Priority remains with
two types of sources: early razryad (military service office) name lists of Oryol of 1577–the 1590s and
the otkaznye/otdelnye books of 1595–1625, where the transfer of a father's pomestye
may preserve the former town or the formula of the original grant by the sovereign.

### The Older Generation Now Has Names

The patronymic formulas have made it possible to replace the nameless "generation of fathers" with
eight cautious search profiles. They are Mikula, the one explicitly named
father of Vasily, Timofey and Karp; two unmerged Vasilys, the fathers of Belyay and
Osip; Persha, the father of Ivan; Timofey, the father of Sofon; Nechay, the father of Ivan;
Pakhom, the father of Ermak; Terenty, the father of Pavel, who was enrolled in 1596.

Two limits are especially important here. Belyay and Osip may have been brothers, but
the book does not call them «Васильевыми детьми» ("the sons of Vasily"), so one common Vasily would be
a premature merge. Timofey, the father of Sofon, may have been Timofey
Mikulovich, but there is as yet no direct transfer of a pomestye or kinship formula.
The family designation belongs to the full formulas of the sons; the fathers themselves are not
recorded as separate holders. Therefore the profiles record the proven name and
fatherhood, but get no dates, holdings or points on the map of their own.

The practical result is seven rare or controllable names to check in
pre-Oryol lists of the 1550s–1580s. Especially distinctive are the secular (non-baptismal) names Persha
and Nechay and the triple combination "Mikula — Vasily, Timofey, Karp": a match of such a
group in another town would be substantially stronger than a simple match of
the surname.

### Zakharov's Full Dissertation Found in the RSL: Exact Pages to Order

The official catalogue card of the RSL (Russian State Library) confirmed that behind the short author's abstract (avtoreferat) stands
a digitised 246-page dissertation by A. L. Zakharov, «"Служилые люди" и
дворянство на территории Орловской губернии в конце XVI–XVIII вв.» ("'Service People' and the Nobility on the Territory of Oryol Governorate at the End of the 16th–18th Centuries"). Its
system number is **01004419514**. The open viewer shows the table of contents,
but under copyright rules the full text is available only in the reading rooms and
virtual reading rooms of the RSL.

The table of contents has for the first time made it possible to narrow a future order to four fragments:

- pp. 40–61 — service people of the Muscovite state and of the zasechnaya cherta (abatis line);
- pp. 62–68 — the Kakurins as an example of an old Oryol service family;
- pp. 113–127 — deti boyarskie and the mechanism of their settlement;
- **pp. 169–184 — "Oryol" noble families of the 16th–18th centuries**.

The last fragment has the highest priority: the abstract states outright that it
examines the main list of families, their origin, the time they were granted pomestya
and their fate in the region. That is exactly where to look for Anpilogovs, even if they
are absent from the subject search of the open part. Until these pages have been read,
no conclusion about the family's town of origin may be attributed to Zakharov.

In parallel, three short articles by the author have been identified that may repeat
material from the closed chapter: «Землевладение орловского дворянства в начальный
период Орловского края» ("Landholding of the Oryol Nobility in the Initial Period of the Oryol Region", 2008, No. 2, pp. 22–28), «Города Орловского края на
защите южных рубежей… Возникновение “коренного” дворянства» ("Towns of the Oryol Region Defending the Southern Frontiers… The Emergence of a 'Native' Nobility", 2008, No. 2,
pp. 32–35) and «Поместное землевладение Орловского региона» ("Pomestye Landholding in the Oryol Region", 2008, No. 3,
pp. 23–42). No free full copies have been found yet.

[Catalogue card of the dissertation in the RSL](https://search.rsl.ru/ru/record/01004419514),
[official viewer](https://viewer.rsl.ru/ru/rsl01004419514),
[free author's abstract](https://new-disser.ru/_avtoreferats/01004419514.pdf).
## An Exact Address in the Otkaznye Books: «Ампиловы» ("Ampilovs"), Part 1, Book 3, No. 400

The official РГАДА alphabetical index to the otkaznye (land transfer) books for Oryol and Bolkhov for 1619–1746 gave not the general advice "look in fond 1209" but a specific reference. On fol. 2, as a separate heading, is written: **«Ампиловы: ч. 1, кн. 3, № 400»** ("Ampilovs: part 1, book 3, No. 400"). In the opening table of the same archival volume the third book of the first part corresponds to the modern general number **10054**. So the next priority document is **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, kn. 10054, old entry No. 400**.

At the stage of the index alone it could not be asserted that the entry concerned Oryol or Bazdyrevo specifically: the heading combines Oryol with Bolkhov and does not print the date of the entry. So a personal profile and a map placement had to wait for the act-by-act inventory. The next section removes some of these limitations.

### Entry No. 400 Revealed: Neustroy Rusinov syn Ampilov, 1649

The act-by-act inventory of the book has been found in the same official РГАДА set. On fols. 135–136 entry No. 400 is summarised as follows: **on 7 September 7158 three holdings in Oryol district were transferred (otkazany) to Neustroy Rusinov syn Ampilov** (Neustroy, son of Rusin, Ampilov). The date corresponds to 7 September 1649 in the Julian calendar. The former holders are named as Aleksey Nekrasov and Leonty Prokhoryev syn Ryshkov; of the lands, **Novoselka** and **the village of Perevesnaya** can be read with confidence, while the name of the first tract (urochishche) near the hamlet (seltso) is for now read provisionally as «Рогоживец» ("Rogozhivets").

This at once corrects several earlier limitations. The entry concerns Oryol district specifically, gives the full name of the bearer of the surname and supplies his father's name: **Rusin**. A profile for Neustroy, `P9680`, has been created; the two former holders of another surname remain only in the transcription. On the map the event is assigned to Oryol district, but the three old lands do not yet get falsely precise coordinates.

For the question of origin the result matters not as a ready answer about a resettlement but as a new two-generation bridge. Rusin Ampilov should now be sought in the Oryol books of the 1620s–1640s, and Neustroy in the census of 1664. If the original entry says «отца его поместье» ("his father's pomestye"), names a former town, or if the census gives a matching patronymic, it will be possible to link him with the generation of 1625. For now such a link remains a task, not established kinship.

An open academic publication of 1977 has been checked as a possible substitute for
the original. It was indeed prepared from the same Oryol otkaznaya book
**РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 199, kn. 10054**, but its table of contents explicitly limits
the Oryol selection to texts of **1625–1648**. Neustroy's entry is dated
7 September 1649 and therefore clearly fell outside the selection. Its
absence from the printed volume is not a negative result for the person:
the full formula of the otkaz still requires the original of entry No. 400 or another
independent copy of precisely this act.

## A Local Bridge to the Heraldry File: 14 Files of the Kursk Chamber of the Civil Court

The official name index of ГАКО (State Archive of Kursk Oblast) to fond 59, opis 2, has for the first time given not
a general recommendation but **14 exact file numbers** with the variants Anpilogov,
Anfilogov and Anpilov. The index belongs to the Kursk Chamber of the Civil
Court, whose fond covers 1780–1869.

The circle of nobles and landowners includes:

- A. N. Anpilogov, landowner and captain — d. 5078;
- G. P. Anpilogov, a landowner of Fatezh — d. 2513;
- I. M. Anpilogov, a nobleman of Rylsk — d. 6907;
- A. I. Anpilogova, a noblewoman of Fatezh — d. 5335;
- F. Anfilogov, landowner — d. 2827;
- Anfilogova without initials, landowner — d. 1644;
- A. Anfilogova, landowner — d. 1362;
- A. I. Anfilogova, a landowner of Shchigry, and I. Ya. Anfilogova,
  a noblewoman of Shchigry — one joint d. 6898.

A separate layer of odnodvortsy (single-household servitors) and peasants is represented by S. I. Anpilogov from
the village of Pol-sela Goryainova (d. 6916), Anpils of Stary Oskol district
(d. 6456) and four files of Anpilogovs of the village of Nikolskoe in Fatezh district
(dd. 6490, 4459, 5074 and 6917; the last names the initials T. I., F. I. and
K. V.).

This is not yet a generational pedigree, nor proof of origin from Oryol.
The index does not print the headings, dates, kinship or contents of the files. But it
creates an important local detour to the Senate file **РГИА, f. 1343, op. 16,
d. 2345**: civil proceedings over land, inheritance and estate status
could include deeds of purchase, partitions, old title deeds and certified extracts from
the proofs of nobility. The headings of files 2513,
5335 and 6898 should be obtained first: their Fatezh–Shchigry geography is closest to the late branch
that submitted proofs to the Heraldry Office (Geroldiya).

On the official page of ГАКО's reference apparatus the full inventory of
fond 59 is not currently disclosed: the index is published, but not the file headings.
Consequently, the next step requires an archival request or examination of the inventory
in the reading room. The request should first obtain the title, dates and size of
files 2513, 5335 and 6898, and only then order the first folios and internal
inventories. Search results consisting only of file numbers do not replace this check.

The lines have not been checked from a search snippet: the official PDF has no
text layer, so the pages were run through recognition, and all surname positions
on frames 13–15 were then read from the images. A research
card `PUB-GAKO-F59-O2-ANPILOGOV-INDEX` has been created. Initials alone in this inventory do not yet
create profiles or map entries until the dates and headings of the files themselves are obtained.

The geographical index to the same inventory added independent links.
The Rylsk nobleman I. M. Anpilogov in file 6907 is linked to **the village (selo) of Kamenevo
in Kursk district**; A. I. Anpilogova of file 5335 to **the town of Fatezh**;
the two Anfilogovas of file 6898 to **the town of Shchigry**. Files 6490 and 6917
are confirmed in the village of Nikolskoe in Fatezh district, and file 6916 is simultaneously
indexed under the village of Goryainova and under Nikolskoe. These links refine
the geography of the search but still do not replace the file headings.

### I. M. Anpilogov in Derlovo: Two Exact Court Files of 1887–1891

In a neighbouring official set it proved possible to move from the index to the full
inventory. **ГАКО, f. 32, op. 1**, the Kursk District Court, volume 1, has been checked.
On PDF frame 362 file No. 2730 is named verbatim: the case charging peasants of
Derlovo in Fatezh district **«в покушении на частную собственность дворянина
И. М. Анпилогова»** ("with an attempt on the private property of the nobleman I. M. Anpilogov"). The proceedings ran from **28 November to 12 December 1887**,
the size is **42 folios**.

On PDF frame 392 is file No. 2974: peasants of the same village were charged
**with an armed attack on the nobleman I. M. Anpilogov**. It began
on **29 February 1888**, ended on **4 July 1891** and runs to
**196 folios**. The rare surname, both initials, the noble status,
the place and the consistent property context all match. Therefore one
profile, `P99102`, two separate Records and a point for historical Derlovo
(Dernovo), present-day Derlovo in Zolotukhino district, have been created. No route to Oryol,
Rylsk or Fatezh is drawn: the man's origin is not stated.

The name index of fond 32 links one more file, No. 4868, with the same I. M., but
the available official volume of the inventory ends before this entry. It
has to be obtained separately. Identity with the Rylsk nobleman
I. M. Anpilogov of f. 59, d. 6907, has not been asserted either: the identical initials make this version
interesting, but the heading of file 6907 is still missing.

For the main sixteenth-century question this is not a direct answer but a new late node.
If the 196-folio file contains property documents, powers of attorney
or a full name, the man can be compared with the Kursk noble
genealogy (rodoslovnaya rospis) and the Senate file РГИА, f. 1343, op. 16, d. 2345. Precisely such a
comparison may show where the dictionary of 1911 got Vasily
Dmitrievich as the founder of the family.

[Official inventory of fond 32](https://archive.rkursk.ru/sites/default/files/Opisi/1_-_dorev/f32.pdf),
[page of ГАКО's reference apparatus](https://archive.rkursk.ru/gako/dorev_opisi),
[note on Derlovo (Dernovo) from the list of 1862](https://nashipredki.com/location/derlovo-dernovo-selo-39509).

### The Register of 1625: Eight Family Entries; the Ninth Turned Out to Be Filippov's

A full check of the name index and of both parts of the published register,
and then of the official digital images of the manuscript itself, has revealed eight direct
family entries: 79, 80, 81, 84, 129, 512, 523 and 1347. Previously only the four Bazdyrevo entries
had been examined in the working sample. The additional
direct entries are:

- entry 129, fol. 203 v.: Ilya, Vasily and Anton — Ivanovy deti
  Anfilogova (the sons of Ivan Anfilogov) — jointly hold land in the village of Belaya;
- entry 512, fol. 227 v.: the Sofonovo pomestye of Timofeev syn Anfilogov,
  already the pustosh Sofonovo;
- entry 523, fol. 228 v.: the Ivanovo pomestye of Nechaev syn Anpilogov
  on the Radomlya;
- entry 1347, fol. 290: Ermak's pomestye of Anpilogov in the pustosh
  Maltsovskaya.

The last three name formulas almost certainly continue the holdings of Sofon
Timofeevich, Ivan Nechaevich and Ermak Pakhomovich described in 1594/1595:
the rare full designations and the same small tracts match. Entry 129
adds a previously unexamined group of brothers and directly preserves the name of their father,
Ivan. Since Ivan Pershin Anfilogov sat in Belaya in 1594/1595,
family continuity is probable but not yet proven: «Першин» (Pershin) is a
patronymic from the name Persha, and the 1625 register does not call the brothers' father
«Иваном Першиным» ("Ivan Pershin").

An important limitation: the register records the name of the pomestye but does not always
say whether the person after whom it is named is still alive. It proves
the long duration of the family's memory of the land, not in itself a resettlement or
the uninterrupted personal service of the holder.

Initially, a complete check of the same section also produced a supposed ninth
entry: entry 1361, fol. 291, was read as **«Анфилоговское поместье Брежнева да
племянника его Курдюка в пустоши Брежневой»** ("the Anfilogov pomestye of Brezhnev and his nephew Kurdyuk in the pustosh Brezhneva"). This reading turned out to be
a false positive. The full entry of the book of 1594/1595 on printed p. 1017
names on the same plot **Filipp Stepanov syn Brezhnev** and his
nephew **Kurdyuk Petrov syn Brezhnev** in the village of Brezhneva. Within
the entry stand separately «двор помещиков Филиппов» ("the household of the pomeshchik Filipp") and «двор помещиков
Курдюков» ("the household of the pomeshchik Kurdyuk").

Five independent features match: Filipp, the surname Brezhnev, the nephew's rare name
Kurdyuk, the direct kinship and the village/pustosh Brezhneva. Therefore
the cursive form in the register should be read as **«Филипповское поместье»** ("Filippov's pomestye"), not
«Анфилоговское» ("Anfilogov's"). The entry introduces no unknown earlier Anfilog.
The Record has been kept as a negative control and furnished with both photocopies, but
excluded from the count of early occurrences of the researched stem; no profile, point or
route is created for the Brezhnevs.

At the same time the archival attribution of all five cards created from this
register has been corrected. The exact visible lines are in **РГАДА, f. 1209,
op. 85, d. 13**, while f. 1209, op. 1, kn. 330–331 are the original cadastral survey books
that the register briefly summarises. The official images have also refined
the folios: entry 512 is on fol. 227 v., entry 523 on fol. 228 v.
The official inventory clarifies the composition of the original set: kn. 330 contains
Korchakov and Nepolotsky stany, and kn. 331 Kamensky, Taychukov and Nugorsk.
Consequently, the entries for Ermak and the Brezhnevs are in kn. 331. The full
entry of 1594/1595 has already settled the dispute over the Brezhnevs; for Ermak the full
entry of 1625/26 still remains a separate task.

[Official image of fol. 291](http://rgada.info/dela/1209/1/1209-opis_13i/0294.jpg),
[public publication of the register](https://forum.vgd.ru/1851/161210/),
[printed p. 1017](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n1017/mode/1up),
[official inventory 1, part 1](http://rgada.info/opisi/1209-opis_1-1.pdf).

## The Ryazan Sotnaya of 1567/68: the Generation of Oryol's Founding Checked

In the full text of the sotnaya gramota (land-tax charter) for the Ryazan archiepiscopal deti boyarskie
the service membership immediately before the founding of Oryol has been checked. The document
covers eight stany of Ryazan district, including **Pekhlets stan**, which is especially important for
the working hypothesis. A corrected research count
gives 153 men from 80 families.

The full list of surnames contains no forms Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Onfilogov,
Ampilogov or close variants. Nor are there Bazdyrevs, Krivolapovs, Trunovs
or Nemtsovs — the closest neighbourly circle of the first Oryol book. Individual
Pakhoms and Nechays belong to other family formulas and give no kinship
link. The result has been checked against the printed publication on pp. 458–462, the digital
copy of the RSL with a full-text layer and the complete research list of
surnames.

This is a significant narrowing, but not an abandonment of the Ryazan version. The sotnaya gramota
lists the **archiepiscopal service corporation**, not all the sovereign's
deti boyarskie of Ryazan district. So now a specific path from
the archbishop's men has been ruled out, while the sovereign's service landholders
of Pekhlets, Kamensky and neighbouring stany remain the main unchecked
direction. No separate profiles or map points are created on the basis of a negative check.

[Catalogue card of the edition in the RSL](https://search.rsl.ru/ru/record/01003933946),
[digital copy](https://viewer.rsl.ru/ru/rsl01003933946),
[study of the composition of the Ryazan corporation](https://iriran.ru/sites/default/files/Busarev_diss.pdf).

## Why There Is No Sovereign's Ryazan Desyatnya: a Documented Loss of 1626

A study of the archival fate of the Ryazan service "town" has given a direct
explanation of the main gap. As late as 1625, in the course of a mestnichestvo (precedence) dispute,
**«резанскую десятню 89-го году»** ("the Ryazan desyatnya of the year [70]89"), that is, the
book of 7089 (1580/1581), was retrieved from the Razryad archive. After the Moscow fire of the following year the dyaks
were already replying to inquiries: **«резанские старые десятни в московский пожар
134-м году в Розряде згорели»** ("the old Ryazan desyatni burned in the Razryad in the Moscow fire of the year [7]134"). The reference in the study leads to РГАДА,
f. 210, op. 10, d. 46, fol. 602.

Consequently, a sovereign's name list of the generation closest to the founding of Oryol
existed, but the main set perished. This does not confirm
Anfilogovs in Ryazan; it explains why a direct negative search of
the surviving books cannot settle the question.

The search now shifts to substitute documents: extracts from desyatni in
mestnichestvo cases, kormlenye and income-and-expenditure books of the chetverti (fiscal offices),
pomestye memoranda (pamyati) and family archives. Of the Ryazan set itself only
a fragment of the review desyatnya of 1621/22 has survived; five lists of the
1610s–1620s preserved with genealogical rolls are also known. For the original
generation it is most important to find an extract from the lost desyatnya of 1580/81 or
an earlier oklad memorandum with a formula of former service.

[Full text of I. A. Kirpichnikov's dissertation](https://djvu.online/file/oZdNrhN2k13Is).

### The First Extract Found from the Lost Desyatnya: Semyon Lyapunov

The report of the loss has been traced to a specific surviving entry.
In the first volume of the official Razryad books, cols. 1053–1054, a review of
1625 is printed. The prikaz took out the old book and wrote down:

> «…служилъ съ Резани и въ Резанской десятнѣ 89-го году отецъ его Семенъ
> Іевлевъ сынъ Ляпуновъ написанъ въ помѣстномъ окладѣ въ 250 четяхъ; а онъ
> Ульянъ служитъ съ Резани жъ…»
>
> *Translation:* "…served from Ryazan, and in the Ryazan desyatnya of the year [70]89 his father Semyon
> Ievlev syn Lyapunov is written with a pomestye oklad of 250 chetverti; and he,
> Ulyan, also serves from Ryazan…"

Thus the desyatnya of 7089 (1580/1581), even a year before the fire, made it possible
to verify three things: the former service town, the father's full name and the size of his
pomestye oklad. The extract concerns Semyon Ievlevich and Ulyan Lyapunov,
not Anfilogovs, so no separate profiles or map entries are created.

For the search for origins this is nonetheless a fundamental find: it has been proved that
mestnichestvo cases preserved personal excerpts from precisely the lost
book that is needed. What is promising now is not an abstract search for "the Ryazan desyatnya" but
a complete check of the cases of 1613–1626 and later copies for the formulas
«служил с Резани» ("served from Ryazan"), «в Резанской десятне» ("in the Ryazan desyatnya") and «в поместном окладе» ("with a pomestye oklad").

[Scan of col. 1053 in Google Books](https://books.google.com/books?id=vW1BAAAAYAAJ&pg=PA1053).

## A Synchronous Check of 1596: Pavel Was an Oryol, Not a Ryazan, Novik

The loss of the old Ryazan desyatni is partly compensated by the all-state
desyatnya of noviki of 1596. A separate Ryazan section of it has been published in full:
according to the final note, 266 men of Ryazan were newly enrolled — non-serving and
landless sons, nephews and brothers of local service men.

The complete Ryazan list contains no Anfilogovs, Anpilogovs, Onfilogovs,
Ampilogovs or a close variant. Nor is there an exact match of the controlled
family formulas. Ivan Romanov syn Mikulin, Mikita Nechaev syn Likhachev,
Fyodor and Sava Grigoryevy deti Pafomovy and Terentey Belyaev syn Panfilov are
other people and other name sequences.

The special value of the result lies in its synchronicity: **Pavel Terentyevich Anfilogov
is recorded in the same document, but under the Oryol heading**. So in April
1596 his service town is already unambiguously Oryol. This does not establish
Pavel's place of birth or the former service of his father Terenty: the older
generation could have moved earlier. But Pavel can no longer be presented as a Ryazan novik
who was transferred to Oryol in 1596.

Source: РГАДА, f. 210, op. 4, d. 120/167; N. P. Likhachev, «Десятни
новиков, поверстанных в 1596 году» ("Desyatni of the Noviki Enrolled in 1596") // Izvestiya Russkogo genealogicheskogo
obshchestva (Proceedings of the Russian Genealogical Society). Issue 3. St Petersburg, 1909. Pp. 113–209.

[Publication of the Ryazan section and note on the desyatni](https://www.62info.ru/history/node/12182).

### Two Families Divided between Ryazan and Oryol in One List

The full Ryazan section has additionally been compared with all extractable
holder formulas of the Oryol book of 1594/95. There is no exact full name of
one person in both towns, but two combinations have been found in which both the father and the surname
match at once:

- **Naum Ivanov syn Bolotov**, a Ryazan novik with an oklad of 100 chetverti, —
  **Timofey Ivanov syn Bolotov**, an Oryol pomeshchik in the village of Bolotovo on
  the Lisitsa. Together with Vasily Ivanovich Bolotov in Kashira and Mitya
  Ivanovich Bolotov in Tula this is already a four-town hypothesis of the sons of one
  Ivan Bolotov.
- **Vasily Ivanov syn Kamenev**, a Ryazan novik with an oklad of 100 chetverti,
  — **Ivan Ivanov syn Kamenev** and **Terekh Ivanov syn Kamenev**, two
  independent Oryol pomeshchiki. In Ivan's entry the household of
  his father Ivan is named separately, which confirms not a misread patronymic but
  a real older generation in Oryol.

The sources do not call the men brothers, so an individual route has not been
proven. However, two independent matches show that the division of branches
between Ryazan and Oryol was not merely theoretically possible. For the origin
of Anfilogovs this strengthens the model of a common Ryazan–Upper Oka family
reservoir, but does not replace a record of the surname. The Bolotovs and Kamenevs get no
profiles or routes on the family map.

## The Chetvert Books after the Time of Troubles: One Oryol Man Traced from 1594 to 1617

The edition by L. M. Sukhotin, «Четвертчики Смутного времени
(1604–1617)» ("Chetvertchiki of the Time of Troubles, 1604–1617"), has been checked in full. This is not a general desyatnya but a record of a narrower group of service men
who were paid a money salary from the chetverti. So the absence of the surname
cannot be turned into a denial of the whole family's Oryol service.

Three Oryol men have been found in the corpus:

- **Voin Kiprianov syn Vorobyov**: on 29 August 1614 his
  old oklad of 18 roubles was confirmed and four roubles were added for the Mtsensk service of 1612/13
  and 1613/14;
- **Mikhail Afanasyev syn Zhilin**: on 14 September 1613 his
  nine-rouble oklad from the time of Tsar Vasily was restored, because the corresponding entries
  of the book of 1610 «в разоренье пропали» ("were lost in the devastation");
- **Grigory Ivanov syn Sotnikov**: on 3 January 1617 his former oklad of
  six roubles was restored according to the Oryol list that Pyotr Danilov
  had sent from Oryol in 1613/14.

The last entry gives an exact match between books. In the cadastral survey book
of 1594/95 **Grisha Ivanov syn Sotnikov** already held his father's pomestye in
a village on the Gosudarnino selishche together with his brothers Kondrat, Oleshka and
Antonko — «Ивановыми детьми Сотникова» ("the sons of Ivan Sotnikov"). The first name, father, surname and
Oryol service corporation all match. An interval of 22 years is normal, so we
are almost certainly dealing with one man: a pomeshchik of 1594/95, a pre-war
chetvertchik under Vasily Shuysky, a participant in the Oryol list of 1613/14 and
the recipient of a restored oklad in 1617.

For our task the mechanism is what matters. After the devastation the prikaz restored
lost entries by means of **razryad searches (sysk)** and newly sent town
lists. So one must look not only for the lost desyatni, but also for petitions for
the restoration of salary, search materials and Pyotr Danilov's list itself.
The full list may have included Oryol deti boyarskie who did not fall within the narrow
circle of chetvertchiki.

The forms Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Onfilogov, Ampilogov and Onpilogov are absent from the complete
edition. For Voin Vorobyov and Mikhail Zhilin there are no exact matches in
the Oryol book of 1594/95 either; identical surnames are not merged
without a match of the full name. Other surnames get no profiles or points on
the family map.

[Digital copy of the 1912 edition](https://archive.org/details/chetvertchikismu00sukh).

### The Large Distribution Book of 1615–1618: the Modern Reference Found

The printed inventory of the Moscow Archive of the Ministry of Justice revealed the next,
much broader source. Under No. 1 of the books of the Novgorod desk are described
**distribution books of money salary for 1615–1618 on 226 folios**.
They cover Cossacks, streltsy and other service men of Novgorod, Belyov,
Bolkhov, **Oryol**, Kromy, Novgorod-Seversky, Tikhvin, Volokolamsk, Vyazma
and Mozhaysk.

The modern archival address has been established as **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6v, d. 1**.
The correspondence is supported by the present title of the inventory — «Книги Новгородского
стола» ("Books of the Novgorod desk"), its end date of 1615 and independent descriptions of name-by-name distributions
of 1617 that refer directly to this file.

The official РГАДА catalogue describes opis 6v as a set of 79 books for
1615–1704, and its digital inventory begins precisely with file No. 1. A modern
name-by-name project labels the processed layer of this file **«Дворяне и
дети боярские разных городов 1617 г.»** ("Dvoryane and deti boyarskie of various towns, 1617"). This explains the apparent discrepancy:
the project copied out only six towns — Yaroslavl, Arzamas, Alatyr, Tula,
Kashira and Ryazan — whereas the archival heading of the whole file is considerably broader.
Consequently, the absence of Oryol from the open table shows the limit of the
sample, not the composition of the original book.

The next layer of checking has been done using the official four-volume РГАДА
alphabetical index to the books of the Novgorod desk — **indexes 115–118**. Index 115
covers books Nos. 1–12 and is fully open: 291 digital images. For
book No. 1 the section of surnames in **A** occupies images 0003–0005, the section in
**O** images 0169–0170. In both sections the spellings
Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Ampilogov, Onfilogov and Onpilogov have been checked; they are absent. Thus
this is no longer a null web search but a negative result from the official
archival index to the right book.

The number of folios has been checked directly on the image of p. 140 of the printed inventory:
it reads **«226 л.»** ("226 fols."). The earlier reading "2926" arose from OCR, which merged
a dash with the number. The same size and modern reference are independently repeated in
a thematic list of files for Oryol district on the ВГД forum. Thanks to this
the search has been reduced from the mistakenly assumed thousands to 226 folios.

The limit of the result is significant. The index does not replace an examination of the 226 folios and
may not cover an entry without a surname, one read unusually or one omitted.
Moreover, the open secondary export of the file is clearly incomplete: it has 395
lines of 1617 — Yaroslavl 183, Alatyr 67, Ryazan 49, Kashira 43, Tula 38
and Arzamas 15, but **not a single Oryol line**. So no negative conclusion about Oryol
can be drawn from it.

An additional targeted check has been made through **Grigory Ivanov syn
Sotnikov**. He is convenient as a control Oryol man: a pomeshchik with this full name
can be traced from the book of 1594/95 to the restoration of his salary according to
the Oryol list of 1613/14. In the official index 115 the section "S" for books Nos. 1–12,
images 0211–0233, has been examined in full. Sotnikov
is not there. Consequently, the distribution book of 1615–1618 cannot be regarded either as
a copy or as a full continuation of Pyotr Danilov's list. The absence from it of
Anfilogovs and of Sotnikov is now a characteristic of the composition of the source,
not an indication that these Oryol families disappeared.

The updated public Google spreadsheet of the service-men project has also been checked. For
the exact reference **210-6в-1** it returns 396 lines of the same six towns and does not
contain a hidden Oryol section. A full-text search for variants of the surname across
the whole current database did not produce a single new pre-Oryol bearer: the
earliest additional layer begins with Oryol entries of 1626 already
known to the project. A search for Anfilog, Anpilog, Anfilof and Onfilog in the position of
a personal name also gave zero lines. This does not replace an archival examination, but
it closes off the assumption that the early settler is already in the modern
open export under a personal name.

Nor can the book be automatically identified with Pyotr Danilov's list
of 1613/14: the set begins in 1615. The next targeted step is to
establish the Oryol folios of the file itself and check the formulas of old oklad,
former service, the father's entry and restoration after the devastation.

A dangerous lead with an identical name has been removed separately. **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1,
d. 1059**, called in the modern inventory the "inspection book (dozornaya kniga) of Oryol district
of 1617–1619", relates to the Vyatka satellite town of **Orlov** and goes together with
Khlynov district. The full Vyatka publication confirms that the book
describes tax-paying and quit-rent villages of the Vyatka land. It must not be attached to the fortress of Oryol on the Oka or
to the Oryol deti boyarskie.

The internet index to the Oryol review of 1620/21 has also been rechecked separately.
Its lines **«Марк Анпилогов» ("Mark Anpilogov") and «Илюшка Онпилогов» ("Ilyushka Onpilogov") are false**: they are absent from the full
index and from the digital file examined. The genuine bearer of the surname on
fol. 67 is **Vasiley Dmitreev Anpilogov**, already entered in the project as Vasily
Dmitrievich. No new profiles are created from the two erroneous lines.

[Exact digital spread of the printed inventory, p. 140](https://archive.org/download/9_20211219_202112/%D0%A2%D0%BE%D0%BC%209_jp2.zip/%D0%A2%D0%BE%D0%BC%209_jp2/%D0%A2%D0%BE%D0%BC%209_0256.jp2).
[List of files for Oryol district with the same reference and size](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/1851/142603/p4781056.htm#pp4773435).
[Official Index 115](http://rgada.info/poisk/index2.php?str=210-ukaz_115),
[start of section A of book No. 1](http://rgada.info/opisi/210-ukaz_115/0003.jpg),
[start of section O of book No. 1](http://rgada.info/opisi/210-ukaz_115/0169.jpg),
[continuation of section O](http://rgada.info/opisi/210-ukaz_115/0170.jpg).

## 1570: Oryol Men Were Already Marching Out «по их спискам» ("by Their Lists")

The full text of the «Разрядная книга 1475–1598 годов» (Razryad Book of 1475–1598) has given earlier direct
evidence of a personal register of the Oryol corps. In the roster of 7075
(1566/67) Oryol is included for the first time among the Ukrainian (frontier) towns: the
voivode Prince Vasily Rostovsky and Vladimir Bezobrazov were posted there. This entry on
p. 224 names only the command.

The roster of 7078 (1570) is considerably more informative. At Oryol it names the voivode
Prince Andrey Vasilyevich Repnin and the golova (commander) Semyon Mikhaylov syn Khotunsky. In
case of a threatened attack they were to march out of Oryol and join the great
regiment together with the other skhodnye (joining) voivodes of the Ukrainian towns. The order
ends with a fundamental formula: **«а люди с ними по их списком»** ("and the men with them according to their lists").

This is not merely an assumption that a list must once have
existed. The document shows the list in action: by it the contingent
was led out of the town and incorporated into the field army. Consequently, no later than
1570 Oryol already had a registered local service corporation. The window of
initial recruitment narrows to **1566/67–1570** — earlier than the general
numerical register of early 1572 and the special name lists
prescribed by the muster of 1577.

The Sovereign's Razryad does not name the rank and file themselves: it preserves the command framework
and leaves the personal town rosters as separate documents. The formula
applies to all the listed Ukrainian commanders and does not give the exact
title of the Oryol attachment. Therefore the presence of Anfilogovs in the list of
1570 has not yet been proven, but it is now known which document to look for.

The new archival target is not only the list of the 1577 muster, but also counterpart copies (protivni),
extracts and later references to the rosters of **7075–7078**. Search formulas:
«люди по их спискам» ("men by their lists"), «с Орла по списку» ("from Oryol by the list"), «роспись украинных городов» ("roster of the Ukrainian towns") and
«сходные воеводы» ("joining voivodes"). Especially important are later land disputes, in which holders
might quote old razryad and pomestye extracts after the loss of the main
books.

The main text of the edition has been checked against the digital copy: pp. 224 and 231–232.
Card: `PUB-RK-1570-OREL-PEOPLE-BY-LISTS`.
[Digital copy of the Razryad Book](https://archive.org/details/buganov-razrjadnaja-kniga-1475-1598).

## 1565: The First Pre-Oryol Upper Oka Onanyin Found in Odoev

Switching the search unit from the ready-made surname Anfilogov to full personal
formulas, early second names and neighbouring families produced the first find
in the immediate pre-Oryol layer. In a scholarly publication of a sotnaya (extract) from the
Odoev cadastral books dated **25 May 1565**, among the monastery peasants of the
small village of Martinovskoye in Pavlovsky stan, the household of **Stepanko Onanyin** is recorded.
The document is one year older than the founding of Oryol.

The editors found the original sotnaya in a later case file on the lawsuit of the stryapchy
I. P. Voeykov against the Nastasov Monastery over hay meadows on the Losinskaya wasteland in
Pogorelsky stan of Belyov district. The archival address of the whole complex is given
in the article as **ЦГАДА, ф. 1209, столбцы по Москве, № 32739, ч. 1–2**; today this is
РГАДА. The text of the sotnaya begins on fol. 88 of the case. Publication: V. Yu. Belikov,
E. I. Kolycheva, "Documents on the Landholding of the Princes Vorotynsky in the Second
Half of the 16th and Early 17th Centuries", *Arkhiv russkoy istorii*, 1992, issue 2,
document no. 1 on p. 100 ff.

The find matters not because it sounds like Anfilogov directly but because of a narrower link.
In the Oryol book of 1594/95 the Onanyins already form a separate service
nest, and within it the former holder is named as **Anpilog Ivanov syn
Onanyina**; the document allows three generations to be reconstructed: Anpilog → his
son Vavil (not named in the article) → grandson Kondraty Vavilovich. Now the form
Onanyin has for the first time been found in the Upper Oka lands immediately before
the creation of Oryol. Odoev therefore becomes a concrete search address for the early
milieu of the name, and not just one of the neighbouring old towns.

This is still not a route. Stepanko is a monastery peasant, whereas
the Oryol Onanyins are service landholders; his patronymic and descendants are not
named. There is no match of the full name, no kinship formula, no record
of enrolment or resettlement. Moreover, the sotnaya describes only the holdings
of the Nastasov Monastery and their boundaries, not the whole of Odoev district. No
Anfilogovs or variants, no personal Anfilogs/Anfilofs, and no Bazdyrevs, Nemtsovs,
Trunovs or Krivolapovs from the Oryol neighbourhood cluster were found in it; for a limited extract
this is a weak negative result.

The next targeted search is the remaining parts of the Odoev survey of 1565/66
and the pomestye entries outside the monastery sotnaya. The main test
formula: **Vavil Anpilogov syn Onanyin** and its spelling variants. No separate profile
or map point is created for Stepanko: he bears an
unrelated name and is so far only a comparative mention.

[Digital copy of the issue of *Arkhiv russkoy istorii*](https://imwerden.de/pdf/archiv_russkoj_istorii_vypusk2_1992__ocr.pdf).

## A Change of Tactics: Searching Not for a Surname but for the Set of the First Oryol Men

The mass search for the spellings Anfilogov, Anpilogov, Onpilogov, Onfilogov,
Ampilogov and Anfilofov in open indexes has been exhausted: it did not lead back beyond the known
Oryol layer. This is to be expected for the 16th century, when one and the same
family name could still be written as a patronymic or a nickname, or not
make it into an abridged index at all. Repeating the same search under new
variants is no longer the main line of work.

The new search unit is **the formation of the service corporation of the new Oryol between
1566/67 and 1570**. By 1570 men were already setting out from the town "по их
спискам" ("according to their lists"), so the original register was compiled in a very narrow
four-year window. One must look not only for the list itself but for the whole
administrative trail that produced it: the order to recruit, the appointment of scribes, the muster,
the oklad entries, the first pomestye grants and later extracts from these papers.

The search is divided into three lines:

1. **Administrative.** Who built Oryol, who recruited its first
   servicemen and settled them on pomestye land, which scribes and clerks carried out the verdict of 15 March 1577
   and which other towns were part of their mission. The books of one scribe
   may have survived under the name of a neighbouring town, even though the Oryol part
   is lost or not named in the title.
2. **Family.** Instead of a single surname, the seven fathers of the early
   Oryol branches are checked — Mikula, two Vasilys not yet merged into one,
   Persha, Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty. The strongest identifier is the rare
   simultaneous trio of brothers **Vasily, Timofey and Karp, sons of Mikula**.
3. **Neighbourhood.** Anfilogovs of the village of Bazdyrevo are searched for together with
   the Bazdyrevs, Trunovs, Nemtsovs and Krivolapovs. The joint appearance of
   several families in an old district is more distinctive than a match of one surname
   and may reveal not an individual settler but a group from the original
   land allotment.

The standard of proof remains strict. A Mikula or Onanyin of the same name is
only a search hint. A route can be added to the map only after a
formula such as «переведён из» ("transferred from"), «служил прежде» ("formerly served"), the name of the former town, a direct
transfer of an old grant, or an unambiguous kinship record. Therefore
Stepanko Onanyin of 1565 remains an important Odoev research address
but does not receive a family route.

## Epifan, 1572: An Exact Analogue of the First Oryol List Found

For Epifan, settled in the same year 7075/1566–1567, precisely the
type of document that Oryol lacks has survived: a description of the new ostrog (stockade), the posad (trading quarter),
the strelets and Cossack slobodas with households listed by name. The modern archival address
has been established from the official inventory: **РГАДА, f. 137, op. 1, Venyov, d. 1, ch. 2,
ll. 201–247**. The book was written by Prince Ivan Vasilyevich Mosalsky and Grigory
Borisovich Yartsov with their associates. The same commission described Venyov, so
the Epifan part has survived inside a file under the top-level heading "Venyov".

The summary formula reports that the people settled in Epifan under Prince Ivan
Fyodorovich Mstislavsky with a ten-year exemption in the year 7075. The part listing names
shows a mixed intake literally: **Васка Карачевец**, **Васка и Гриша
Резанцовы**, **Онанья Дедиловский**, **Васка Крапивенский**, **Истомка
Коломнетин**, **Васюк Медынец** (Vaska of Karachev, Vaska and Grisha of Ryazan, Onanya of Dedilov,
Vaska of Krapivna, Istomka of Kolomna, Vasyuk of Medyn). Next to them stand men marked «новоприход»,
«новоприходец» and «приходец» ("newcomer", "new arrival"). Empty places were left for the households of Cossacks
who were still to be "прибрать" ("recruited"). So the new town was staffed not
by a single resettlement of a ready-made corporation but by continuing recruitment from several
old towns.

### Mikulka Moiseev — the First Targeted Candidate by the Rare Family Formula

On **fol. 214 v.**, in the Korobyina sloboda, in the hundred of Stepan Likharev, a
rank-and-file Cossack **Микулка Мосеев** (Mikulka Moseev) is recorded. The diminutive form Mikulka does not mean
a child; this is how scribes usually recorded adult servicemen. Moseev is a
patronymic from Moisey, not a ready-made surname.

By generation this man is compatible with the Mikula whose three adult sons —
Vasily, Timofey and Karp Mikulichi Anfilogov — jointly held their father's
zhrebiy (share) in Oryol district in 1594/95. But so far only the rare name of the
father matches. The Epifan book does not call Mikulka an Anfilogov, does not list his
sons and says nothing of a transfer to Oryol. He is therefore a candidate for targeted
checking, not a found ancestor.

In the Epifan desyatnya (muster roll) of 1585 Mikulka Moiseev is no longer present. A new line-by-line
check does not allow this to be counted as a special migration signal. Of all
the **fourteen** men in his row of 1572, only the rare Malets Ondreev
reliably recurs in 1585; almost all the others likewise disappear or
have only overly general name matches. So the desyatnya enrolled a selection of
Cossacks rather than repeating the whole former complement. Mikulka might have died, dropped out,
changed service or not been included in the enrolment, but his absence does not by itself
make the route Epifan → Oryol more likely.

For Arkhipko Onanyin the earlier comparison was wrong. He is recorded as a **foot
strelets** (musketeer), and the desyatnya of 1585 is not a census of streltsy. Therefore his
absence from it means neither death, nor dropping out, nor resettlement. Arkhip
should be sought in strelets rosters, money and grain distributions and surety
records, without mixing them with Cossack desyatni. Those named in 1585, Anton
Evseev syn Onanyin and Persha Onanyin syn Levontyev, are different full formulas; for
Persha the word Onanyin is a patronymic and Levontyev is the family name.

### Arkhipko Onanyin — Another Early Upper Oka Onanyin

On **fol. 209**, among the foot streltsy of the Great Street of the Epifan Strelets
sloboda, **Arkhipko Onanyin** is recorded. After Stepanko Onanyin of Odoev
in 1565, this is the second pre-Oryol Upper Oka bearer of the form Onanyin.
Later in Oryol there is a separate service nest of Onanyins, within which
Anpilog Ivanov syn Onanyina is named as a former holder.

Kinship has not been established: Arkhip was a strelets, Stepanko of Odoev a
monastery peasant, and the Oryol Onanyins pomestye holders. But now the form
Onanyin has been encountered at two old Upper Oka points immediately before and
soon after the founding of Oryol. This makes the **Odoev — Epifan — Oryol** corridor
a meaningful search zone, though not yet a family route.

There is also a cautious neighbourhood signal. In the same Epifan Strelets sloboda
**Ivanko Ovsyannikov** is recorded. In Oryol district in 1594/95 there are also
well-developed circles of Onanyins and Ovsyannikovs. The joint recurrence of
two names is stronger than a chance match of one name, but the full persons and
patronymics differ. For now this is a sign of a common Upper Oka recruiting
reservoir.

### Which Intermediate Books Actually Existed

K. V. Baranov's catalogue of cadastral books narrowed the next search to one survey
cycle. In **1584/85** Elizary Rzhevsky and the dyak (secretary) Semeyka Pakhomov surveyed
the Venyov and Epifan districts together. The Epifan book is attested by extracts
printed in *Akty sluzhilykh zemlevladeltsev* (Acts of Service Landholders), vol. III, nos. 65 and 445;
the Venyov one by nos. 317 and 318. At the same time Konstantin Naumov compiled the Dedilov
book, known from no. 254 of the same volume.

This does not yet mean that the full Epifan original of 1584/85 has survived:
the catalogue records the book from later references in acts and from extracts. But instead of
an unbounded search "somewhere between 1572 and 1585" there is now a precise
corpus: the Rzhevsky–Pakhomov extracts, transfers of Cossack grants and separate
strelets rosters of the same town. The main methodological conclusion is to stop
treating absence from someone else's service book as a sign of resettlement.

### The Varvarins: A Control Chain from the First Cossack to a Brother's Pomestye

Two extracts turned out to be more than bibliographic references. Act no. 65 makes it possible
to test on a real family the whole method that is needed for the Oryol
Anfilogovs.

In the Ulanova Cossack sloboda in 1572 **Senka Varvarin** is recorded. In the desyatnya
of 1585, under no. 283, stands **Ievko Semyonov syn Varvarin** — one of the Cossacks
enrolled as deti boyarskie. In the repeat desyatnya of 1591 the same Ievko, under
no. 310, is marked **«взят в полон»** ("taken captive").

A vvoznaya gramota (charter of entry into possession) of September 1591 reveals the missing links. **Kondrashko
Semyonov syn Varvarin** directly calls Iev his own brother. Iev was seized by the
Crimean men during the "царёв приход" (the khan's invasion) of 1591; he left a wife,
Annitsa, and a daughter Ulyanitsa, aged a year and a half. Kondrat was temporarily given his brother's
pomestye with the obligation to serve and to support his sister-in-law and niece, and, if Iev
returned from captivity, to give him back the land together with the income and grain.

The act quotes verbatim the Epifan book of Elizary Rzhevsky and Semeyka Pakhomov
of 1584/85: Ievko's zhrebiy in the Rozhdestvenskaya sloboda comprised 10
chetverti of ploughland and 11 chetverti of fallow per field. The modern address of the charter:
**РГАДА, f. 1209, stolbtsy po Tule, no. 365/37572, fol. 77**; publication — АСЗ,
vol. III, no. 65.

**Names and meaning.** Senka is Semyon, Ievko is Iev, Kondrashko is Kondrat. The formula
"Semyonov syn Varvarin" gives both the father's name and the family name at once.
Senka Varvarin of 1572 is with high probability the father of Iev and
Kondrat: the town, surname, generation and named father all match. But a literal
phrase "Senka, father of Iev and Kondrat" does not yet exist, so the senior link remains
a strong identification. The brotherhood of Iev and Kondrat is proven by the act itself.
"Царегородский полоненик" ("captive of Tsargrad") attached to Kondrat describes his own experience or
status as a returned captive, not the place of the family's origin.

For Mikulka this yields an exact search scheme: **first Cossack → children with a patronymic →
transfer of the father's or brother's pomestye**. The main search
formulas therefore become not only "Mikulka Moiseev" but also **Vasily Mikulin,
Timofey Mikulin, Karp Mikulin, Mikula's children, father's pomestye, brother's
pomestye**. A complete check of the published 16th-century desyatni and of the full volume
АСЗ III has not yet produced this trio outside Oryol; this is a negative result for the
surviving corpus, not proof of local origin.

There is also a control taken directly from Mikulka's row. All fourteen
formulas of this row were checked: Kuzma Yuryev, Fetko Ievlev, Ivanko Ivanov, Ignatko
Istomin, Filatko Gavrilov, Ermolka Ivanov, Olekseyko Ivanov, Malets Ondreev,
Mikulka Moseev, Pronya Rodinov, Alistratko Ivanov, Ivanko Olekseev, Mikhalko
Fyodorov and Kuzemka Ivanov. Only the rare **Malets Ondreev**,
who stood next to him in 1572, recurs in 1585 under no. 265 as **Malets
Ondreev syn Babin**. This is almost certainly the same man, but the later record
added the family name Babin. Consequently, matching a stable
pair "personal name + father" really can find a person before
his family name appears in the source. At the same time, one recurrence out of
fourteen shows why Mikulka's absence cannot be turned into
proof of resettlement. In 1591 Malets is already absent; the reason is not
given.

Card of the control chain:
`PUB-EPIFAN-1572-1591-VARVARIN-FAMILY-LAND-CHAIN`. No profiles or map entries
are added: the Varvarins and Babins do not belong to the researched surname series,
and the documents show no resettlement to Oryol.

Card:
`PUB-RGADA-137-1-VENEV-1-2-EPIFAN-FIRST-SETTLERS-1572`.
[Full folio-by-folio transcription](https://document.wikireading.ru/atbRCXiagc),
[official inventory of fond 137](http://rgada.info/opisi/137-opis_1m.pdf),
[digital copy of the 1877 edition](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia).
Profiles and the map do not change: no record yet links a specific
bearer of the researched name with a move from Epifan to Oryol.

## A Second Change of Tactics: Not One "Clan" but Seven Oryol Branches

The book of 1594/95 does not show one family that suddenly arrived in Oryol.
The eight early entries name at least seven different fathers: **Mikula,
Vasily, Persha, Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty**; and the two Vasilyevichi
have not even been proven to be brothers. So an identical name could already
unite several branches, or could have arisen independently from the personal name
Anfilog. Looking for one common town of origin for all of them is too strong an
assumption.

Each branch is now handled as a separate case. For Mikula, the rare
trio of sons Vasily—Timofey—Karp is sought; for Osip Vasilyevich, a separate charter
on the transfer of the former pomestye of Onokha Krivolapov; for Ivan Pershin and Ermak
Pakhomov, rare pairs of first name and patronymic; for Pavel Terentyevich, a town
razbornaya kniga (muster review book), a surety or a land act naming his father Terenty. Only
after a common senior person is found can branches be merged.

In parallel, a separate atlas of the personal name **Anfilog / Anfilof** of the
15th–16th centuries is being compiled: Ryazan, Kashin, Yaroslavl, the Dvina, Tver and Oryol district itself.
This is not a map of ancestors. Its task is to show where the name actually occurred and where
patronymic names could have arisen from it independently. No such
person is declared an ancestor of Oryol Anfilogovs without a transitional record.

The main archival turn is from short name lists to land files.
The full Oryol otkaznaya kniga (book of land conveyances) of 1619–1701 is held under the historical
call number **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 199, kn. 10054**. The scholarly edition
*Pamyatniki yuzhnovelikorusskogo narechiya. Otkaznye knigi* (Moscow, 1977) prints
from it only acts selected on linguistic grounds; in the published selection
Anfilogovs and Bazdyrevo were not found. This is not a negative check of the manuscript.
The full volume remains a priority, because it is precisely conveyances (otkazy) and allotments (otdelnye) that usually
name the former holder, a deceased father, a brother, a widow, children and the
old sovereign's grant.

The practical order is now: **the first serviceman without a stable
surname → his children by patronymic → transfer of the father's or brother's pomestye
→ only then a route**. The Varvarin control chain in Epifan proved
that this method works. For Anfilogovs the necessary last link has not yet been
found, so it is premature to complete the map of origin.

### The Chancery Trail of the 1577 Muster

Another way has appeared to look for the lost Oryol list: not by surname and
not only by the word "Oryol", but through the people through whose hands the document passed between
the Razryad (Military Office) and the Pomestny prikaz (Service Land Office). A scholarly reference work on the prikazy names in the
Pomestny prikaz in 1577 Andrey Sherefedinov, Vasily Koludarov, Andrey
Sudokov-Myasnoy and Bogdan Ksenofontov. The last is documented there from
14 January 1577 to 30 September 1578 — exactly the probable window for
carrying out the March verdict on the Oryol muster and the addition of land.

The preface to the first volume of *Akty Moskovskogo gosudarstva* (Acts of the Muscovite State) at first gave
a new archival target: **thirty stolbtsy (scroll files) expressly called lists of service
people** — nos. 14, 17, 19, 21–24, 29, 31–33, 36–37, 41–45, 49, 53, 55, 57,
61–63, 70, 78, 91, 95 and 103. However, checking them in full against the official inventory 9
of fond 210 immediately cut off a false path: all thirty units are dated
**1616–1648**.

The oldest list of 1577, printed in АМГ as no. 26, is not among
them but in **delo no. 1, stolpik 3**. The official inventory on p. 3 describes it
with complete clarity: **«7085/1576–77 гг. Список боярский. — 36 л.»** ("7085/1576–77. Boyar list. — 36 fols."). This is a general
court list of selected servicemen, not an Oryol annex to the verdict on the Cossacks.
Photocopies of pp. 2–3 of the inventory are kept in the card. Thus neither the general list
no. 26 nor the thirty later list scrolls are the sought-for
roster of the Oryol men by name; their check can be closed.

This is now confirmed not only by the inventory and the printed edition but also by the
official digital original itself, **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6, d. 1**. The Oryol
entry begins on fol. 235 and continues on fols. 235 v.–236; the order to
compile separate name lists is on fols. 241 v.–242. Fol. 243 v.
concludes the verdict, fol. 244 is blank, and a different document begins on fol. 245.
Consequently, the sought-for roster was not attached to the verdict in this book.
Five control spreads of the manuscript are kept in the card
`PUB-AMG-T1-N23-OREL-KAZAKI-1577`; the `openScan` link now leads directly to
the [official folio-by-folio РГАДА copy](http://rgada.info/kueh/index2.php?str=210_6_1).

The neighbouring act **АМГ, vol. I, no. 21** is especially important. It shows the live route
of the paperwork just one day before the verdict we are interested in. On 2 March a pamyat (memorandum) about the
Putivl and Rylsk deti boyarskie was sent from the Razryad **«к Ондрею
Мясному да к Богдану Ксенофонтьеву»** ("to Ondrey Myasnoy and to Bogdan Ksenofontyev"); on 14 March the pamyat and an extract
came back from the Pomestny prikaz **«за приписью диака Ондрея Мясново»** ("countersigned by the dyak Ondrey Myasnoy"). On 15 March
the verdict on the review of the Ukraine (frontier) Cossacks and the Oryol deti
boyarskie was adopted. So the Oryol executive pamyat or its duplicate (protiven) may have been
registered under the name of one of these dyaks, rather than under a separate
heading "Oryol list".

The reading has been checked against the official folio-by-folio copy **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6,
d. 1, fols. 223–226**. At the same time an important error was found and corrected:
Vasily Koludarov had been inserted into this phrase from the general staff list of
the Pomestny prikaz, but he is not in act no. 21 itself. The date for Andrey Sherefedinov,
27 February 1577, in the reference work goes back to an unrelated Poshekhonye charter,
АСЗ, vol. I, no. 305; Koludarov is confirmed by another act, АСЗ, vol. III, no. 250.
Finally, Veselovsky's dates for Myasnoy and Ksenofontov rest on the same act
no. 21, not on a separate Oryol file. So there is only one documentarily proven
link here: **Myasnoy — Ksenofontov**.

Act no. 21 itself has now been moved into a separate control card,
`PUB-AMG-T1-N21-PUTIVL-RYLSK-PROCEDURE-1577`. It records the full set of
papers that arose: the pamyat, the pomestye extract, the name list, the expenditure books
and the surety records. The field executor Grigory Lobodimsky distributed money in
Putivl and Rylsk and returned the books and surety records to the Razryad. For Oryol this is not
an identity of persons but an exact procedural model: the next main unknown is the
name of the man to whom the Oryol lists were handed for execution on the spot.

For Oryol itself the first equally precise name marker has now appeared.
The razryadnaya kniga (service register) reports that **in April 1577** the voivodes there were
Roman Dmitriyevich Buturlin and **Grigory Fyodorov Andreeva-Pavlov**. At the
field assembly both are again named "с Орла" ("from Oryol") in the vanguard regiment. The contemporaneous
boyar list АМГ no. 26 places Grigory in the section **«Кашира»** (Kashira) and gives him
the note **«на Орле»** ("at Oryol"). This is no longer general historical background but a documented
case of a commanding officer from the old Kashira service
corporation being sent to Oryol immediately after the verdict of 15 March.

The reading has a caveat that must not be hidden: the main redaction calls him
«Григорей Федоров ... сын Павлов», whereas list O at the first mention
gives «Дмитреев». The repeated line of the field assembly and the independent form
«Григорей Федоров Ондреева-Павлов» in the boyar list support the reading
**Fyodorov**, but the variant **Dmitreev** is kept in the card. The card also notes a
predecessor: in 1575/76 Ivan Timofeyevich Fustov served in Oryol, also
registered in 1577 under Kashira. His Oryol service cannot be extended to 15 March
1577: the later formula «воеводы по прежней росписи» ("voivodes as per the previous roster") does not repeat the
names.

The limit of the conclusion is fundamental. The documents prove a **Kashira → Oryol channel for
a commanding officer**, but not the transfer of rank-and-file Kashira deti boyarskie,
Cossacks or families. Grigory Pavlov is not named as the scribe of the muster. So no
route is created for Anfilogovs, but Roman Buturlin and Grigory Pavlov become
new targeted keys to the voivodes' reports (otpiski) of March–summer and the papers of the
field executor. The result has been placed in the control card
`PUB-RK-AMG-1575-1577-OREL-KASHIRA-COMMAND`.

For Buturlin the preceding rotation has also been reconstructed directly from the
razryad records: in 1575/76 he was voivode of Novosil, in autumn 1576
of Bolkhov, in April 1577 of Oryol. This makes the Upper Oka frontier
belt not merely a probable geography but a documented circuit of movement of
commanding officers. But it is still the route of one voivode, not of his subordinates
and not of the Anfilogov family.

The targeted search now runs by the combination of these two names, the dates March 1577 —
September 1578 and the service formulas **старые
20 четвертей, придача 30 четвертей, всего 50 четвертей, смотр на лицо,
месячные сторожи** ("old 20 chetverti, an addition of 30 chetverti, 50 chetverti in all, muster in person,
monthly watches"). On the map this changes nothing yet: a chancery
chain has been found, but not the former town of a single Anfilogov. Andrey Sudokov-Myasnoy
likewise cannot automatically be identified with Ivan Nikitich
Sudokov-Myasnoy, the later Oryol town golova (commander): the personal names
differ, and kinship is not proven here.

S. B. Veselovsky's reference work gives the signatory's full biography: he is **Andrey
Nikitich Sudokov-Myasnoy**, in 1549 a syn boyarsky "у коня" ("at the horse"), in 1550 a
Tula syn boyarsky of the third grade and a tysyachnik (one of the Thousand), then an envoy to Crimea and a dyak of the
Pomestnaya izba. The Tula biography is important against the background of the already found
Tula–Krapivna neighbourhood trail, but does not by itself mean that Tula men were recruited
into Oryol: the verdict ordered the selection of men already in Oryol service.

There is also an almost detective-story coincidence. The Tver cadastral book of 1539/40, on the
folios with Onfilogovo, was countersigned by **дьяк Андрей Мяснов** (the dyak Andrey Myasnov). It is not yet known whether
this is the same Andrey Nikitich: there is no patronymic in the book's heading, 38 years
separate the documents, and Veselovsky's reference work does not include the Tver countersignature in his biography.
So the attractive story "one dyak saw Onfilogovo and then distributed
Oryol lands" is not yet proven — and even a proven identity of the official would
not be proof of the resettlement of the inhabitants. The lead is kept for comparing
original countersignatures and the early service biography, without a new route on the map.
The open Storozhevaya kniga (Watch Book) of 1577 does not settle the question here: on fols. 223–230
it copies the report of the reply pamyat "за приписью" (countersigned by) Myasnoy, but does not preserve his
autograph. A palaeographic comparison needs another
original act of Andrey Nikitich.

[Act no. 21](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/21-40/21.php),
[verdict no. 23](https://drevlit.ru/docs/russia/XVI/1560-1580/Akty_Popov_I/21-40/23.php),
[reference work of the Institute of Russian History](https://iriran.ru/sites/default/files/2024-05/PrikazyMoskovskogoGosudarstvaXVI-XVIIveka_2015.pdf).

### The Full АМГ Volume: No Later Reference to the Oryol Roster

On 29 August not only the nearest block of acts but the whole recognised text
of the first volume of *Akty Moskovskogo gosudarstva* — documents of the Razryadny prikaz
for 1571–1634 — was checked. The search covered the full and truncated forms of the researched
surname, all Oryol occurrences and chancery words for scribes, clerks,
rosters, the Pomestny prikaz and additions of 20, 30 and 50 chetverti. OCR was used
only for navigation; significant passages were checked against the printed text and previously
saved folios of the manuscript.

There are no new surname matches. Nor does the volume contain a later act that would name
the executor of the Oryol muster, refer to a returned name list or
give the former town of the early Oryol men. The substantive Oryol passages of the 16th century
come down to the already analysed watch and recruitment documentation of 1576–1579;
later documents give only overall numbers, regimental appointments and
unrelated episodes.

This is an important negative boundary, not proof that the roster perished. A
repeated search within the published volume is closed; still open are the unpublished
and vaguely described stolbtsy of fonds 210 and 1209, duplicates, surety books,
pomestye memoranda and later land lawsuits. The next search runs by the link
Andrey Myasnoy — Bogdan Ksenofontov — Roman Buturlin — Grigory
Andreeva-Pavlov and by the formulas of execution of the verdict, not by the heading
"Oryol list".

[Full searchable copy of the volume on the Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/aktymoskovskogogosudarstvaizdannyeimpa44).

## 1700: The Original Personal Skazka of Akim Ivanovich Anpilogov

In an open collection of scans the file **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 12,
d. 1888** has been found — skazki (sworn statements) of Livny soldiers, reitars and men of town service about
their service, families and pomestye estates. The official inventory dates the file to 1700 and
gives its size as 1,252 folios. The surname index places Anpilogov on
fol. 1182; the check showed that this folio carries a dorsal note,
while the main text of the personal skazka occupies the preceding fol. 1181.

The narrator is **Akim Ivanov syn Anpilogov**, a man of Oryol, a syn boyarsky and
pomestye holder of the village of Bozdyreva. He states that his pomestye, held without a formal grant, had been
forcibly seized by the stolnik Mikhail Demidov syn Kosogov, his house and property
were ruined, and the peasant Filka Afanasyev was taken away together with his wife and children.
The same skazka expressly names **«внук мой Тит Анпилогов»** ("my grandson Tit Anpilogov"), who held
30 chetverti in Livny district, Zatrutsky stan, the village of Dmitrievskoye,
**«Щигры тож»** ("also called Shchigry").

This is strong evidence, but it points in the opposite direction from the original search:
the Livny branch came **out of Oryol Bozdyrevo**, rather than
bringing the surname to Oryol. Livny is now excluded from the candidates for the
ancestral homeland of the Oryol core and recorded as a later zone of settlement.
Separate profiles for Akim and Tit have been created, a Record with a detailed analysis of the names
and of their family and service meaning, and a map route
Bazdyrevo → Livny Shchigry. Tit's intermediate parent is not named and is not
reconstructed by guesswork. [PUB-RGADA-210-12-1888-L1181-1182-AKIM-TIT-ANPILOGOVY]

## A Third Change of Tactics: Searching for a Resettled Group, Not a Surname

Several search cycles have shown the limit of the earlier method. A simple match of the
form **Anfilogov / Anpilogov / Ampilov** before the founding of Oryol is either not found
or does not allow the identity of the person to be proven. This is to be expected: in an early list
a settler might be recorded only by his name and father, and a stable family
name might appear only among his sons in the new place. Therefore the search for
one ready-made surname is replaced by the reconstruction of a **service group**.

The unit of comparison becomes not one Anfilogov but the whole immediate circle of each
branch: rare pairs of name and patronymic, brothers, former holders of the land and neighbours
in the same locality. The strongest signature is **Mikula → Vasily, Timofey and
Karp**. For Osip Vasilyevich the control link is **Onokha and Lev Deevichi
Krivolapov**; for the Bazdyrevo cluster, the simultaneous neighbourhood of the **Trunovs,
Nemtsov, the Krivolapovs and Bazdyrev**. A match of one name will be considered
weak, of two rare elements a reason to read the original, and of three or more in one
pre-Oryol list a serious candidate for the corporation of origin.

This matters for the question of a single origin as well. In the book of 1594/95 at least seven fathers stand behind
Anfilogovs: **Mikula, Vasily, Persha,
Timofey, Nechay, Pakhom and Terenty**. They need not have been one clan already
at the time the town was founded. At least three models are possible: several
related families were resettled as one group; independent people from different places
already bore the same name; some of the surnames arose in Oryol itself from the
personal name Anfilog. In favour of the last possibility speaks the local
**Anpilog Ivanovich Onanyin**, a former holder of land and grandfather of Kondrat
Vavilovich Onanyin. But this is an example of the name at work, not an ancestor of Anfilogovs.

The first circle of comparison is now limited not to all of Rus but to the service corridor
**Odoev — Tula — Dedilov — Epifan — Mtsensk — Karachev — Oryol**. Here elements of the neighbourhood cluster
that are pre-Oryol or nearly contemporary with Oryol have already been found:
Krivolapov and Trunov in Zaupsky stan of Tula, Bazdyrev in Dedilov, several
lines of Trunovs in the Krapivna–Solova area, Bazdyrev and Nemtsov in Odoev.
So far they do not add up to one resettled group: the surname
elements match, but the same fathers, brothers and land transfer are not proven. This makes the
Upper Oka corridor the main direction, but not a finished route on the map.

The second circle is documents that may directly name the former town:
the executive pamyat on the muster of the Oryol men in 1577; draft and name lists
of the original recruitment of 1566/67–1570; conveyance and allotment charters for
plots already occupied in 1594/95; later nobility and land proceedings
with certified extracts from lost books. The official catalogue of РГИА
confirmed the main file on the nobility of Anfilogovs of Kursk province —
**f. 1343, op. 16, d. 2345, 1826–1887**, — but shows it as a
final unit without open folios or an internal inventory. So further
browsing of the catalogue is replaced by a targeted order for the contents of the file and the razryad certificates.

The new standard of proof is strict. Only a formula
like «переведён из…» ("transferred from…"), «прежде служил по…» ("formerly served in…"), «поместье отца, который
был испомещён из…» ("the pomestye of his father, who was given land from…"), or a matching documentary chain of a person, his children and
land will go onto the map of origin. Joint recurrence of neighbours will be shown as a research
link in the cards, but not as a resettlement that has already taken place.

### The First Result of the Group Tactic: The Nobility Files of the Bazdyrevs

Searching not only for Anfilogovs but for their immediate landed circle produced
the first precise archival lead. In the official catalogue of РГИА two files
of the Department of Heraldry on the nobility of the **Bazdyrevs of Kursk province** have been found:
**f. 1343, op. 17, d. 307** for 1840, 1857 and 1859 and **d. 308** for
1859. This is not a chance parallel of namesakes. In the Oryol book of
1594/95 Nikifor Afanasyev syn Bazdyrev held part of the same village of
Bazdyrevo where the five homesteads of Anfilogovs stood; the name of the village itself
preserved this name.

A full digital copy of the same alphabetical genealogical
book of the nobility of Kursk province has been found and read — **РГИА, f. 1343, op. 51, d. 593**. It removed
the ambiguity of the catalogue initials and, most importantly, divided the entries by parts of the book.

On fol. 30, entry no. 392, stand **Lieutenant Kirill Ivanovich Anpilogov**, his wife
Praskovya Pimenovna and their children Pyotr, Lyubov, Alexander and Yekaterina. The family is entered
in **Part II**, that is, by military service; confirmation followed by a decree of the
Heraldry of 24 February 1848, no. 5182. This entry is useful for a late branch,
but promises no ancient genealogy going back to the 16th century.

Entry no. 941 on fol. 64 is of an entirely different character: **Yakov Semyonovich Bazdyrev**,
protocol of 24 June 1835, **Part VI** of ancient noble families, with subsequent
confirmation on 11 March 1841, no. 1350. On fol. 81 v., entry no. 1228, there is
a different family: Sub-lieutenant **Dementy Ivanovich Bazdyrev**, his wife Ivanna
Petrovna and children Vasily, Anfisa and Marfa; this is again Part II, confirmed in
1859. Consequently, the later Bazdyrevs cannot be mechanically treated as one
line: the book directly shows an ancient branch and a service branch.

It is precisely Yakov Semyonovich's Part VI entry that changes the priority. Now the Bazdyrev
file no. 307 should be read not just "on the off chance" but checked as a possible
evidentiary proceeding of the ancient branch: which acts it presented, with which
ancestor its pedigree began and which estate it named. File no. 308 of 1859 is logically
to be compared first of all with the service family of Dementy Ivanovich. This is a working
hypothesis until the file numbers are linked to the entries by internal folios.

For two other surnames of the neighbourhood cluster land proceedings have been found:
**РГИА, f. 1583, op. 2, d. 169**, 31 folios, "Of the odnodvortsy Trunovs, about an estate",
1821, and **f. 1583, op. 5, d. 1769**, a long-running dispute of the odnodvortsy (single-homestead freeholders)
Krivolapovs with the Grankins over land. The district of the latter file and of Pavlenki have not yet been
established, so it remains a control. The value of such proceedings lies in
possible old deeds and extracts, not in the late date of the dispute itself.

The new order of checking: first obtain the internal inventory and the genealogical
pedigree of Bazdyrev file no. 307 and check its link to Yakov Semyonovich;
then separate from it the proceeding of Dementy Ivanovich and read the short
Trunov file to establish the estate.
Only an old town, previous service, a matching estate or an unbroken chain of generations
back to the people of 1594/95 will count as proof of origin.
As long as there is no such formula, the map does not change. The full archival navigator is kept
in the card **PUB-RGIA-OREL-BAZDYREVO-NEIGHBOUR-DOSSIERS**.

### Persha Turned Out to Be a Search Variant of Petrusha

Checking the rare name of the father of Ivan Pershin Anfilogov produced an important correction to the
method. In the Epifan desyatnya of 1585, under no. 263, stands **Persha Timofeev syn
Filatov**, and a cross-reference leads to no. 307 of the next desyatnya. In 1591, under this
number, is recorded **Petrusha Timofeev syn Filatov, умре** ("died"). The patronymic,
second name and cross-reference number match. This is one person recorded in two
forms of the name.

The second pair is even stronger. In 1585, no. 276 is **Persha Semyonov syn Roslyakov**,
with a reference to no. 93; in 1591, no. 93 is **Petrusha Semyonov syn Roslyakov**. In the same
book of 1591, under no. 247, the same guarantor is again called **Persha
Roslyakov**. Consequently, Persha and Petrusha could alternate not only
between two books but also within one documentary complex.

The same desyatnya shows the next step — the formation of a family name. In it stand
**Matyusha Petrushin syn Timofeeva** and **Borisko Pershin syn Levontyeva**: these are
two independent families in which the patronymic is formed from Petrusha or Persha. Next to them
are named the deceased **Vlas Pershin syn Fomina** and the serving **Avilko Vlasov syn
Pershin**. The full combination makes the chain Persha → Vlas → Avilko probable:
for Vlas, Pershin still reads as a patronymic, while for his probable son it is already
retained as the family part. There is no direct statement that the two Vlases are identical,
so this three-generation link is marked as strong but not final.

For the overall task this matters more than any single match. The document shows the live
mechanism by which identical names could arise independently among the
children of different people with the same personal name, and then become fixed. So the
seven paternal branches of Oryol Anfilogovs really could have had not one
but several origins. For now this is a historically confirmed model, not
proof of the origin of any particular branch.

This does not automatically permit calling the Oryol Persha Pyotr. The control
pair **Persha Onanyin syn Levontyev** keeps the form Persha in both 1585 and 1591,
and onomastic reference works also allow for Persha a link with
Porfiry. The reliable conclusion is already this: the patronymic **Pershin** gives the form of the
father's name, but not his undoubted full baptismal name.
