both recorded with the diminutive name Kondrashka — one married, the other unmarried.

The archival addresses are given precisely: **РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6d, d. 18, fol. 34,
106v; d. 20, fol. 12**. The published fragments of the originals make it possible to
verify the surname form, the patronymic, the service category and the transfer formula. This is
no longer a supposed corridor but a state resettlement of a family by name.
Therefore the route **Stary Oskol → Tsarev-Alekseev** is added to the map.

Every part of the naming matters. «Данило» (Danilo) is the documentary form of the name
Daniil. «Левонтьев сын» ("son of Levonty") reveals the father Leonty. «Анпилов» (Anpilov) is a short form of
the researched surname group, which in the neighbouring Oskol records is also rendered
as Anpilogov. The formulas «сведён с Оскола» ("brought over from Oskol") and «выбран на вечное житьё» ("selected for permanent residence") mean
not a chance presence but the administrative transfer of a serviceman and
his family to a new fortress.

A longer chain, not yet closed, has also emerged. In 1615 the regimental Cossack
**Danilko Onpilogov** was already serving in Oskol. A researcher reports a regimental
Cossack Danila Anpilogov in documents of 1635–1645, and then documents
of 1647/48 give the full patronymic for the first time. The rare name, the surname
group, the town and the service all match. This makes the identity of the two Danilas plausible, but not yet
proven: the record of 1615 gives neither patronymic nor age, and for the
intermediate mentions the exact files and folios have not yet been published. Therefore
profiles P0081 and P0082 have been left separate, and the line 1615 → 1647/48 is not drawn on the map.

The tactics now change once more: one must not search for the surname across all inventories but
reconstruct the service biography of a single regimental Cossack. The key query is
the exact call numbers of the Oskol documents of 1635–1645 and whether they contain the formula
«Левонтьев сын» ("son of Levonty"). If it is found, the two records can be merged into
a continuous biography spanning more than thirty years.

[Publication of fragments and archival references](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/2160/38740/p5750448.htm),
[discussion of the intermediate mentions](https://forum.vgd.ru/post/2160/38740/p5739006.htm).
### A New Approach: Look Not for the Surname but for the Change of Generations

The blind search for the variants Anpilogov/Anfilogov in yet more indexes was
halted. Instead, all **90 Kursk lines** of the compilation of servicemen of the Time of Troubles,
1605–1618, were compared with the full folio-by-folio roster of Kursk for 1630.
What emerged was not a single namesake but a longitudinal cross-section of the whole service milieu.

At least eight rare combinations of first name and surname recur as probably the same
people: Obakum Babin, Lukyan Gostev, Filimon Zhmakin, Sava Kosinov, Stepan
Nozdrin, Afanasy Pykhtin, Osip Sukhochev and Mokey Khalin. At least seven more pairs
show a probable change of generation, where the first name of the earlier man becomes
the patronymic of a later bearer of the same surname:

- Lomaka Boltenok — Yakushko Lomakin syn Boltenok;
- Neustroy Bukreev — Fedosko Neustroev syn Bukreev;
- Klemen Gnezdilov — Pronka Klementyev syn Gnezdilov;
- Lukyan Golovin — Maksimko Lukyanov syn Golovin;
- Filimon Zhmakin — Lukyanko Filimonov syn Zhmakin;
- Semyon Sogolaev — Ivashko Semyonov syn Sogolaev;
- Erofey Sofonov — Tereshka Erofeev syn Sofonov.

Each pair taken separately still allows for namesakes and requires the original folio. But
together they prove the usefulness of the new model: the Troubles compilation contains
the older layer of precisely the Kursk corporation that is seen in detail in 1630.
Therefore, for the line **«Анпилогов Федор»** ("Anpilogov Fedor") the main candidate now
becomes **Fyodor Grigoryevich Onpilogov**, recorded on fol. 98 of the 1630
list. The Oryol Fyodor Vasilyevich of 1641 is not excluded, but moves down to
second place.

The next search changes too. First it is necessary to uncover the archival origin
not of Fyodor on his own but of the rare control pairs of Boltenoks, Bukreevs and
Zhmakins. The call number of any early line will show the type and series of the original file,
after which Fyodor is searched for in it directly and his surroundings are compared with fol. 98 of the
1630 roster. Until a patronymic appears, profiles P9737 and P0072 remain separate;
no point or route is created on the map.

[Compilation of servicemen of the Time of Troubles](https://dompredkov.ru/sluzhilye-lyudi-smuty-1605-1618gg/),
[full roster of Kursk for 1630](https://www.old-kursk.ru/book/kurkulev_a/ku260428.html).

### A Complete Check of 115 Acts: the Yelets Surname Did Not Hide in a Neighbouring Document

After selective successes with the Yelets petitions, the entire open corpus of G. N.
Anpilogov, «Новые документы о России конца XVI — начала XVII в.» (New Documents on Russia of the Late 16th — Early 17th Century), was checked
in full: documents Nos. 1–115, including the four parts of the large Putivl otdelnaya
kniga (allotment book) No. 7. The search ran on the stems **анпил-, ампил-, анфил-, амфил-, онпил- and
онфил-**, and then separately on the seven fathers' names of the Oryol core.

The published texts contain not a single form of the surname series under study and not
a single personal name Anfilog/Anfilof. This is no longer a chance search-engine result
but a negative result for the whole printed sample. But it applies only to the
115 published acts: the decayed Yelets file was considerably larger, the text
has losses, and its unpublished folios have not yet been examined in photocopies.

The check of the fathers' names provided a useful control against false routes. The
Putivl book of 1594 has two different «Першины пашни» ("Pershin's ploughlands") — Kovalyov's and
Kabanov's; the Yelets roster of 1593 names Nechay Mikhailov syn Kozlov,
Mikul Yakovlev syn Tolstogo and two men with the patronymic Nechaev. In the northern,
Novgorod–Olonets part of the corpus there appear Nechayko Pakhomov and the peasant
Mikula Terentyev. The coincidences are striking, but the third designations, status and
geography differ. They show how common the names were, not kinship.

The tactics therefore change again. There is no longer any need to look for a ready-made surname in the printed part
of the Yelets complex. Priority goes to the
**unpublished folios of РГАДА, f. 141, op. 1, 1592, d. 1** and to the late
Oryol land files that might quote the lost original
grants. The formula needed is not just a surname but «жил прежде» ("lived formerly"), «остался отец» ("the father remained"),
«брат/дядя» ("brother/uncle"), «написался на новый город» ("enrolled in the new town") or a direct transfer of a pomestye.
No profiles or routes are created on the basis of name coincidences.

[Full index of the corpus on Vostlit](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1580-1600/Anpilogov_new_dok/index.htm).

#### Fyodor of 1630 Turned Out to Be a Long-Serving Man, Not a Novik

After the generational comparison, the structure of the Kursk roster itself was checked,
not only the name on fol. 98. **Fedka Grigoryev syn Onpilogov** stands within
the section of town deti boyarskie with a pomestye oklad of **200 chetverti**.
The heading «По 200 чети» ("At 200 cheti") begins on fol. 91 and holds until the new heading
«По 150 чети» ("At 150 cheti") on fol. 101. The extracts on the noviki of 1627, 1628 and 1629 are placed
considerably lower, under separate rubrics.

This refines the age of his service career. In 1630 Fyodor Grigoryevich was
not a man of the latest intake but a member of an already established Kursk corporation.
Such a position is compatible with his possible mention in 1605–1618 and
strengthens him as the main candidate for the Troubles-era **Fyodor Anpilogov**.
At the same time a new search level has appeared — the father **Grigory**: if
the two Fyodors are identical, it is he who should belong to the first generation of the
Kursk branch.

The limit of proof remains strict. The roster does not say when Fyodor
was first enrolled, where Grigory came from, or whether the father bore the same
family designation. Therefore the profiles have not been merged, and no separate biography
of Grigory or route on the map has been created. The next address is the early Kursk
enrolment and supplementary-grant lists before 1627, where the 200-chetverti section might
be explained by an original grant or a later supplement.

### 1579–1591: Terenty Ievlev Again Becomes a Candidate for Pavel's Father

The weak point of the Ryazhsk hypothesis has been tested not by reasoning but by an internal
control of both desyatni. In 1579 **Trenka Semyonov syn Ievleva** stands under
No. 362 in the rubric of the dead or killed, after whom «не осталось жон и детей,
а поместья лежат пусты» ("no wives or children remained, and the pomestya lie empty"). Previously this formula was considered a direct objection
to the idea that he could have been the father of Pavel and Panka Terentyevich Ievlev.

A complete comparison of the whole rubric Nos. 334–367 with the desyatnya of 1591 showed
that the heading did not take into account adult sons who had already set up on their own:

- **Isayko Ivanov syn Korovaev**, No. 348 in 1579, stands in the same
  "childless" rubric; in 1591 **Tereshka Isaev syn Korovaev**
  (No. 267), **Nenash Isaev syn Korovaev** (No. 417) and **Pyatunka Isaev syn
  Korovaev** (No. 419) are serving;
- **Neupokoets Osipov syn Ostretsov**, No. 367 in 1579; in 1591 —
  **Vaska Neupokoev syn Ostretsov**, No. 429.

These are two independent transitions with the exact rare name of the father and the same family
designation. Consequently, the phrase «не осталось детей» ("no children remained") meant the absence of
minors or dependent members of the household remaining at his pomestye, and
not biological childlessness in general.

**Names and meaning.** Trenka is a diminutive documentary form of Terenty.
Now the chain looks concrete: **Terenty
Semyonovich Ievlev**, who died before January 1579, may have had adult sons who had already set up on their own; in 1591
**Pavel Terentyevich Ievlev** twice acts as a guarantor, and **Panka** is called
«Орбеевым, а Терентьевым сыном Иевлевым тож» ("Orbeev, also known as Terentyev syn Ievlev"). In 1596 there appears in Oryol the
novik **Pavel Terentyevich Anfilogov**. This for the first time gives a working three-step
model of one generation, rather than a lone coincidence of Pavel and Terenty.

The limit of proof is kept. There is no direct record «Павел и Панка — дети
Тренки Семёновича» ("Pavel and Panka are the children of Trenka Semyonovich"), no inheritance of his 30 chetverti and no formula «Иевлев,
Анфилогов тож» ("Ievlev, also known as Anfilogov"). Therefore Terenty has been raised from a cut-off namesake to a
strong candidate for the father, but the Ryazhsk–Oryol route is not yet
put on the map. The next check is targeted: the fate of Trenka's empty 30 chetverti,
the separate grants to Pavel and Panka, and extracts concerning the children of the deceased Terenty.

### 1579–1597: the Old Designation Could Disappear from the Next Desyatnya

The formal objection to the Ryazhsk hypothesis ran like this: if the Oryol
Pavel of 1596 was the former Ryazhsk Pavel Ievlev, the scribe should have written
«Анфилогов, Иевлев тож» ("Anfilogov, also known as Ievlev"). This requirement has been tested on men whom the
editors of the desyatni themselves link by cross-referenced numbers.

The cleanest control is **Kozarin (Kazarin) Ivanovich**:

- 1579, No. 242 — **Kozarinko Ivanov syn Kurchov**;
- 1591, No. 67 — **Kazarin Ivanov syn Verevkin, a Kurchev tozh** (also known as Kurchev);
- 1594, No. 473 — **Kazarin Ivanov syn Verevkin Kurchev**;
- 1597, No. 653 — only **Kazarinko Ivanov syn Verevkin**, and the editor
  expressly refers this line to No. 473 of the 1594 desyatnya.

In other words, the third designation **Kurchev**, with which the biography began,
dropped out of the formula entirely within eighteen years. The man's identity, meanwhile, is
established not by a modern guess but by the edition's internal system of cross-references.
A similar shortening is visible with Panka: in 1591 he is «Орбеев, а Терентьев сын
Иевлев тож» ("Orbeev, also known as Terentyev syn Ievlev"), whereas in 1594 and 1597 only «Арбеев сын Иевлев» ("Arbeev syn Ievlev") remains.

**What this means for Pavel.** The absence of the word **Ievlev** in the Oryol record of 1596
can no longer be considered an independent refutation of the identity. The practice of the
same series of documents allows that a former third designation was no longer
repeated. But the control proves only the mechanism, not Pavel himself: until there is
a record «Иевлев, Анфилогов тож» ("Ievlev, also known as Anfilogov"), a transfer from Ryazhsk, a shared pomestye or
an inheritance extract, the Ryazhsk–Oryol route remains a strong hypothesis and
is not put on the map.

### Odoev 1576/77: a Lost Cadastre That Could Have Named Ievlev Has Been Found

The Pekhlets line about the zherebey granted to Oleksey Oglomazov «из Иевлева поместья
Одоевцова» ("from the pomestye of Ievlev the Odoev man") does not give the personal name of the previous holder. The search has been moved from
the late lists of the surname to the history of Odoev land registration itself.

The official РГАДА reference guide states: **Odoev district was described in
1576/77 by Andrey Skryabin and the podyachy (under-clerk) Stepan Vitovtov**. The authors
link this cadastre with the distribution of the former lands of Prince M. I. Vorotynsky
to new pomeshchiki and suggest that in the 1580s–1590s no new description of the district
may have been carried out at all. This is exactly the chronological layer in which
the Ryazhsk Terenty Semyonovich Ievlev served.

However, the catalogue of surviving Odoev books begins only with 1614/15:

- payment book of 1614/15 — **РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, kn. 488,
  l. 464–492 ob.**;
- dozor (inspection survey) of 1615/16 — **kn. 524, l. 151–316**, copy in kn. 488,
  l. 493–560;
- payment book of 1619/20 — **kn. 488, l. 562–586**;
- survey and boundary book of 1627–1629 — **kn. 327, l. 1–543**.

The Skryabin–Vitovtov book of 1576/77 does not appear in this series as a separate surviving
unit. So the next targeted search is for extracts from it,
pomestye files on old grants, and the reference entries of later books with
the formulas «по старым писцовым книгам» ("according to the old survey books"). It is exactly such a retrospective that could
restore the personal name of Ievlev the Odoev man and show whether his Pekhlets
pomestye belonged to the Ryazhsk group.

**Names and meaning.** At the same time the tempting chain Semyon
Ivanovich Ievlev (charters of 1539/1544) → Terenty Semyonovich Ievlev has been checked.
The genealogical register of 1686 names as Semyon's sons only **Tit and
Ivan**, not Terenty. Therefore the Tula Semyon cannot be declared the father of the
Ryazhsk Terenty on the strength of a patronymic alone. The possible Ryazhsk branch remains
separate or unrecognised; the map does not change for now.

[Official РГАДА reference guide](http://rgada.info/sprav/sprav/zenchenko_ivanova_2013.pdf).

### A New Search Not for Pavel but for the Rare Combination "Terenty Ievlev"

After the dead end with Pavel's neighbours the tactics were changed: what is checked is not only
Pavel himself but the stability of the rare personal name **Terenty** within different
branches of Ievlevs and the documented cases of their movement between towns.

The first result relates to the early seventeenth century. The onomastic index to
the three-volume description of documents of **РГАДА, f. 141, op. 1, for 1505–1626**
contains the full form **«Иевлев Терентий Нечаев»** ("Ievlev Terenty Nechaev"). This is Terenty Ievlev with
the patronymic Nechaev, not Trenka Semyonovich from the Ryazhsk desyatnya of 1579.
The public index, however, has lost the issue number, page, year, file and
folio. Therefore the find so far gives a new precise object of archival search, but not
a relative of Pavel.

The second result is a late control of a possible resettlement. In the razbornaya
kniga (service review book) of 1680 (**РГАДА, f. 210, op. 6, d. 117**), among eight Ryazhsk
Ievlevs, **Terenty Ievlev** is named. Eight or nine years later a man with the same
combination of surname and first name is recorded in the town of Dobry in the smetnaya kniga (budget book) of
1688–1689 (**f. 210, op. 6d, d. 153**). In Ryazhsk alongside him stand Vasily,
Efim, Zinovy, Ignaty, Larion, Timofey and Filipp Ievlev; in Dobry — Kuzma,
Sergey and Terenty. Of all the names only Terenty recurs.

**Names and meaning.** This is not yet a proven route: the open tables show
only surname, first name and town, without patronymic, age, children or pomestye. But
the full entries of both files, according to the publication's description, usually contain precisely this
information. If the patronymic and family composition match, this will be a direct example
of a move **Ryazhsk → Dobry** — of the same type that we are looking for in Pavel's case almost
a century earlier. If they do not match, this will still prove independent
repetition of a rare name in several branches and will lower the value of the coincidence
"Terenty + Ievlev" without additional features.

The next two addresses are therefore as concrete as possible: use the name index to find
the exact file of the early **Terenty Nechaevich Ievlev** in f. 141, and order or
find scans of Terenty's full entries in **f. 210, op. 6, d. 117** and **op. 6d,
d. 153**. No profiles or map are created: Ievlevs here are a control surname, and
the identity of the Ryazhsk and Dobry Terentys has not yet been established.

[Onomastic index](https://olri.ru/onomastika.html),
[Ryazhsk, 1680](https://dompredkov.ru/rabornaya-kniga-ryashan-belozertsev-kaluzhan-meshheryan-i-dr-1680/),
[Dobry, 1688–1689](https://dompredkov.ru/smetnye-knigi-gg-dobryj-usman-sokolsk-demshinsk-1688-1689/).

#### A Correction of Scale: Ievlevs Are Not One Ryazhsk Family

To test the premise itself, all **631
index lines** for the stem Ievlev were exported from the public database. This is not 631 people: one service
biography recurs in different books, and identical names may belong to
different people. But the distribution is still telling: **Tula — 168 lines,
Moscow — 77, Belyov — 68, Odoev — 63, Ryazhsk — 30, Yaroslavl — 28, Kozlov —
23, Arzamas — 18, Ryazan — 17**.

The earliest index line unexpectedly leads even further north: **Mikhail Ievlev,
1588, Novgorod, Obonezhskaya pyatina, РГАДА, f. 137, op. 1, d. 11**. This
agrees with the genealogical tradition of the senior Tula branch about its origin in
Novgorod, but does not make Mikhail a relative of the Tula or Ryazhsk Ievlevs:
there is no patronymic and no linking charter.

The main conclusion is negative but useful. **Ievlev** is a multi-branch
designation and a poor independent indicator of resettlement. The Ryazhsk Pavel
remains a strong candidate only cumulatively: Pavel + the rare father
Terenty, two guarantees, disappearance after 1591 and the appearance of
a novik of the same name in Oryol in 1596. The next document must add
a patronymic, land, a relative or a direct transfer; yet another bare coincidence
of surname is no longer counted as strengthening.

### 31 August 2026: One Volume Instead of Three, and the Lodygin Line Closed

The structure of the onomastic web page yielded a bibliographic result
that was not in the line itself. **«Иевлев Терентий Нечаев»** ("Ievlev Terenty Nechaev") is placed in the
first block of Voskoboinikova's index. The next block is labelled separately as
book 2; an independent description by an owner of the edition identifies it as the volume for
**1614–1623**. The first issue of 1994 covers documents from 1505
**up to and including 1613**. Consequently, the early Terenty Nechaevich should be
sought in the first volume, not in the whole three-volume set for 1505–1626.

This is not yet an archival call number. In its indexes the site mixes people from the documents with
the names of authors of scholarly literature — for example, the same block contains the historian
**G. N. Anpilogov**. Therefore Terenty's line remains a search key until
the printed descriptive entry is checked: one cannot create a profile or declare
him a relative of the Ryazhsk Ievlevs on the strength of a web list alone.

At the same time the reverse route from the Kashin **Anfilof Lodygin** was checked,
reconstructed from the patronymic of his son Peshok around 1500. The open database
contains 169 index lines for Lodygins. They yielded no pre-Oryol southern chain:

- 1583 — Semeyka Lodygin, Obonezhskaya pyatina of Novgorod;
- 1588 — Vasily, Nikifor, Timofey and two Yakov Lodygins, Bezhetskaya
  pyatina;
- 1608 — in Bezhetskaya pyatina Vasily, Ivan, Timofey, Fyodor and
  Yakov are seen again;
- Chernigov and Rylsk appear only in 1605–1618, and a new Rylsk
  layer in 1631–1638.

**Names and meaning.** Peshok does indeed preserve the name of his father Anfilof, but
Lodygin is an independent family designation. The later movement of
Lodygins to the south shows a possible mechanism of migration, but it begins
too late and does not lead to Oryol before 1594. The attractive hypothesis
"Kashin Anfilof → southern Anfilogovs" is therefore postponed until shared
land, a patronymic or a direct record of a move turns up. No route has been added to the map.

[Onomastic index](https://olri.ru/onomastika.html),
[discussion and bibliography of book 2](https://forum.vgd.ru/397/9562/),
[open database of servicemen](https://next-fast-table-gold.vercel.app/).

### 31 August 2026: Not One Donor Town but Seven Biographies

The search for Mikula's family trio was repeated, taking into account historical and possible
erroneous forms of the name. The live index of **421,095 lines** was calibrated against
the control query "Lodygin", which again gave 169 entries. A query on the
stem **Нику-** (Niku-) returned only eight lines in the whole database; the earliest
dates from 1631. So the variant Nikula/Nikulin does not conceal pre-Oryol
Vasily, Timofey and Karp in the current open corpus.

More important was the re-check of two old coincidences of 1582 —
**Mikulinsky Vasyuk** and **Mikulinsky Timoshka**. The public page for
Derevskaya pyatina explains how the list is built: 938 mentions are gathered from two
files, different pogosty and different categories of landholders; one man could
recur under several holdings. The open list is sorted by
surname and first name, and the full land details of the lines are not shown. So Vasyuk and Timoshka
stand side by side only in an alphabetical listing. The source so far does not call them brothers,
does not place them in one holding and does not give a Karp. The Novgorod trail for Mikula's
trio has been downgraded: it should be revisited only through the primary folios of file
716, not because two lines are close together on a web page.

Instead, "control routes" have been gathered for families from the same early Oryol
service milieu:

- **Kashira → Oryol:** Filipp Fyodorovich Teplov is expressly named as granted a pomestye in
  Oryol; his Oryol Cheremoshnya lies next to the Sofonova of Sofon Timofeevich
  Anfilogov.
- **Ryazan, Pekhlets stan → Ryazhsk:** Pyotr Nikiforovich Bazdyrev, also known as Sviridov,
  in 1591 expressly transferred his service from Ryazan. He may be a son of the
  Oryol Nikifor Bazdyrev, a neighbour of Anfilogovs, but the kinship and the direction
  of the family's movement are not yet proven.
- **Vladimir and the sovereign's court:** Shalimovs and Trunovs have independent
  pre-Oryol traces. Their possible continuations in Oryol rest on rare surnames
  and suitable generations, but not yet on a direct transfer formula.
- **Korchakov stan itself:** Anpilog Ivanovich Onanyin proves the local
  use of the name in the right generation, but his documented
  descendants continue to be called Onanyins.

**A new model.** Even the immediate surroundings of the western Oryol cluster cannot be
reduced to a single town of origin: the reliable and probable lines diverge
towards Kashira, Ryazan/Pekhlets, Vladimir and the local Korchakov stan. Therefore
looking for a single ready-made list of settlers for all Anfilogovs becomes less
reasonable. The research has been divided into seven biographical tasks: Terenty—Pavel,
Mikula—three sons, Vasily—Belyay and Osip, Persha—Ivan, Timofey—Sofon,
Nechay—Ivan and Pakhom—Ermak. Each branch needs its own fourth
feature — former land, a guarantor, a brother or a direct record of a former town.

This is not yet proof of several origins: the control families are not
Anfilogovs. But it changes the order of work. First priority remains with
Pavel Terentyevich Ievlev as an individual candidate; second, with the
division of the shared zherebey of the three Mikulichi; third, with the Kashira surroundings
of Sofonova. Other families' routes are not transferred to the map.

[Public description of the Derevskaya pyatina book of 1582](https://dompredkov.ru/pomeshhiki-derevskoj-pyatiny-novgoroda-1582g-ch-1-polovina-morozova/),
[open database of servicemen](https://next-fast-table-gold.vercel.app/).

### 31 August 2026: a Rare Persha in the Tver Mikulin District

The search for the branch **Persha → Ivan Pershin Anfilogov** has been moved from a ready-made surname to
the rare name of the father's generation. A complete search of the open OCR of the same large
volume of survey materials yielded the only literal pre-Oryol Persha. On
printed p. 192, in the old layer of holders of the village of Kobylino, stands **Fedka
Stepanov**, and in the updated layer **«Перша Степанов сын»** ("Persha, son of Stepan"). Below are listed
Ileyka, Gavrilko, Okifanko and Persha, who **«служат князю Дмитрею
Ивановичю Микулинскому»** ("serve Prince Dmitry Ivanovich Mikulinsky"). The match of patronymic and land looks like a change of
holder within one household of Stepan, although the book does not call Fyodor and Persha
brothers and does not explain the mechanism of the transfer.

The chronology fits the generation of the father of the Oryol Ivan: the entry itself belongs to
the updated layer of the Tver dozornaya kniga (inspection book) of **1551–1554**. It cannot be limited
to 1552: the Tysyachnaya kniga (Thousand Book) of 1550 simultaneously records two Princes
Dmitry Ivanovich Mikulinsky, and the inspection book does not distinguish which of them
Persha served. The find is especially notable because it is in the same manuscript complex,
**РГАДА, f. 1209, op. 1, kn. 466**, where the Tver Onfilogovo is recorded.
However, a shared volume is a link between sources, not proof of kinship.

**Names and meaning.** Persha here is an independent personal name or a colloquial
form of a calendar name; Stepanov syn is his patronymic. The Oryol formula
of 1594/1595 is built differently: Ivan is the man, Pershin points to his father
Persha, Anfilogov is a second family designation. The Tver Persha fits by the
rarity of the name and the age generation, but the source does not give him a son
Ivan, the designation Anfilogov, land shared with the Oryol men or a move south.
Therefore he is recorded as a candidate to be tested, not as a father who has been found.

The geographical test did not strengthen the hypothesis. In a scholarly GIS layer
two Kobylinos of the same name were found, but they belong to other entries; Persha's
village has not yet been located. Neither a point nor a route has been added to the map.
The next test is to trace the people of Prince Dmitry Mikulinsky and the transfers of
Kobylino in later documents: to look not for a lone name but for Stepan's household — Fyodor
Stepanov → Persha/Petrusha Stepanov → Ivan Pershin and the children's second designations.
If no family continuation appears,
the direction will be shelved without spending further time on it.

[Facsimile of printed p. 192](https://archive.org/details/moscha-regia/page/n196/mode/1up),
[scholarly historical-geographical materials on the Tver land](https://www.aquilopress.ru/Pro/Upload/Magazine_Issue/file48.pdf).

### 31 August 2026: Two Princes Dmitry Change the Dating of Persha

Checking not a lone name but Persha's whole service milieu produced an important
correction. D. A. Seliverstov counted in the Mikulin stan-district **36
landholders** who served Prince Dmitry Ivanovich Mikulinsky. So
Persha Stepanov belonged to a large private service household, and was not
a chance holder of one village who happened to bear the name.

However, in a note the researcher specifically warns: the Thousand Book
of 1550 simultaneously records **two Princes Dmitry Ivanovich Mikulinsky** —
one in article I for Tver, the other in article III. One, of the Punkov branch, was killed at
the taking of Kazan in 1552; but which of the two these 36 men served is unclear
from the inspection book. The full text of the Thousand Book confirms both separate
lines.

This corrects the earlier, too rigid dating: the formula about Persha's service
cannot automatically be limited by the death of one prince in 1552. The reliable
interval is set by the source itself — **the Tver inspection book of 1551–1554**.
The full published text of the Dvorovaya tetrad (Court Register) does not contain Persha's name, so
his transfer from a private princely household directly into the Sovereign's Court
cannot yet be traced.

**Names and meaning.** Dmitry Ivanovich Mikulinsky in this entry is an ambiguous
patron, not one automatically recognisable prince. Persha Stepanov remains
a rare and chronologically possible candidate for the generation of the father of the Oryol
Ivan Pershin Anfilogov, but the new check does not strengthen the identity. It changes
the shape of the next search: what is needed are documents on the fate of the whole private household after
1552–1556 — transfers to the sovereign's service, new pomestya and transfers of
holdings — and not only the biography of the prince killed at Kazan. The map does not
change.

[D. A. Seliverstov, «Московское войско в битве при Орше 1514 г.» (The Muscovite Army at the Battle of Orsha, 1514), pp. 334–335](https://archaeolog.ru/media/periodicals/voen-arch/voen_arh_3.pdf),
[full text of the Thousand Book of 1550](https://www.vostlit.info/Texts/Dokumenty/Russ/XVI/1540-1560/Tys_kniga_1550/text.htm).
